## Programming fundamentals

This file covers data types, the statement types every imperative program is built from, arithmetic, relational and Boolean operators, constants, string handling, random numbers, exception handling, and subroutines with parameters, return values, and local and global variables.

GCSE covered most of the basics. Revise these first if they feel shaky:

- [GCSE: Programming fundamentals](../../gcse/computer-science/03-programming-fundamentals.md) (data types, variables, selection, iteration, operators)
- [GCSE: Data structures, strings and files](../../gcse/computer-science/04-data-structures-strings-and-files.md)
- [GCSE: Subprograms and structured programming](../../gcse/computer-science/05-subprograms-and-structured-programming.md)
- [GCSE: Robust programs and testing](../../gcse/computer-science/06-robust-programs-and-testing.md)

This file goes further: pointer and user-defined types, condition-at-end loops, truncation versus rounding, XOR, exception handling, interfaces, parameter passing by value and by reference, and scope and lifetime.

Code is in Python 3. AQA's Paper 1 is on screen in the language your school chose (C#, Java, Python or VB.NET); OCR and Eduqas papers are written, and accept any sensible pseudo-code or high-level language unless a question says otherwise.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Data types | Integer, real, Boolean, char, string, date/time, pointer, records, arrays, user-defined | Primitive types: integer, real, character, string, Boolean (1.4.1(a)) | Boolean, character, string, integer, real, and their storage needs (C2.3) |
| Parameters | Use parameters and interfaces | By value and by reference (2.2.1(c)) | Call by value and call by reference (C1.3) |
| Exceptions | Named (4.1.1.9) | Not named | Not named (validation is in C1.3) |
| IDE | Not named here | Use an IDE to develop and debug (2.2.1(e)) | IDE tools (C1.7, see file 33) |
| Counts and rogue values | Not named | Not named | Named (C1.3 Programming constructs) |
| Paper | Paper 1 (on screen, with skeleton program) | Paper 02 (written) | Component 1 (written) |

### 4.1.1.1 Data types

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.1 · OCR 1.4.1(a) · Eduqas C2.3: Data types.*

#### What you need to know

- A data type says what values a piece of data can take and what operations make sense on it. It also fixes how much memory it uses and how its bits are read.
- Built-in types you must know: integer, real/float, Boolean, character, string, date/time, pointer/reference, records (or equivalent) and arrays (or equivalent).
- A pointer (reference) type holds a memory address, usually of an object created while the program runs (dynamically). Python hides pointers, but every Python variable is in fact a reference to an object.
- A user-defined type is built from built-in types, for example a record type `Student` with a string name and an integer mark, or an enumerated type `Suit`.
- Eduqas: know typical storage sizes. A Boolean needs 1 bit in theory (often 1 byte in practice); a character 1 byte (ASCII) or 1 to 4 bytes (UTF-8 Unicode); an integer often 2, 4 or 8 bytes; a real often 4 bytes (single) or 8 bytes (double); a string one character's size per character plus a length or terminator.

#### Explained

Choosing the right type matters for three reasons. It stops nonsense operations (you cannot multiply a name). It controls memory: storing a yes/no answer as a Boolean is far cheaper than as a string. It controls accuracy: money stored as a float can pick up tiny rounding errors, so many systems store pence as an integer instead.

A date/time type stores a moment so that you can compare dates and do arithmetic on them (days between two dates) without parsing text every time.

A user-defined type lets the code speak the language of the problem. Compare `marks[3][1]` with `students[3].mark`: the second says what it means.

```python
from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import date
from enum import Enum

class Suit(Enum):            # user-defined enumerated type
    HEARTS = 1
    SPADES = 2

@dataclass
class Student:               # user-defined record type
    name: str                # built-in string
    mark: int                # built-in integer
    joined: date             # built-in date/time

s = Student("Ali", 72, date(2025, 9, 4))
t = s                        # t is a second reference to the SAME object
t.mark = 80
print(s.mark)                # 80, because s and t point to one object
```

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a pointer "stores the data". It stores the address where the data is.
- Storing phone numbers as integers. They are identifiers, not quantities: a leading zero is lost and arithmetic on them is meaningless. Use a string.
- Forgetting that a Boolean only needs one bit when asked for minimum storage.

### 4.1.1.2 Programming concepts

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.2 · OCR 2.2.1(a) · Eduqas C1.3: Programming constructs, Identifiers.*

#### What you need to know

- Statement types: variable declaration, constant declaration, assignment, iteration, selection, and subroutine (procedure or function) calls.
- The three combining principles of every imperative language: sequence, selection (choice, branching) and iteration (repetition).
- Definite iteration: the number of repetitions is known when the loop starts (a `for` loop over a range).
- Indefinite iteration: the loop repeats until a condition changes. The condition can be tested at the start (a `while` loop, which may run zero times) or at the end (`repeat ... until` or `do ... while`, which always runs at least once). You need to understand both, even if your language only has one.
- Nesting: a selection or iteration inside another one.
- Meaningful identifier names make code self-documenting: easier to read, debug and maintain, especially by someone else.
- Eduqas: a count (counter) is a variable increased by 1 each time something happens; a rogue value (sentinel) is a special input such as −1 that is not real data and signals the end of input. Self-documenting identifiers, annotation (comments) and layout (indentation, white space) all help.

#### Explained

Python has no condition-at-end loop, so you build one with `while True` and a `break` at the bottom. The loop body always runs once before the test.

```python
## Condition at the start: may run zero times
total = 0
n = int(input("Number (-1 to stop): "))
while n != -1:              # -1 is a rogue value
    total += n
    n = int(input("Number (-1 to stop): "))

## Condition at the end: always runs at least once (repeat ... until valid)
while True:
    choice = input("Menu choice 1-3: ")
    if choice in ("1", "2", "3"):
        break

## Definite iteration, nested
for row in range(1, 4):
    for col in range(1, 4):
        print(row * col, end=" ")
    print()
```

#### Worked example

Trace this pseudo-code with input 3, 8, 0. State the output.

```
count ← 0
total ← 0
REPEAT
    x ← USERINPUT
    IF x > 0 THEN
        count ← count + 1
        total ← total + x
    ENDIF
UNTIL x = 0
OUTPUT total / count
```

| x | x > 0? | count | total | x = 0? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3 | True | 1 | 3 | False |
| 8 | True | 2 | 11 | False |
| 0 | False | 2 | 11 | True |

Output: 11 / 2 = 5.5. If the first input were 0, count would stay 0 and the program would crash dividing by zero. A good answer spots that.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a `while` loop always runs at least once. Only a condition-at-end loop does.
- Using a `for` loop when the number of repeats is not known in advance (for example reading input until a rogue value).
- Calling single letters like `x` meaningful. Loop counters `i` and `j` are accepted by convention; anything else should say what it holds.

### 4.1.1.3 Arithmetic operations

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.3 · OCR 2.2.1(a) · Eduqas C1.3: Mathematical operations.*

#### What you need to know

- Addition, subtraction, multiplication, real (float) division, integer division with remainder (DIV and MOD), exponentiation, rounding and truncation.
- Truncation removes the fractional part (toward zero): 7.9 → 7, −7.9 → −7.
- Rounding goes to the nearest value: 7.5 → 8 normally. Be aware that Python's `round` uses banker's rounding (to the nearest even number on an exact half): `round(2.5)` is 2.

| Operation | Pseudo-code | Python | 17 and 5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Real division | / | / | 3.4 |
| Integer division | DIV | // | 3 |
| Remainder | MOD | % | 2 |
| Exponent | ^ | ** | 1419857 |
| Truncate | | int(x), math.trunc(x) | int(3.4) = 3 |

#### Worked example

Convert 200 seconds to minutes and seconds: 200 DIV 60 = 3; 200 MOD 60 = 20. Answer 3 min 20 s.

Is year 2024 a leap year? `year MOD 4 = 0 AND (year MOD 100 ≠ 0 OR year MOD 400 = 0)`: 2024 MOD 4 = 0, 2024 MOD 100 = 24 ≠ 0, so True. 1900: MOD 4 = 0 but MOD 100 = 0 and MOD 400 = 300, so False.

Extract digits of 4729: units = 4729 MOD 10 = 9; then 4729 DIV 10 = 472; repeat.

#### Common mistakes

- Python's `//` and `%` round toward minus infinity for negatives: `-7 // 2` is −4, not −3, and `-7 % 2` is 1. Many other languages truncate toward zero. Say which language you mean.
- Confusing truncation with rounding: truncating 2.99 gives 2.

### 4.1.1.4 Relational operations

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.4 · OCR 2.2.1(a).*

#### What you need to know

- Equal to (= in pseudo-code, == in Python), not equal to (≠ or <>, != in Python), less than, greater than, less than or equal to, greater than or equal to.
- A relational operation always gives a Boolean.
- Strings compare character by character by character code, so "Zebra" < "apple" is True in ASCII because 'Z' (90) < 'a' (97).

#### Common mistakes

- Using = (assignment in Python) for comparison.
- Off-by-one errors from < instead of <= in loop conditions. Trace the boundary value.
- Comparing floats for exact equality: `0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3` is False in Python. Compare with a small tolerance instead.

### 4.1.1.5 Boolean operations

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.5 · OCR 2.2.1(a) · Eduqas C1.3: Logical operations in algorithms and programs.*

#### What you need to know

- NOT, AND, OR and XOR (exclusive or: true when exactly one input is true). Eduqas also names NAND (NOT AND) and NOR (NOT OR) in programs.
- Precedence in most languages: NOT first, then AND, then OR. Use brackets to make meaning clear.
- Many languages short-circuit: in `A AND B`, B is not evaluated if A is false; in `A OR B`, B is not evaluated if A is true.

#### Explained

Python has no logical XOR keyword, but for Booleans `a != b` is XOR, and `^` is bitwise XOR on integers.

```python
def valid_login(user_ok, pass_ok, locked):
    return user_ok and pass_ok and not locked

i = 10
data = [1, 2, 3]
if i < len(data) and data[i] > 0:   # short-circuit stops the index error
    print("positive")

a, b = True, False
print(a != b)          # XOR → True
print(not (a and b))   # NAND → True
print(not (a or b))    # NOR → False
```

Truth tables for gates are in [22-logic-gates-and-boolean-algebra.md](22-logic-gates-and-boolean-algebra.md).

#### Common mistakes

- Writing `if x == 1 or 2:`. This is always true because `2` alone is truthy. Write `x == 1 or x == 2`.
- Swapping the order of a short-circuit test (`data[i] > 0 and i < len(data)`) so the unsafe check runs first.

### 4.1.1.6 Constants and variables

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.6 · Eduqas C1.3: Variables and constants.*

#### What you need to know

- A variable is a named store whose value can change while the program runs. A constant is a named value that cannot change while the program runs.
- Advantages of named constants: one change in one place updates every use; the name explains the value (`VAT_RATE` not `0.2`); the compiler or interpreter can stop accidental changes; some compilers can substitute the value and save memory.

#### Explained

Python has no true constants. The convention is capital letters, and tools (and readers) treat them as fixed. In C# you would write `const double VatRate = 0.2;` and changing it is a compile-time error.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a constant "can never be changed". The programmer can edit its value in the source code; it is the running program that cannot change it.

### 4.1.1.7 String-handling operations

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.7 · OCR 2.2.1(a).*

#### What you need to know

- Length, position (index of a character or substring), substring, concatenation, character to character code, character code to character.
- Conversions: string ↔ integer, string ↔ float, and date/time ↔ string.
- Strings are usually indexed from 0. Python strings are immutable: you build a new string rather than change one in place.

| Operation | Python | Result for s = "Computer" |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Length | len(s) | 8 |
| Position | s.index("p") | 3 |
| Substring | s[3:6] | "put" (index 3 up to but not including 6) |
| Concatenation | s + "s" | "Computers" |
| Char → code | ord("C") | 67 |
| Code → char | chr(67) | "C" |
| String → int / float | int("42"), float("4.5") | 42, 4.5 |
| Int / float → string | str(42) | "42" |
| String ↔ date | datetime.strptime("04/09/2025", "%d/%m/%Y"), d.strftime("%d/%m/%Y") | date object, "04/09/2025" |

#### Worked example

Write a function that applies a shift of 3 to capital letters (a Caesar shift), using character codes.

```python
def shift(text, k):
    out = ""
    for ch in text:
        if "A" <= ch <= "Z":
            code = (ord(ch) - 65 + k) % 26 + 65   # 65 is the code for "A"
            out += chr(code)
        else:
            out += ch
    return out

print(shift("XYZ", 3))   # ABC: X is 88 → (23+3) mod 26 = 0 → 65 "A"
```

#### Common mistakes

- Mixing up AQA pseudo-code `SUBSTRING(start, end, s)`, which includes the end index, with Python slicing, which excludes it. Read the question's definition.
- Concatenating a number and a string without converting: `"Score: " + 5` is an error in Python.

### 4.1.1.8 Random number generation

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.8.*

#### What you need to know

- Languages provide pseudo-random number generators (PRNGs). They produce a sequence that looks random from a starting seed; the same seed gives the same sequence.
- Typical uses: games, simulations, sampling, generating test data, shuffling.

```python
import random
random.seed(1)                 # repeatable, useful for testing
die = random.randint(1, 6)     # integer from 1 to 6 inclusive
p = random.random()            # float with 0 <= p < 1
card = random.choice(["H", "D", "C", "S"])
```

#### Common mistakes

- Python's `randint(1, 6)` includes 6, but `randrange(1, 6)` does not. Check the bounds of the function you use.
- Calling PRNG output truly random. It is deterministic; for security (keys, tokens) use a cryptographically secure generator (Python's `secrets` module).

### 4.1.1.9 Exception handling

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.9.*

#### What you need to know

- An exception is an error or unexpected event that happens while a program is running (a run-time error), such as dividing by zero, a missing file or input of the wrong type.
- Exception handling means catching the exception and running code that deals with it, instead of letting the program crash.
- Structure: try (the code that might fail), catch/except (what to do for each type of error), and optionally finally (code that always runs, such as closing a file).
- When an exception is raised, normal execution stops, the current state is saved, and control jumps to the handler. If the subroutine has no handler, the exception passes back up to the caller, and so on up the call stack.

```python
def read_mark():
    while True:
        try:
            mark = int(input("Mark 0-100: "))
            if not 0 <= mark <= 100:
                raise ValueError("out of range")
            return mark
        except ValueError:
            print("Enter a whole number from 0 to 100.")

try:
    f = open("marks.txt")
    data = f.read()
except FileNotFoundError:
    data = ""
finally:
    print("Done trying to read")
```

#### Common mistakes

- Catching every exception with a bare `except:` and doing nothing. The error is hidden, which makes bugs harder to find.
- Confusing exception handling with validation. Validation checks data before use; exception handling deals with failures when they happen. Robust programs use both.

### 4.1.1.10 Subroutines

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.10 · OCR 2.2.1(d) · Eduqas C1.3: Modular programming.*

#### What you need to know

- A subroutine is a named "out of line" block of code that runs (is called) when its name is written in a statement. Control passes to it and returns to the statement after the call.
- A function returns a value; a procedure does not (though AQA treats both as subroutines).
- Advantages: code is written once and reused; programs are split into small parts that are easier to write, test, debug and read; different programmers can write different subroutines; changes are made in one place; subroutines can go into a library for other programs.
- Eduqas: standard (built-in) functions such as `len` and `abs`, standard modules (libraries such as `math`), and user-defined subprograms. Library code is already tested and saves time, but you may not know exactly how it works, and it may be larger than you need.

#### Common mistakes

- Answering "makes the program shorter" alone. Examiners want the reason: no repeated code, so changes and testing happen once.

### 4.1.1.11 Parameters of subroutines

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.11 · OCR 2.2.1(d) · Eduqas C1.3: Parameters.*

#### What you need to know

- A parameter is a variable named in the subroutine definition that receives a value when it is called. The value passed in the call is the argument.
- A subroutine's interface is its name, its parameters (number, order and types) and its return type. Callers only need the interface, not the code inside.
- By value: a copy of the argument is passed. Changes inside the subroutine do not affect the caller's variable. Safer; uses extra memory for large data.
- By reference: the address of the argument is passed. Changes inside the subroutine change the caller's variable. Saves memory and time for large structures, and lets a subroutine send back more than one result, but causes side effects that are hard to trace.

#### Explained

Python passes object references by value ("pass by object reference"). If the subroutine reassigns the parameter, the caller is not affected (like by value). If it changes a mutable object such as a list in place, the caller sees the change (like by reference).

```python
def add_one(n):          # behaves like by value
    n = n + 1
    return n

def add_item(items):     # behaves like by reference for a mutable list
    items.append(99)

x = 5
add_one(x)
print(x)                 # 5

lst = [1, 2]
add_item(lst)
print(lst)               # [1, 2, 99]
```

#### Worked example

Pseudo-code with explicit passing (OCR style):

```
procedure swap(byRef a, byRef b)
    temp = a
    a = b
    b = temp
endprocedure

x = 3
y = 7
swap(x, y)
print(x, y)
```

Output: 7 3. If both parameters were `byVal`, the swap would happen only to the copies and the output would be 3 7.

#### Common mistakes

- Using "parameter" and "argument" as if they were the same in a definition question. The parameter is in the definition; the argument is the value in the call.
- Saying by reference "copies the variable". It passes its address.

### 4.1.1.12 Returning values from a subroutine

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.12 · OCR 2.2.1(d).*

#### What you need to know

- A function sends a value back to the caller with `return`; the call then stands for that value in an expression.
- Many languages return one value; to return several, return a record, array or tuple, or use by-reference parameters.

```python
def min_max(values):
    lo = hi = values[0]
    for v in values[1:]:
        lo = min(lo, v)
        hi = max(hi, v)
    return lo, hi          # a tuple holding two values

smallest, largest = min_max([4, 9, 1, 7])
```

#### Common mistakes

- Printing inside a function instead of returning the value, so the caller cannot use it.

### 4.1.1.13 Local variables in subroutines

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.13 · OCR 2.2.1(c) · Eduqas C1.3: Scope of variables.*

#### What you need to know

- A local variable is declared inside a subroutine. It exists only while the subroutine runs (its lifetime) and can be used only inside it (its scope).
- Good practice because: subroutines are self-contained and reusable; the same name can be used in different subroutines without clashes; memory is freed when the subroutine ends; there are no hidden side effects on the rest of the program; subroutines can be tested on their own.

### 4.1.1.14 Global variables

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.14 · OCR 2.2.1(c) · Eduqas C1.3: Scope of variables.*

#### What you need to know

- A global variable is declared in the main program, outside any subroutine. Its scope is the whole program and its lifetime is the whole run.
- Contrast: globals are easy to share but any part of the program can change them, so errors are hard to trace; they use memory for the whole run; they make subroutines depend on the rest of the program. If a local and a global share a name, the local hides (shadows) the global inside that subroutine.
- Sensible uses: values that genuinely belong to the whole program and rarely change, such as settings (often better as constants).

```python
count = 0                  # global

def bump():
    global count           # Python needs this to assign to the global
    count += 1

def shadow():
    count = 100            # a new local, hides the global
    return count

bump()
print(shadow(), count)     # 100 1
```

#### Worked example

State the output and the scope of each `n`.

```python
n = 5
def f(n):
    n = n * 2
    return n
print(f(n) + n)
```

Inside `f`, `n` is a parameter, so it is local to `f`. `f(5)` returns 10; the global `n` is still 5. Output: 15.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a local variable "keeps its value between calls". It is created afresh on each call (unless declared static, which is a separate idea).

### Check questions

1. State the difference between definite and indefinite iteration, and explain when a condition-at-end loop must be used. [3 marks]
2. Evaluate: (a) 29 DIV 4, (b) 29 MOD 4, (c) truncate(−3.7), (d) 2 ^ 10. [4 marks]
3. Explain two advantages of using a named constant for a tax rate. [2 marks]
4. A program reads integers until the user types −1, then outputs how many numbers were greater than 50. Write it in Python or pseudo-code. Use a rogue value. [5 marks]
5. Using character codes, write a function `upper_first(word)` that returns the word with its first letter capitalised if it is a lower-case letter a–z. Do not use built-in case conversion. [4 marks]
6. Explain the difference between passing a parameter by value and by reference, giving one advantage of each. [4 marks]
7. Explain why a programmer should use local rather than global variables. [3 marks]
8. A user types "twelve" when a program expects an integer. Describe how exception handling could stop the program crashing. [3 marks]
9. State the output of this code and explain it. [3 marks]

```python
total = 1
def g(total):
    total = total + 10
    return total * 2
x = g(total)
print(x, total)
```

10. Give the minimum storage in bits for: a Boolean; an ASCII character; a string of 12 ASCII characters (ignore any length field). [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Definite: number of repeats known before the loop starts (1). Indefinite: repeats until a condition is met (1). Condition-at-end loop when the body must run at least once, e.g. asking for input before it can be validated (1).
2. (a) 7 (1); (b) 1 (1); (c) −3 (1); (d) 1024 (1).
3. Any two: change the value in one place only (1); the name makes the code easier to read/understand (1); the value cannot be changed accidentally while the program runs (1).
4. Example:

```python
count = 0
n = int(input())
while n != -1:
    if n > 50:
        count = count + 1
    n = int(input())
print(count)
```

Counter initialised to 0 (1); first input before the loop (1); loop ends on −1 and −1 is not counted (1); correct condition and increment (1); output after the loop (1). Accept a condition-at-end loop that skips counting −1.

5. Example:

```python
def upper_first(word):
    if word == "":
        return word
    c = word[0]
    if "a" <= c <= "z":
        c = chr(ord(c) - 32)
    return c + word[1:]
```

Takes first character (1); tests it is in a–z (1); uses ord/chr with offset 32 (1); joins the rest of the word and returns (1).
6. By value: a copy of the value is passed so changes inside do not affect the original (1); advantage: no side effects/original data protected (1). By reference: the address/location is passed so changes affect the original (1); advantage: no copy needed so saves memory/time for large structures, or allows more than one result back (1).
7. Any three: local variables exist only while the subroutine runs so free memory (1); cannot be changed accidentally by other parts of the program (1); same identifier can be reused in different subroutines (1); subroutine is self-contained so easier to test/reuse (1).
8. Put the conversion inside a try block (1); a ValueError/exception is raised when the conversion fails and control passes to the except/catch block (1); handler shows a message and asks again/sets a default, so the program continues (1).
9. Output: 22 1 (1). The parameter `total` is local to `g`; g returns (1 + 10) × 2 = 22 (1); the global `total` is unchanged, still 1 (1).
10. Boolean 1 bit (1); ASCII character 7 bits (accept 8 bits/1 byte if extended ASCII stated) (1); 12 characters: 84 bits (accept 96 bits/12 bytes for 8-bit) (1).

## Stack frames, recursion and programming paradigms

This file covers how a call stack makes subroutine calls work, recursion (base case, general case, tracing, and comparing with iteration), what a programming paradigm is, and the structured (procedural) approach with hierarchy charts. Object-oriented programming has its own file, [03-object-oriented-programming.md](03-object-oriented-programming.md); functional programming is in [32-functional-programming.md](32-functional-programming.md).

Builds on [GCSE: Subprograms and structured programming](../../gcse/computer-science/05-subprograms-and-structured-programming.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stack frames | Return address, parameters, local variables (4.1.1.15) | Implied by recursion (2.2.1(b)) | Implied by procedure calling and return (C1.3: Parameters) |
| Recursion | Base and general case, solve simple problems | Use recursion and compare with iteration | Use recursion; its "potential elegance" |
| Paradigms | Procedural and object-oriented | Need for and characteristics of a variety of paradigms; procedural | Procedural, event-driven, visual and mark-up; choose a type of language for a problem |
| Hierarchy charts | Named | Not named | Not named |

### 4.1.1.15 Role of stack frames in subroutine calls

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.15 · OCR 2.2.1(b) · Eduqas C1.3: Parameters.*

#### What you need to know

- When a subroutine is called, a stack frame (activation record) is pushed onto the call stack. It holds:
  - the return address: where to continue in the caller when the subroutine ends;
  - the parameters (the values or addresses passed in);
  - the local variables of the subroutine.
- When the subroutine ends, its frame is popped: the return address is used to jump back, and its locals and parameters disappear.
- Because a stack is last in, first out, the most recently called subroutine is always the one that finishes first. This is what allows nested calls and recursion.
- If calls nest too deeply (often from recursion with no reachable base case), the stack runs out of memory: a stack overflow.

#### Explained

Picture the program:

```python
def square(n):
    result = n * n
    return result

def sum_squares(a, b):
    total = square(a) + square(b)
    return total

print(sum_squares(3, 4))
```

| Moment | Call stack (top at right) |
| --- | --- |
| main calls sum_squares(3, 4) | main · [sum_squares: return → print line; a=3, b=4; total] |
| sum_squares calls square(3) | main · [sum_squares …] · [square: return → middle of the total line; n=3; result] |
| square(3) returns 9 | main · [sum_squares …] |
| sum_squares calls square(4), gets 16 | push then pop again |
| sum_squares returns 25 | main |

#### Common mistakes

- Listing "the code of the subroutine" as part of a stack frame. The code is stored once elsewhere; only the return address, parameters and locals go in the frame (plus, in real systems, saved registers).
- Saying the stack is first in, first out.

### 4.1.1.16 Recursive techniques

*Refs: AQA 4.1.1.16 · OCR 2.2.1(b) · Eduqas C1.3: Recursion.*

#### What you need to know

- A recursive subroutine calls itself.
- It needs a base case (a stopping condition that returns without a recursive call) and a general case (which calls itself on a smaller version of the problem, moving towards the base case).
- Each call gets its own stack frame, so each call has its own copy of the parameters and locals.
- Without a reachable base case, calls never stop and the stack overflows.
- Recursion vs iteration:
  - Recursion: often shorter, closer to the mathematical definition, natural for trees, divide-and-conquer and backtracking ("elegant"). But each call uses stack memory and call overhead, so it is usually slower and can overflow; harder to trace.
  - Iteration: uses a fixed amount of memory and no call overhead, but some problems (tree traversal, Towers of Hanoi) are awkward to write without an explicit stack.
- Any recursive algorithm can be rewritten iteratively (sometimes with your own stack), and vice versa.

#### Worked example

Factorial: n! = n × (n − 1)!, with 0! = 1.

```python
def fact(n):
    if n == 0:            # base case
        return 1
    return n * fact(n - 1)   # general case
```

Trace `fact(3)`:

| Call | n | Waits for | Returns |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| fact(3) | 3 | 3 × fact(2) | 3 × 2 = 6 |
| fact(2) | 2 | 2 × fact(1) | 2 × 1 = 2 |
| fact(1) | 1 | 1 × fact(0) | 1 × 1 = 1 |
| fact(0) | 0 | (base case) | 1 |

Calls go down (four frames on the stack at the deepest point), then results come back up in reverse order.

Trace this mystery function for `m(13)` and state its purpose.

```python
def m(n):
    if n < 2:
        return str(n)
    return m(n // 2) + str(n % 2)
```

m(13) = m(6) + "1"; m(6) = m(3) + "0"; m(3) = m(1) + "1"; m(1) = "1". Unwinding: "1" + "1" = "11", + "0" = "110", + "1" = "1101". It converts a non-negative integer to binary (13 = 1101).

Iterative version of factorial for comparison:

```python
def fact_iter(n):
    result = 1
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        result *= i
    return result
```

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the base case after the recursive call, so it is never reached.
- Forgetting to `return` the recursive call's value in a function, so `None` comes back.
- Saying recursion is "more efficient". It is usually less efficient in memory and time; its advantage is clarity for naturally recursive problems.

### 4.1.2.1 Programming paradigms

*Refs: AQA 4.1.2.1 · OCR 1.2.4(a) · Eduqas C1.4: Programming paradigms, Types of computer language.*

#### What you need to know

- A programming paradigm is a style or approach to programming, with its own way of structuring code and thinking about a problem. Languages are designed around one or more paradigms.
- Imperative paradigms say how to solve the problem as a sequence of commands that change the program's state:
  - Procedural: the program is a sequence of instructions grouped into procedures and functions that act on data passed to them. Examples: C, Pascal, and Python used procedurally.
  - Object-oriented: the program is a set of objects, each combining data (attributes) and the code that works on it (methods), which interact by sending messages. Examples: Java, C#.
- Declarative paradigms say what the result should be, not how to get it:
  - Functional: programs are built from pure functions with no side effects and no changing state (Haskell). See file 32.
  - Logic: facts and rules are stated, and the system searches for answers to queries (Prolog).
  - Query languages such as SQL are declarative too.
- Eduqas names further types:
  - Event-driven: the flow of the program is decided by events (mouse clicks, key presses, sensor signals, messages). An event loop waits for events and calls the matching event handler. Used for GUIs and games.
  - Visual: programs are built by arranging graphical blocks or connecting icons (Scratch, block-based tools for robots). Good for beginners and for quick prototypes; limited for large systems.
  - Mark-up: not programming languages in the full sense. They describe the structure and presentation of a document using tags (HTML, XML). They have no general processing such as loops.
- Choosing a paradigm or language for a problem: think about the problem's nature (data-heavy, interactive, mathematical), speed and hardware access needed (low-level for drivers), the team's skills, available libraries, and the target platform.
- OCR also says why so many paradigms exist: different problems suit different ways of thinking, and some ideas are easier to express in one style.

#### Explained

| Problem | A sensible choice | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Device driver for a sensor | Low-level/procedural (assembly, C) | Direct hardware control, speed, small memory |
| Large business system with many similar entities | Object-oriented | Classes model entities; inheritance and encapsulation help teams and maintenance |
| GUI app or game | Event-driven (often OO too) | Responds to user actions in any order |
| Parallel data processing at scale | Functional | No side effects, so work can be split safely across processors |
| Expert system of rules | Logic (Prolog) | Facts and rules map directly to the language |
| Web page layout | Mark-up (HTML with CSS) | Describes structure, not processing |
| Querying a database | SQL (declarative) | Say what data you want; the DBMS works out how |

```python
## Event-driven sketch using Python's tkinter
import tkinter as tk

def on_click():                 # event handler
    label.config(text="Clicked!")

root = tk.Tk()
label = tk.Label(root, text="Waiting")
button = tk.Button(root, text="Press", command=on_click)  # bind event to handler
label.pack(); button.pack()
root.mainloop()                 # event loop: waits for events, dispatches them
```

#### Common mistakes

- Calling HTML a programming language without qualification. It is a mark-up language.
- Thinking a language belongs to one paradigm only. Python supports procedural, object-oriented and functional styles.

### 4.1.2.2 Procedural-oriented programming

*Refs: AQA 4.1.2.2 · OCR 1.2.4(b).*

#### What you need to know

- The structured approach designs a program top-down: break the problem into sub-problems, then break those down until each is small enough to code as one subroutine.
- It uses only the three control structures (sequence, selection, iteration), with no unstructured jumps (GOTO), and subroutines that each do one job, communicating through parameters and return values rather than globals.
- A hierarchy chart shows the program as a tree of modules: the whole program at the top, each box split into the subroutines it calls. It shows structure, not order or conditions.
- Advantages: each module is small, so easier to write, test and debug; modules can be developed by different people in parallel; modules can be reused; the program is easier to read and maintain; errors are confined to one module.
- OCR 1.2.4(b) lists procedural techniques: program flow, variables and constants, procedures and functions, arithmetic, Boolean and assignment operators, string handling and file handling (all in files 01 and 04).

#### Worked example

A program marks a quiz: it loads questions from a file, asks each one, scores the answers and saves a report.

```
                    Quiz program
        ┌──────────────┼───────────────┬───────────────┐
  Load questions   Run quiz        Show results    Save report
        │          ┌───┴────┐          │
   Open file    Ask       Check     Calculate %
   Read lines   question  answer
```

Each box becomes a subroutine. "Run quiz" calls "Ask question" and "Check answer" for each question; the chart does not show the loop, only that Run quiz uses them.

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing a hierarchy chart with decisions or loops. That is a flowchart.
- Calling "top-down design" a paradigm. It is a design method used within the procedural paradigm.

### Check questions

1. State three items stored in a stack frame and explain why each is needed. [3 marks]
2. Explain why a call stack must be last in, first out. [2 marks]
3. Write a recursive function `power(b, e)` that returns bᵉ for integer e ≥ 0. Identify the base case. [4 marks]
4. Trace `f(4)` and give the value returned. [3 marks]

```python
def f(n):
    if n <= 1:
        return 1
    return f(n - 1) + f(n - 2)
```

5. Give two disadvantages of recursion compared with iteration. [2 marks]
6. Explain what is meant by a stack overflow and how a recursive subroutine can cause one. [2 marks]
7. Describe two differences between the procedural and object-oriented paradigms. [4 marks]
8. (Eduqas) Describe the event-driven paradigm and give an example of where it would be used. [3 marks]
9. Draw a hierarchy chart for a program that reads exam results from a file, works out each student's grade, and prints a class summary with the average and highest mark. [4 marks]
10. Explain two advantages of the structured approach to program design. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Return address, so control goes back to the correct instruction in the caller (1); parameters, the values/addresses passed in, needed by the subroutine (1); local variables, storage for this call only (1).
2. The last subroutine called must finish first and return to the one that called it (1); so the most recent frame must be removed first (1).
3. Example:

```python
def power(b, e):
    if e == 0:
        return 1
    return b * power(b, e - 1)
```

Base case e == 0 returns 1 (1), identified (1); recursive call with e − 1 (1); multiplies by b and returns (1).
4. f(4) = f(3) + f(2); f(3) = f(2) + f(1); f(2) = f(1) + f(0) = 1 + 1 = 2 (1); f(3) = 2 + 1 = 3 (1); f(4) = 3 + 2 = 5 (1).
5. Any two: uses more memory as a frame is stored for each call (1); slower because of call overhead (1); risk of stack overflow for deep recursion (1); harder to trace/debug (1).
6. The call stack runs out of memory/space (1); each recursive call adds a frame and if the base case is never reached (or recursion is too deep) frames keep being added (1).
7. Any two pairs: procedural separates data and the procedures acting on it, OO combines them in objects (2); procedural program is a sequence of procedure calls, OO is objects interacting through methods (2); OO uses classes/inheritance/encapsulation, procedural does not (2).
8. Program flow is decided by events/user actions (1); an event loop listens for events and runs the matching event handler (1); example such as a GUI form, game or touchscreen kiosk (1).
9. Top box for the program (1); second level such as Load results, Calculate grades, Produce summary (1); third level under Produce summary such as Calculate average, Find highest, Print (1); correct tree form with no loops/decisions (1).
10. Any two: modules are smaller so easier to code/test/debug (1); modules can be shared among a team (1); modules can be reused (1); easier to maintain/understand (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/03-object-oriented-programming.md -->

## Object-oriented programming

This file covers classes, objects, instantiation and constructors, encapsulation and access specifiers, inheritance, polymorphism and overriding, aggregation and composition, abstract, virtual and static methods, class diagrams, and three OO design principles. There is no GCSE equivalent: OOP is new at A-level.

The whole of this file is one AQA spec point, so it is split into parts by idea.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Core ideas | Class, object, instantiation, encapsulation, inheritance, aggregation, composition, polymorphism, overriding | Classes, objects, methods, attributes, inheritance, encapsulation, polymorphism | Object, class and method, and the role of the OO approach |
| Methods | Abstract, virtual, static; public, private, protected | Not named beyond methods | Not named |
| Diagrams | Class diagrams with +, −, #, inheritance arrows, black and white diamonds | Not required | Not required |
| Design principles | Encapsulate what varies; favour composition over inheritance; program to interfaces | Not named | Not named |
| Code in exam | Write OO code on screen (Paper 1) | Read and write OCR-style pseudo-code classes | Describe |

OCR's exam pseudo-code uses `class Pet`, `private name`, `public procedure new(...)` (the constructor), `inherits`, and `super.new(...)`.

### Classes, objects and instantiation

*Refs: AQA 4.1.2.3 · OCR 1.2.4(e), 2.2.1(f) · Eduqas C1.4: Programming paradigms.*

#### What you need to know

- A class is a blueprint (template) that defines the attributes (properties, fields: the data) and methods (the procedures and functions) that all objects of that type will have.
- An object is an instance of a class: a particular thing with its own values for the attributes.
- Instantiation is creating an object from a class. A constructor is the special method that runs when an object is created, usually to set the initial attribute values. It can be explicit (written by you) or implicit (provided by the language). A reference to the new object is stored in a variable of the class type.
- A method is a subroutine that belongs to a class and works on an object's attributes.
- Why OO is used: it models real-world things naturally; code is organised into self-contained, reusable classes; encapsulation protects data; inheritance reduces repeated code; large programs are easier for teams to build and maintain; classes can be tested on their own.

#### Worked example

```python
class BankAccount:
    interest_rate = 0.02                 # class (static) attribute, shared by all

    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):   # constructor
        self.__owner = owner             # private attribute (name mangled)
        self.__balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):           # public method
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("amount must be positive")
        self.__balance += amount

    def get_balance(self):               # getter
        return self.__balance

a = BankAccount("Sana", 100)             # instantiation
b = BankAccount("Tom")                   # a second, separate object
a.deposit(50)
print(a.get_balance(), b.get_balance())  # 150 0
```

### Encapsulation and access specifiers

#### What you need to know

- Encapsulation is bundling an object's data and the methods that act on it in one unit (the class), and hiding the data so it can only be accessed through those methods (information hiding).
- Access specifiers:
  - public (+ in a class diagram): accessible from anywhere;
  - private (− ): accessible only inside the class itself;
  - protected (#): accessible inside the class and its subclasses.
- Attributes are usually private, with public getter and setter methods. The setter can validate new values, so the object can never get into an invalid state.
- Benefits: data cannot be corrupted by other code; the internal design can change without breaking code that uses the class, as long as the public methods stay the same; reduces errors in team projects.

#### Explained

Python has no enforced access specifiers. A leading double underscore makes Python rename (mangle) the attribute, which stops casual access from outside; a single underscore is the convention for protected. In Java or C# `private` is enforced by the compiler.

#### Common mistakes

- Defining encapsulation only as "hiding data". Mention combining data and methods into one object too.
- Thinking private attributes cannot be read at all. They can be read through public methods the class chooses to provide.

### Inheritance

#### What you need to know

- Inheritance lets a subclass (child, derived class) take on all the attributes and methods of a superclass (parent, base class), and add its own or change some. It models an "is a" relationship: a SavingsAccount is a BankAccount.
- Benefits: code written once in the superclass is reused; changes to the superclass flow to all subclasses; a clear hierarchy.
- Drawbacks: subclasses depend on the superclass, so changing it can break them (tight coupling); deep hierarchies are hard to follow.
- In a class diagram, inheritance is a line with a hollow triangle arrowhead pointing to the superclass. AQA only examines single inheritance (one superclass).

#### Worked example

```python
class SavingsAccount(BankAccount):        # inherits BankAccount
    def __init__(self, owner, balance, limit):
        super().__init__(owner, balance)  # call the superclass constructor
        self.__withdrawals_left = limit   # new attribute

    def add_interest(self):               # new method
        self.deposit(self.get_balance() * self.interest_rate)
```

OCR pseudo-code for the same idea:

```
class SavingsAccount inherits BankAccount
    private withdrawalsLeft
    public procedure new(givenOwner, givenBalance, limit)
        super.new(givenOwner, givenBalance)
        withdrawalsLeft = limit
    endprocedure
endclass
```

### Polymorphism and overriding

#### What you need to know

- Polymorphism ("many forms") means one method name or interface can behave differently depending on the class of the object it is called on. A program can treat objects of different subclasses through their shared superclass, and each runs its own version.
- Overriding: a subclass provides its own version of a method that is already defined in its superclass, with the same name and parameters. The subclass version is the one that runs for subclass objects.
- (Overloading, several methods with the same name but different parameters in one class, is a different idea; not every language supports it.)

#### Worked example

```python
class Shape:
    def area(self):
        raise NotImplementedError

class Rectangle(Shape):
    def __init__(self, w, h):
        self.w, self.h = w, h
    def area(self):                   # overrides Shape.area
        return self.w * self.h

class Circle(Shape):
    def __init__(self, r):
        self.r = r
    def area(self):                   # overrides Shape.area
        return 3.14159 * self.r ** 2

shapes = [Rectangle(2, 3), Circle(1)]
for s in shapes:
    print(s.area())    # 6, then 3.14159: the same call, different behaviour
```

The loop does not need to know which shape it has. Adding a Triangle class later needs no change to the loop.

### Abstract, virtual and static methods

#### What you need to know

- Abstract method: declared in a superclass with no body (no implementation). Every concrete subclass must override it. A class with an abstract method is an abstract class and cannot be instantiated.
- Virtual method: has a body in the superclass but may be overridden in a subclass. (In C# a method must be marked `virtual` to be overridable; in Java and Python all ordinary methods behave as virtual.)
- Static method: belongs to the class itself, not to any object. It is called through the class name and cannot use any one object's attributes. Useful for utility functions and for counting how many objects have been made.

```python
from abc import ABC, abstractmethod

class Employee(ABC):                     # abstract class
    count = 0                            # static (class) attribute

    def __init__(self, name):
        self.name = name
        Employee.count += 1

    @abstractmethod
    def pay(self):                       # abstract method: no body
        ...

    def describe(self):                  # virtual: can be overridden
        return f"{self.name} earns {self.pay()}"

    @staticmethod
    def total_staff():                   # static method
        return Employee.count

class Salaried(Employee):
    def __init__(self, name, salary):
        super().__init__(name)
        self.salary = salary
    def pay(self):
        return self.salary / 12

print(Salaried("Ana", 36000).describe())  # Ana earns 3000.0
print(Employee.total_staff())             # 1
## Employee("X") would raise TypeError: abstract class
```

### Aggregation and composition

#### What you need to know

- Both are "has a" relationships (association) where one object contains or uses others.
- Composition: the contained objects cannot exist without the container. If the container is destroyed, so are its parts. Example: a House has Rooms. In a class diagram: a line with a filled (black) diamond at the container end.
- Aggregation: the contained objects can exist independently of the container. If the container is destroyed, they survive. Example: a Team has Players; disband the team and the players still exist. In a class diagram: a line with a hollow (white) diamond at the container end.

```python
class Room:
    def __init__(self, name): self.name = name

class House:                               # composition
    def __init__(self):
        self.rooms = [Room("Kitchen"), Room("Bedroom")]   # created inside, die with house

class Player:
    def __init__(self, name): self.name = name

class Team:                                # aggregation
    def __init__(self, players):
        self.players = players             # passed in: exist outside the team

p = [Player("Lee"), Player("Mo")]
t = Team(p)
del t                                      # players in p still exist
```

### Class diagrams

#### What you need to know

- A class box has three parts: class name; attributes (with access symbol and often type); methods (with access symbol, parameters and return type).
- Symbols: + public, − private, # protected. Inheritance: hollow triangle arrow to the superclass. Composition: black diamond at the whole. Aggregation: white diamond at the whole.

#### Worked example

```
┌──────────────────────────┐
│ Vehicle                  │
├──────────────────────────┤
│ - registration: String   │
│ # speed: Integer         │
├──────────────────────────┤
│ + Vehicle(reg: String)   │
│ + accelerate(): void     │
│ + getReg(): String       │
└──────────────────────────┘
             △
     ┌───────┴───────┐
┌─────────┐     ┌─────────┐          ◆───── Engine    (Car is composed of an Engine)
│ Car     │     │ Lorry   │
├─────────┤     ├─────────┤          ◇───── Driver    (Lorry aggregates a Driver)
│ - doors │     │ - load  │
└─────────┘     └─────────┘
```

Reading it: Car and Lorry inherit from Vehicle; `speed` is protected so Car's own methods can change it; `registration` is private so Car must use `getReg()`. A Car's Engine is created and destroyed with the Car (composition); a Driver exists independently of any Lorry (aggregation).

### Object-oriented design principles

#### What you need to know

- Encapsulate what varies: put the parts of a design that are likely to change behind their own class or interface, so changes do not ripple through the rest of the program.
- Favour composition over inheritance: build objects by combining other objects ("has a") rather than by deep "is a" hierarchies. Composition is more flexible (parts can be swapped at run time) and avoids fragile hierarchies.
- Program to interfaces, not implementation: write code that depends on what an object can do (its interface, often an abstract class), not on a particular concrete class. Then any class meeting the interface can be used. AQA says you will not be tested on writing code to interfaces in the practical exam.

#### Explained

A game character can fly, swim or walk, and abilities change in play. With inheritance you might need FlyingSwimmingCharacter, WalkingFlyingCharacter and so on. With composition, a Character has a Movement object, and you swap `FlyMovement()` for `SwimMovement()` at run time. The changing part (how it moves) is encapsulated in its own classes, and Character only uses the Movement interface.

### Common mistakes

- Mixing up the diamonds. Black (filled) = composition = parts die with the whole.
- Saying polymorphism "is when a subclass inherits methods". Inheritance passes methods down; polymorphism is the same call behaving differently depending on the object's class.
- Writing `class` when the question asks for an object, or the reverse: a class is the template, an object is an instance.
- Forgetting to call the superclass constructor in a subclass constructor, so inherited attributes are never set.

### Check questions

1. Define the terms class, object and instantiation. [3 marks]
2. Explain what is meant by encapsulation and why it is useful. [3 marks]
3. Explain the difference between aggregation and composition, with an example of each. [4 marks]
4. A superclass `Animal` has a method `speak()` that returns "...". Subclasses `Dog` and `Cat` return "Woof" and "Miaow". Write the classes in Python or OCR pseudo-code, and explain how this shows polymorphism. [5 marks]
5. State what is meant by an abstract method, and explain why a class containing one cannot be instantiated. [2 marks]
6. Give one use of a static method. [1 mark]
7. In a class diagram, what do the symbols +, − and # mean? [3 marks]
8. A `Library` class contains a list of `Book` objects that are created elsewhere and can be moved between libraries. Draw the class diagram relationship and name it. [2 marks]
9. Explain the principle "favour composition over inheritance". [2 marks]
10. Explain why a setter method is preferred to making an attribute public. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Class: a template/blueprint defining attributes and methods (1). Object: an instance of a class with its own attribute values (1). Instantiation: creating an object from a class (using a constructor) (1).
2. Combining data and the methods that act on it in one object (1); data hidden/private, accessed only through methods (1); so data cannot be changed in invalid/unexpected ways, or internal code can change without affecting other code (1).
3. Both are "has a" relationships (1). Composition: contained objects cannot exist without the container/destroyed with it, e.g. a house and its rooms (1 + 1 for example). Aggregation: contained objects exist independently, e.g. a team and its players (1). (Max 4; examples must match the definitions.)
4. Example:

```python
class Animal:
    def speak(self): return "..."
class Dog(Animal):
    def speak(self): return "Woof"
class Cat(Animal):
    def speak(self): return "Miaow"
```

Animal class with speak (1); Dog and Cat inherit from Animal (1); each overrides speak (1). Explanation: the same method call `speak()` gives different results depending on the object's class (1); code can treat any Animal the same way, e.g. a loop over a list of animals (1).
5. A method with no implementation that subclasses must override (1); the class is incomplete, as calling the method on an object would have no code to run (1).
6. Any one: a utility function not needing object data, e.g. a conversion; a method to count/return the number of objects created (1).
7. + public (1); − private (1); # protected (1).
8. Hollow (white) diamond at the Library end, line to Book (1); aggregation (1).
9. Build classes by combining objects ("has a") rather than extending classes ("is a") (1); because it is more flexible, e.g. parts can be swapped at run time, and avoids fragile/deep hierarchies (1).
10. The setter can validate the new value before storing it (1); so the object cannot be put into an invalid state / the internal representation can be changed without affecting callers (1).
