## Arrays, records, files and abstract data types

This file covers what a data structure is, one- and multi-dimensional arrays (up to three dimensions), records, lists and tuples, reading and writing text and binary files, abstract data types, and static versus dynamic structures. Stacks, queues and linked lists are in [05-stacks-queues-and-linked-lists.md](05-stacks-queues-and-linked-lists.md); graphs, trees, hash tables and dictionaries in [06-graphs-trees-hash-tables-and-dictionaries.md](06-graphs-trees-hash-tables-and-dictionaries.md); vectors in [07-vectors.md](07-vectors.md). File organisation (serial, sequential, indexed and direct access) for Eduqas is in [36-file-organisation-and-data-security.md](36-file-organisation-and-data-security.md).

Builds on [GCSE: Data structures, strings and files](../../gcse/computer-science/04-data-structures-strings-and-files.md), which covers 1D and 2D arrays, records and simple text files.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Arrays | 1D and n-dimensional; 1D as a vector, 2D as a matrix | Up to 3 dimensions | Up to 3 dimensions; manipulate records and arrays |
| Lists and tuples | Not named | Named (1.4.2(a)) | Not named |
| Files | Text and binary files | File handling (1.2.4(b) in procedural techniques) | Files in Component 2 section 4 (file 36) |
| ADTs | Named list; static vs dynamic | Not named as a term | Choose and justify a structure for a situation |

### 4.2.1.1 Data structures

*Refs: AQA 4.2.1.1 · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- A data structure is a way of organising and storing related data in a computer so that it can be used efficiently: a collection of data items together with the relationships between them and the operations that can be done on them.
- Choosing the right structure matters: it decides how quickly you can find, add and remove items, and how much memory you use.
- Eduqas: select, identify and justify a data structure for a situation.

#### Explained

| Situation | Good structure | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Marks for 30 students in 6 tests | 2D array | Fixed size, same type, indexed by student and test |
| Undo history in an editor | Stack | Last change undone first |
| Print jobs waiting | Queue | First come, first served |
| A road network | Graph | Places connected by roads with distances |
| Look up a product by barcode | Hash table / dictionary | Near-constant time search by key |
| Sorted list that changes a lot | Linked list or binary search tree | Insert without shifting items |
| One customer's details | Record | Fields of different types together |

### 4.2.1.2 Single- and multi-dimensional arrays

*Refs: AQA 4.2.1.2 · OCR 1.4.2(a) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- An array is a finite, ordered set of elements of the same data type, stored in contiguous (next-to-each-other) memory and accessed by index. Arrays are usually indexed from 0 (OCR and AQA pseudo-code: from 0 unless stated).
- A 1D array is indexed by one integer: a list of values, or a vector. A 2D array is indexed by two integers (row, column): a table or matrix. An n-dimensional array is indexed by a tuple of n integers. OCR and Eduqas use up to three dimensions.
- Because elements are the same size and contiguous, the address of any element can be calculated directly, so access by index takes constant time.
- A list (OCR) is an ordered collection that can grow and shrink and (in Python) hold mixed types; it is dynamic. A tuple is an ordered, immutable collection: once created, items cannot be changed, added or removed.

#### Explained

Address calculation for a 2D array stored row by row (row-major), with base address B, each element s bytes, and C columns: address of `[r][c]` = B + (r × C + c) × s. Example: B = 1000, s = 4, C = 5, element [2][3] → 1000 + (2 × 5 + 3) × 4 = 1052.

```python
## 2D: 3 students × 4 tests
marks = [[56, 71, 64, 80],
         [45, 52, 61, 49],
         [90, 88, 95, 91]]
print(marks[1][2])         # 61: student 1, test 2

## total for each student
for row in marks:
    print(sum(row))

## average of test 0 across students
print(sum(marks[s][0] for s in range(3)) / 3)

## 3D: 2 classes × 3 students × 4 tests
grid = [[[0] * 4 for _ in range(3)] for _ in range(2)]
grid[1][2][3] = 77         # class 1, student 2, test 3

point = (3, 4)             # tuple: immutable
## point[0] = 5 would raise TypeError
```

#### Worked example

A 3 × 3 noughts-and-crosses board is stored as `board[row][col]`. Write a function that returns True if a player has filled any row.

```python
def row_win(board, p):
    for r in range(3):
        if board[r][0] == board[r][1] == board[r][2] == p:
            return True
    return False
```

#### Common mistakes

- Swapping row and column order in a 2D index. State which index is which.
- Writing `[[0] * 4] * 3` in Python: this makes three references to the same row, so changing one row changes all three. Use a comprehension.
- Calling a Python list an array without comment. A Python list is dynamic and can mix types; a true array is fixed-size and single-type.

### 4.2.1.3 Fields, records and files

*Refs: AQA 4.2.1.3 · OCR 1.4.2(a) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- A record is a collection of related fields, possibly of different types, treated as one unit (one row about one entity). A field is one item of data in a record. A file is a collection of records stored on secondary storage.
- Text files store data as characters (human-readable, often one record per line, fields separated by a delimiter such as a comma). Binary files store data as raw bytes in the same form used in memory (not human-readable, but compact, fast, and able to store numbers and images exactly).
- File operations: open (for read, write or append), read a line/record, write, close; test for end of file.

#### Worked example

Write student records to a CSV text file, read them back, and write and read a binary file of integers.

```python
## write a text file (overwrites)
students = [("Ali", 72), ("Beth", 65)]
with open("students.txt", "w") as f:
    for name, mark in students:
        f.write(f"{name},{mark}\n")

## read it back, line by line until end of file
records = []
with open("students.txt", "r") as f:
    for line in f:
        name, mark = line.strip().split(",")
        records.append({"name": name, "mark": int(mark)})

## append a record
with open("students.txt", "a") as f:
    f.write("Cara,80\n")

## binary file: store integers as 4-byte values
import struct
with open("scores.bin", "wb") as f:
    for n in [10, 300, 70000]:
        f.write(struct.pack("<i", n))      # 4 bytes, little-endian integer
with open("scores.bin", "rb") as f:
    data = f.read()
nums = [struct.unpack("<i", data[i:i+4])[0] for i in range(0, len(data), 4)]
print(nums)   # [10, 300, 70000]
```

In a text file, 70000 takes 5 bytes plus a separator; in the binary file it takes exactly 4, and every value is the same length, which makes it possible to jump straight to the nth record.

OCR pseudo-code for file handling:

```
myFile = openRead("students.txt")
while NOT myFile.endOfFile()
    line = myFile.readLine()
    print(line)
endwhile
myFile.close()
```

#### Common mistakes

- Opening a file in write mode when you meant append, which wipes the file.
- Forgetting to convert text read from a file to a number before calculations.
- Forgetting to close a file (or to use `with`), so data may not be saved.

### 4.2.1.4 Abstract data types and static and dynamic structures

*Refs: AQA 4.2.1.4 · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- An abstract data type (ADT) is defined by its values and the operations allowed on it, not by how it is stored. A stack, for example, is anything that supports push, pop, peek, is-empty and is-full with last-in-first-out behaviour.
- The data structure is the concrete way an ADT is implemented, for example a stack built from an array and a top pointer, or from a linked list. This separation is data abstraction (see [11-abstraction-and-computational-thinking.md](11-abstraction-and-computational-thinking.md)).
- ADTs you must know: queue, stack, graph, tree, hash table, dictionary, vector. If a language does not provide them, you must be able to build them from arrays, records and pointers.
- Static data structure: size is fixed when it is created (usually at compile time), for example a fixed-length array.
- Dynamic data structure: size can change while the program runs, using memory allocated from the heap (a pool of free memory) as needed, for example a linked list or a Python list.

| | Static | Dynamic |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Size | Fixed in advance | Grows and shrinks at run time |
| Memory | May waste space if not full; can run out if too small | Uses only what is needed, plus pointer overhead per item |
| Access | Direct by index, fast (address calculated) | Often must follow pointers from the start (slower) |
| Implementation | Simpler | More complex; needs memory management |
| Risk | Overflow when full | Heap can run out; memory fragmentation |
| Good for | Known, fixed amount of data (days of the week, a game board) | Unknown or changing amount of data |

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a dynamic structure "has no maximum size". It is limited by available memory.
- Defining an ADT by its implementation ("a stack is an array with a pointer"). An ADT is defined by its behaviour; the array is one possible implementation.

### Check questions

1. Define the term data structure. [1 mark]
2. Explain why accessing element `a[i]` of an array takes the same time whatever the value of `i`. [2 marks]
3. A 2D array `seats` stores 20 rows of 30 seats. Each element is 1 byte; row-major order; base address 4000. Calculate the address of `seats[7][12]`. [2 marks]
4. State one difference between a list and a tuple in Python. [1 mark]
5. Explain two differences between a text file and a binary file. [4 marks]
6. Write code that reads a text file `temps.txt` (one temperature per line) and outputs the highest value. [4 marks]
7. Explain what is meant by an abstract data type, using a queue as an example. [2 marks]
8. Compare static and dynamic data structures, giving two advantages of each. [4 marks]
9. A 3D array `sales[shop][month][product]` holds sales for 4 shops, 12 months and 50 products. Write code to total all sales for shop 2. [3 marks]
10. Suggest and justify a data structure to hold, for one exam board, every student's surname, candidate number and whether they have special arrangements. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. A way of organising/storing related data so it can be accessed and used efficiently (1).
2. Elements are the same size and stored contiguously (1); so the address is calculated directly from the base address and index rather than searched for (1).
3. 4000 + (7 × 30 + 12) × 1 (1) = 4222 (1).
4. A tuple is immutable/cannot be changed after creation; a list can be changed (1).
5. Any two pairs: text stores data as characters, binary as raw bytes/machine representation (2); text is human-readable, binary is not (2); binary is usually more compact/fixed-size records allow direct access (2); text needs conversion of numbers when read, binary does not (2).
6. Example:

```python
highest = None
with open("temps.txt") as f:
    for line in f:
        t = float(line)
        if highest is None or t > highest:
            highest = t
print(highest)
```

Opens file for reading (1); loops through lines/until end of file (1); converts to number and compares with a running maximum (1); outputs after the loop (1).
7. Defined by its values and operations/behaviour, independent of implementation (1); e.g. a queue is defined by enqueue, dequeue, is-empty, is-full with FIFO behaviour, whether built from an array or a linked list (1).
8. Static: memory size known/fixed so no overflow of heap, simple; direct indexed access is fast (2). Dynamic: uses only the memory needed; can grow while running so no fixed limit other than memory (2). Accept relevant disadvantages contrasted.
9. Example:

```python
total = 0
for m in range(12):
    for p in range(50):
        total += sales[2][m][p]
```

Fixes shop index 2 (1); nested loops over months and products (1); accumulates total (1).
10. Array of records (1); each record holds fields of different types: surname string, candidate number (string or integer), special arrangements Boolean (1); array/list lets every student be processed in a loop/indexed (1). Accept a list of records or a table/dictionary keyed by candidate number with justification.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/05-stacks-queues-and-linked-lists.md -->

## Stacks, queues and linked lists

This file covers the stack and its operations, linear, circular and priority queues, and linked lists, each implemented with arrays and pointers, with traced examples. Stacks are used again for Reverse Polish ([08](08-graph-and-tree-traversal-and-reverse-polish.md)) and the call stack ([02](02-stack-frames-recursion-and-paradigms.md)).

New at A-level: there is no GCSE equivalent.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stacks | Push, pop, peek/top, test empty, test full | Create, traverse, add, remove | Represent with pointers and arrays |
| Queues | Linear, circular and priority: add, remove, test empty, test full | Queues (type not specified) | Represent with pointers and arrays |
| Linked lists | Not named (but used to build dynamic structures) | Named: create, traverse, add, remove | Named: represent with pointers and arrays |

AQA does not name linked lists, but AQA questions on dynamic structures and priority queues often assume them; they are worth knowing.

### 4.2.3.1 Stacks

*Refs: AQA 4.2.3.1 · OCR 1.4.2(b), 1.4.2(c), 2.3.1(e) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- A stack is a last in, first out (LIFO) structure: items are added and removed at the same end, the top.
- Operations:
  - push(item): add to the top. If the stack is full: stack overflow.
  - pop(): remove and return the top item. If the stack is empty: stack underflow.
  - peek() or top(): return the top item without removing it.
  - isEmpty(), isFull().
- Array implementation: an array plus a top pointer holding the index of the top item (−1 when empty).
- Uses: the call stack for subroutines, undo in editors, the back button in browsers, evaluating Reverse Polish expressions, depth-first search, backtracking, checking matched brackets.

#### Worked example

```python
class Stack:
    def __init__(self, size):
        self.items = [None] * size
        self.top = -1                  # empty
        self.size = size

    def is_empty(self):
        return self.top == -1

    def is_full(self):
        return self.top == self.size - 1

    def push(self, item):
        if self.is_full():
            raise OverflowError("stack full")
        self.top += 1
        self.items[self.top] = item

    def pop(self):
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("stack empty")
        item = self.items[self.top]
        self.top -= 1                  # item is not erased, just no longer in the stack
        return item

    def peek(self):
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("stack empty")
        return self.items[self.top]
```

Trace with size 3: push(4), push(9), pop(), push(2), push(7), push(1).

| Operation | items[0..2] | top | Returned / note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| start | – – – | −1 | |
| push(4) | 4 – – | 0 | |
| push(9) | 4 9 – | 1 | |
| pop() | 4 9 – | 0 | 9 (still in memory, outside the stack) |
| push(2) | 4 2 – | 1 | overwrites 9 |
| push(7) | 4 2 7 | 2 | full |
| push(1) | 4 2 7 | 2 | overflow error |

Using a stack to check brackets match:

```python
def balanced(text):
    pairs = {")": "(", "]": "[", "}": "{"}
    s = []
    for ch in text:
        if ch in "([{":
            s.append(ch)                  # push
        elif ch in ")]}":
            if not s or s.pop() != pairs[ch]:
                return False
    return not s                          # empty at the end means balanced

print(balanced("(a[b]{c})"), balanced("(]"))   # True False
```

#### Common mistakes

- Checking for full or empty after changing the pointer instead of before.
- Saying pop "deletes" the data from memory. It moves the pointer; the old value stays until overwritten.

### 4.2.2.1 Queues

*Refs: AQA 4.2.2.1 · OCR 1.4.2(b), 1.4.2(c), 2.3.1(e) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- A queue is a first in, first out (FIFO) structure: items join at the rear (tail) and leave from the front (head).
- Operations: enqueue (add an item at the rear), dequeue (remove the item at the front), isEmpty, isFull.
- Uses: print queues, keyboard buffers, processor scheduling, breadth-first search, simulations of real queues.

#### Linear queue

- Array plus front and rear pointers. Enqueue: increase rear, store item. Dequeue: take item at front, increase front.
- Problem: as items leave, the front moves along and the spaces at the start cannot be reused. The queue can report full when most of the array is empty. Fix: shuffle all items forward after each dequeue (slow, O(n)), or use a circular queue.

#### Circular queue

- The array is treated as a ring: when a pointer reaches the last index it wraps round to 0, using MOD.
- rear ← (rear + 1) MOD maxSize; front ← (front + 1) MOD maxSize.
- A size counter is the simplest way to test for full (size = maxSize) and empty (size = 0).
- Advantage: reuses freed spaces with no shuffling. Disadvantage: slightly more complex to code.

```python
class CircularQueue:
    def __init__(self, max_size):
        self.items = [None] * max_size
        self.max = max_size
        self.front = 0
        self.rear = -1
        self.size = 0

    def is_empty(self): return self.size == 0
    def is_full(self):  return self.size == self.max

    def enqueue(self, item):
        if self.is_full():
            raise OverflowError("queue full")
        self.rear = (self.rear + 1) % self.max
        self.items[self.rear] = item
        self.size += 1

    def dequeue(self):
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("queue empty")
        item = self.items[self.front]
        self.front = (self.front + 1) % self.max
        self.size -= 1
        return item
```

#### Worked example

A circular queue has 4 spaces. Trace: enqueue A, B, C; dequeue; dequeue; enqueue D, E, F.

| Operation | [0] [1] [2] [3] | front | rear | size |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| start | – – – – | 0 | −1 | 0 |
| enqueue A, B, C | A B C – | 0 | 2 | 3 |
| dequeue → A | A B C – | 1 | 2 | 2 |
| dequeue → B | A B C – | 2 | 2 | 1 |
| enqueue D | A B C D | 2 | 3 | 2 |
| enqueue E | E B C D | 2 | 0 (wrapped) | 3 |
| enqueue F | E F C D | 2 | 1 | 4 (full) |

Old values (A, B) stay in the array until overwritten; they are outside the queue. After the last step the array holds [E, F, C, D], front = 2, so the order of leaving is C, D, E, F. A linear queue of 4 would have reported full at the enqueue of E.

#### Priority queue

- Each item has a priority. Items with higher priority leave first; items with equal priority leave in FIFO order.
- Implementation choices:
  - insert in order: on enqueue, search from the rear for the right place and shift items (or link in, for a linked list). Dequeue is then just removing the front. Enqueue O(n), dequeue O(1);
  - or add at the rear and search for the highest priority when dequeuing;
  - or a heap (not required), with O(log n) for both.
- Uses: processor scheduling with priorities, A&E triage, Dijkstra's algorithm (file 10).

```python
def pq_enqueue(queue, item, priority):
    # queue is a list of (priority, item), highest priority at index 0
    i = len(queue)
    while i > 0 and queue[i - 1][0] < priority:   # strictly less keeps FIFO for ties
        i -= 1
    queue.insert(i, (priority, item))

q = []
for item, p in [("print", 1), ("alarm", 5), ("email", 1), ("update", 3)]:
    pq_enqueue(q, item, p)
print([x for _, x in q])   # ['alarm', 'update', 'print', 'email']
```

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting MOD so the circular pointer runs off the end of the array.
- Testing a circular queue for empty with `front == rear`: with these pointer conventions that is true when exactly one item is present. Use a size count, or define the pointers carefully.
- In a priority queue, placing an equal-priority item ahead of earlier ones, which breaks FIFO for ties.

### Linked lists

*Refs: OCR 1.4.2(b), 1.4.2(c), 2.3.1(e) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures, C1.3: Traversal of data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- A linked list is a dynamic structure of nodes. Each node holds data and a pointer to the next node. A start (head) pointer holds the first node's address; the last node's pointer is null.
- Items are logically in order but can be anywhere in memory.
- With an array implementation, there are two lists sharing one array: the data list and a free-space list of unused nodes, each with its own start pointer.
- Operations:
  - traverse: start at the head and follow pointers until null;
  - add: get a free node, store the data, then change pointers to link it in at the right place (no items move);
  - remove: change the previous node's pointer to skip the removed node, and return that node to the free list.
- Advantages: insertion and deletion do not shift data; size can change. Disadvantages: no direct access by index (must traverse from the head, O(n)), and extra memory for the pointers.
- Variants: doubly linked (pointers forward and back), circular (last points to first).

#### Worked example

Array-based linked list holding names in alphabetical order. `start = 2`, `free = 4`.

| Index | Data | Next |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Kim | 3 |
| 1 | Eve | 0 |
| 2 | Ben | 1 |
| 3 | Sam | −1 (null) |
| 4 | – | 5 |
| 5 | – | −1 |

Traversal from 2: Ben → 1 Eve → 0 Kim → 3 Sam → end.

Insert "Jo" in order:

1. Take the free node: new = 4; free ← next[4] = 5.
2. data[4] ← "Jo".
3. Traverse to find where it goes: after Eve (index 1) and before Kim (index 0).
4. next[4] ← next[1] = 0 (Jo points to Kim).
5. next[1] ← 4 (Eve points to Jo).

Order is now Ben → Eve → Jo → Kim → Sam, and no existing data moved.

Delete "Kim": find it (index 0) and its previous node (Jo, index 4). next[4] ← next[0] = 3 (Jo now points to Sam). Return node 0 to the free list: next[0] ← free (5); free ← 0.

```python
class Node:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.next = None

class LinkedList:
    def __init__(self):
        self.head = None

    def traverse(self):
        cur = self.head
        while cur is not None:
            print(cur.data)
            cur = cur.next

    def insert_in_order(self, data):
        new = Node(data)
        if self.head is None or data < self.head.data:   # new first item
            new.next = self.head
            self.head = new
            return
        prev = self.head
        while prev.next is not None and prev.next.data < data:
            prev = prev.next
        new.next = prev.next
        prev.next = new

    def delete(self, data):
        if self.head is None:
            return False
        if self.head.data == data:
            self.head = self.head.next
            return True
        prev = self.head
        while prev.next is not None and prev.next.data != data:
            prev = prev.next
        if prev.next is None:
            return False                  # not found
        prev.next = prev.next.next        # skip the node
        return True
```

#### Common mistakes

- Changing the previous node's pointer before saving where it used to point, which loses the rest of the list. Always set the new node's pointer first.
- Forgetting the special cases: empty list, inserting before the head, deleting the head, item not found.
- Saying a linked list allows binary search. Binary search needs direct access by index, which a linked list does not have.

### Stacks and queues built from linked lists

#### What you need to know

- A stack can be a linked list where push and pop both work at the head: O(1), and no fixed size.
- A queue can be a linked list with front and rear pointers: enqueue links a new node after the rear; dequeue removes the head. No wrap-around needed.

### Check questions

1. State what is meant by LIFO and give two uses of a stack. [3 marks]
2. A stack of size 5 is empty. Show its contents and the top pointer after: push(3), push(8), push(1), pop(), peek(), push(6). State the values returned. [4 marks]
3. Write a `pop` function for an array-based stack, including a check for underflow. [3 marks]
4. Explain the problem with a linear queue implemented as an array, and how a circular queue solves it. [3 marks]
5. A circular queue has 5 spaces, front = 3, rear = 1 and size = 4. (a) Which index will the next item be stored in? (b) Which index is the next item removed from? (c) Is the queue full? [3 marks]
6. Describe how an item is added to a priority queue so that equal priorities are still served first come, first served. [3 marks]
7. Using the linked-list table in this file (before the insertion of Jo), describe the pointer changes to delete "Eve". [3 marks]
8. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of a linked list compared with an array. [2 marks]
9. Write an algorithm to count the nodes in a linked list. [3 marks]
10. Explain why a queue is suitable for a printer's jobs but a stack is suitable for an undo feature. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Last in, first out: the last item added is the first removed (1). Two uses from: call stack/subroutine return addresses, undo, browser back, RPN evaluation, depth-first search, backtracking (2).
2. After push 3, 8, 1: [3, 8, 1], top = 2 (1). pop returns 1, top = 1 (1). peek returns 8, top still 1 (1). push(6): [3, 8, 6], top = 2 (1).
3. Check top = −1 (or isEmpty) and report error (1); store items[top] in a variable (1); decrement top and return the item (1).
4. Front pointer moves along as items are removed so spaces at the start cannot be reused (1); queue may appear full when it is not (1); circular queue wraps pointers round to the start (using MOD) so freed spaces are reused (1).
5. (a) (1 + 1) MOD 5 = 2 (1). (b) 3 (1). (c) No: size 4 < 5 (1).
6. Start at the rear and move towards the front past items of lower priority (1); stop at the first item with equal or higher priority (1); insert the new item behind it/shift or relink items (1).
7. Eve is at index 1, previous node is Ben at index 2 (1); next[2] ← next[1] = 0 so Ben points to Kim (1); node 1 added to the free list: next[1] ← free (4), free ← 1 (1).
8. Advantage: items inserted/deleted without moving others, or size can change (1). Disadvantage: no direct access/must traverse, or extra memory for pointers (1).
9. count ← 0; cur ← start (1); while cur ≠ null: count ← count + 1; cur ← next[cur] (1); return/output count (1).
10. Print jobs should be done in the order sent, FIFO (1); undo must reverse the most recent action first, LIFO (1).

## Graphs, trees, hash tables and dictionaries

This file covers graph terms and the adjacency matrix and adjacency list, trees, rooted and binary trees, building and changing a binary search tree, hash tables with hashing functions and collision handling, and dictionaries. Traversing graphs and trees is in [08-graph-and-tree-traversal-and-reverse-polish.md](08-graph-and-tree-traversal-and-reverse-polish.md); searching a binary tree in [09-searching-and-sorting.md](09-searching-and-sorting.md); shortest paths in [10-shortest-path-dijkstra-and-a-star.md](10-shortest-path-dijkstra-and-a-star.md).

New at A-level: there is no GCSE equivalent.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Graphs | Terms; adjacency matrix and list compared | Directed and undirected graphs | Not named in C1.1 (shortest paths and routes are named: files 10, 27) |
| Trees | Tree, rooted tree, binary tree; uses | Tree and binary search tree: create, traverse, add, remove | Trees with pointers and arrays |
| Hash tables | Hashing, collisions, rehashing | Hash table (add, remove, search) | Hash tables (C1.1); hashing algorithms compared (C2.4) |
| Dictionaries | Key-value pairs; information retrieval | Not named | Not named |

### 4.2.4.1 Graphs

*Refs: AQA 4.2.4.1 · OCR 1.4.2(b), 1.4.2(c).*

#### What you need to know

- A graph is a set of vertices (nodes) connected by edges (arcs). It represents complex relationships between items.
- Undirected graph: edges have no direction (a road you can drive both ways). Directed graph (digraph): each edge has a direction, drawn as an arrow (a one-way street, a link from one web page to another).
- Weighted graph: each edge has a value (weight, cost), such as distance, time or cost.
- Neighbours (adjacent vertices) share an edge. The degree of a vertex is the number of edges at it.
- Uses: road and rail networks and satellite navigation, computer networks and internet routing, social networks (people and friendships), web page links (search engine ranking), task dependencies and scheduling, states and moves in a game.

#### Adjacency matrix and adjacency list

- Adjacency matrix: a 2D array where entry [i][j] shows whether there is an edge from vertex i to vertex j (1 or 0), or its weight (with ∞ or blank for no edge). For an undirected graph it is symmetric about the leading diagonal.
- Adjacency list: for each vertex, a list of its neighbours (with weights for a weighted graph). Often a dictionary of lists.

#### Worked example

Undirected weighted graph: A–B 4, A–C 2, B–C 1, B–D 5, C–D 8.

Adjacency matrix (blank = no edge):

| | A | B | C | D |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A | | 4 | 2 | |
| B | 4 | | 1 | 5 |
| C | 2 | 1 | | 8 |
| D | | 5 | 8 | |

Adjacency list:

```python
graph = {
    "A": {"B": 4, "C": 2},
    "B": {"A": 4, "C": 1, "D": 5},
    "C": {"A": 2, "B": 1, "D": 8},
    "D": {"B": 5, "C": 8},
}
```

| | Adjacency matrix | Adjacency list |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Memory | n² entries whatever the number of edges | Proportional to vertices + edges |
| Best for | Dense graphs (many edges) | Sparse graphs (few edges) |
| Is there an edge from i to j? | One look-up, O(1) | Search i's list, up to O(degree) |
| List all neighbours of i | Scan a whole row, O(n) | Read i's list directly |
| Adding a vertex | Resize whole matrix | Add a new list |

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to record both directions for an undirected edge in an adjacency list.
- Choosing a matrix for a huge sparse graph like a social network: most entries would be empty and memory wasted.

### 4.2.5.1 Trees, rooted trees and binary trees

*Refs: AQA 4.2.5.1 · OCR 1.4.2(b), 1.4.2(c), 2.3.1(e) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- A tree is a connected, undirected graph with no cycles. A tree does not have to have a root.
- A rooted tree has one vertex chosen as the root. This gives parent–child relationships: the root has no parent, every other node has exactly one parent and is a descendant of the root. A leaf has no children. A subtree is a node and all its descendants.
- A binary tree is a rooted tree where each node has at most two children (left and right).
- A binary search tree (BST) is a binary tree in which, for every node, all values in its left subtree are smaller and all values in its right subtree are larger (or equal, by a stated rule).
- Uses of rooted trees: file system directories, family trees, organisation charts, syntax trees in compilers, expression trees, decision trees, BSTs for fast searching and sorting, Huffman coding trees.
- Array representation (Eduqas, OCR): three arrays, or an array of records, with fields left pointer, data, right pointer; −1 or 0 as null.

#### Worked example

Insert 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80, 35 into an empty BST.

```
            50
          /    \
        30      70
       /  \    /  \
     20    40 60   80
          /
        35
```

Each new value starts at the root and goes left if smaller, right if larger, until it finds an empty place. 35: 35 < 50 left; 35 > 30 right; 35 < 40 left; empty, so 35 is 40's left child.

Array (pointer) form, in order of insertion:

| Index | Left | Data | Right |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 1 | 50 | 2 |
| 1 | 3 | 30 | 4 |
| 2 | 5 | 70 | 6 |
| 3 | −1 | 20 | −1 |
| 4 | 7 | 40 | −1 |
| 5 | −1 | 60 | −1 |
| 6 | −1 | 80 | −1 |
| 7 | −1 | 35 | −1 |

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.left = None
        self.right = None

def insert(root, data):
    if root is None:
        return TreeNode(data)
    if data < root.data:
        root.left = insert(root.left, data)
    else:
        root.right = insert(root.right, data)
    return root
```

Removing from a BST (OCR):

- Leaf: set the parent's pointer to null.
- One child: link the parent straight to the child.
- Two children: replace the node's value with its in-order successor (the smallest value in its right subtree), then remove that successor node (which has at most one child).

Remove 30 from the tree above: it has two children. The smallest value in its right subtree (40, 35) is 35. Copy 35 into the 30 node, then remove the old 35 leaf. The tree's in-order listing is still sorted: 20, 35, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80.

A balanced BST of n nodes has height about log₂ n, so searching is O(log n). If values arrive already sorted, every node goes right and the tree becomes a "linked list" of height n, with O(n) search.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling any binary tree a BST. A BST also needs the ordering rule.
- Inserting by comparing only with the root and not walking down.
- Saying a tree must have a root. That is a rooted tree.

### 4.2.6.1 Hash tables

*Refs: AQA 4.2.6.1 · OCR 1.4.2(b), 1.4.2(c) · Eduqas C1.1: Data structures, C2.4: File organisation.*

#### What you need to know

- A hash table stores key-value pairs in an array. A hashing function turns the key into an array index (the hash), so a value can be stored and found in (close to) constant time, O(1), without searching.
- Simple hashing algorithms:
  - modulo (division): index = key MOD table size (a prime table size spreads keys better);
  - for text keys, add (or weight and add) the character codes, then MOD table size;
  - folding: split the key into parts, add the parts, then MOD;
  - mid-square: square the key and take the middle digits.
- A good hash function is quick to calculate, spreads keys evenly across the table, and always gives the same index for the same key.
- A collision happens when two different keys hash to the same index.
- Handling collisions:
  - Rehashing (open addressing): apply a further rule to find another slot. Linear probing tries the next slot (index + 1) MOD size, and so on, until a free one is found. Other rules step by a larger fixed amount or use a second hash function. When searching, follow the same rule until the key or an empty slot is found.
  - Chaining (closed addressing): each slot holds a linked list of all items with that hash.
- Clustering: with linear probing, collided items bunch together, making later collisions more likely and searches slower.
- Load factor = items stored ÷ table size. As it rises, collisions rise. When it passes a threshold (often around 0.7), make a larger table and rehash every item into it.
- Deleting with open addressing: mark the slot as "deleted" (a tombstone) rather than empty, or later searches would stop too early.
- Uses: database indexes, caches, symbol tables in compilers, password storage look-ups, implementing dictionaries and sets. Eduqas: hashing to find a record's address in a direct (random) access file (file 36).

#### Worked example

Table of size 7, hash(key) = key MOD 7, linear probing. Insert 15, 22, 8, 29, 3.

| Key | key MOD 7 | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 15 | 1 | slot 1 |
| 22 | 1 | collision; try 2: slot 2 |
| 8 | 1 | collision at 1 and 2; slot 3 |
| 29 | 1 | collision at 1, 2, 3; slot 4 |
| 3 | 3 | collision at 3 (8) and 4 (29); slot 5 |

Final table: [–, 15, 22, 8, 29, 3, –]. Searching for 3 takes three probes (slots 3, 4, 5); searching for 36 (36 MOD 7 = 1) probes 1 to 6 and stops at the empty slot 6: not found. A table size or hash that spreads these keys better would avoid this cluster.

String key with a character-sum hash, table size 11: "CAT" → 67 + 65 + 84 = 216; 216 MOD 11 = 7. "ACT" gives the same sum, so it collides: a weakness of simple summing (anagrams always collide). Weighting by position (for example sum of code × position) reduces this.

```python
SIZE = 7
table = [None] * SIZE

def insert(key, value):
    i = key % SIZE
    start = i
    while table[i] is not None and table[i][0] != key:
        i = (i + 1) % SIZE                    # linear probe (rehash)
        if i == start:
            raise OverflowError("table full")
    table[i] = (key, value)

def lookup(key):
    i = key % SIZE
    start = i
    while table[i] is not None:
        if table[i][0] == key:
            return table[i][1]
        i = (i + 1) % SIZE
        if i == start:
            break
    return None                               # reached an empty slot: not present
```

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a hash table gives O(1) "always". With many collisions it degrades towards O(n).
- Stopping a search at the first slot when it holds a different key. You must keep probing.
- Confusing hashing for a table with cryptographic hashing (file 20), though the idea of a one-way function from key to fixed value is shared.

### 4.2.7.1 Dictionaries

*Refs: AQA 4.2.7.1.*

#### What you need to know

- A dictionary (associative array, map) is a collection of key-value pairs where each value is accessed by its unique key, not by position.
- Operations: add a pair, look up a value by key, change a value, delete a pair, test whether a key exists.
- Dictionaries are usually implemented with hash tables, so look-ups are fast.
- Information retrieval example: represent a document by a dictionary of word counts, which lets you compare documents and find relevant ones.

#### Worked example

Build a word-count dictionary for the sentence "The dog saw the other dog", ignoring case and punctuation.

```python
text = "The dog saw the other dog"
counts = {}
for word in text.lower().split():
    counts[word] = counts.get(word, 0) + 1
print(counts)   # {'the': 2, 'dog': 2, 'saw': 1, 'other': 1}
```

Two documents can then be compared by the words they share and how often (see dot products in [07-vectors.md](07-vectors.md)).

#### Common mistakes

- Expecting duplicate keys. Assigning to an existing key replaces its value.
- Saying a dictionary is accessed by index. It is accessed by key.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between a directed and an undirected graph, giving one use of each. [4 marks]
2. Draw the adjacency matrix for the directed graph with edges P→Q, Q→R, R→P, P→R. [3 marks]
3. Explain when an adjacency list is a better choice than an adjacency matrix. [2 marks]
4. State the conditions for a graph to be a tree, and define a rooted tree. [3 marks]
5. Insert these values in order into an empty BST and draw it: 40, 25, 60, 10, 30, 55, 70, 28. [3 marks]
6. Describe how 25 would be removed from your tree in Q5. [3 marks]
7. Using hash = key MOD 11 and linear probing, insert 34, 45, 12, 56, 23 into an empty table of 11 slots. Show the final position of each key. [4 marks]
8. Explain what is meant by a collision and describe one method other than linear probing for dealing with it. [3 marks]
9. Explain why a hash table becomes slower as its load factor rises, and what can be done. [3 marks]
10. Write code that builds a dictionary counting how many times each letter occurs in a string, ignoring spaces. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Undirected: edges can be followed in both directions; directed: edges have a direction/one way (2). Uses, one each: undirected road network/friendship network; directed one-way streets/web links/task dependencies (2).
2. Rows = from, columns = to (accept the transpose if labelled). P row: Q 1, R 1; Q row: R 1; R row: P 1; all others 0 (1 mark per correct row, max 3).
3. When the graph is sparse/has few edges compared with vertices (1); list uses memory only for edges that exist, matrix uses n² (1).
4. Connected (1); undirected with no cycles (1). Rooted tree: one vertex designated as root, giving parent–child relationships (1).
5. 40 root; left 25 (children 10 and 30); right 60 (children 55 and 70); 28 is the left child of 30 (1 for root and level 2, 1 for level 3, 1 for 28 correctly placed).
6. 25 has two children (1); replace it with its in-order successor, the smallest value in its right subtree, 28 (1); remove the original 28 node (a leaf) so 30 has no left child (1). Accept in-order predecessor 10 with the matching steps.
7. 34 → 1; 45 → 1, collides, → 2; 12 → 1, → 3; 56 → 1, → 4; 23 → 1, → 5 (all hash to 1). 1 mark for showing all hash to 1; 3 marks for positions 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 (−1 per error).
8. Two different keys produce the same hash/index (1). Chaining: each slot holds a linked list; colliding items are added to the list at that slot (2). Accept a second hash function/rehash with a different step described.
9. More items means more collisions, so more probes or longer chains for each search (1); performance tends towards O(n) (1); create a larger table and rehash all items into it (1).
10. Example:

```python
counts = {}
for ch in s:
    if ch != " ":
        counts[ch] = counts.get(ch, 0) + 1
```

Loop through characters skipping spaces (1); add new key with count 1 (1); increment existing key (1).

## Vectors

This file covers what a vector is, its notations (list, function, dictionary, 1D array, arrow), vector addition and scalar multiplication, convex combinations, the dot product and using it to find the angle between two vectors. Only AQA examines this.

#### Board differences

Only AQA 7517 has this content. OCR H446 and Eduqas A500QS do not examine vectors.

### 4.2.8.1 Vectors

*Refs: AQA 4.2.8.1.*

#### What you need to know

- A vector is an ordered list of numbers, all taken from the same field (set of numbers), usually the real numbers ℝ. A vector with n entries over ℝ is an n-vector, and the set of all of them is written ℝⁿ. Example: [2.0, 3.14159, −1.0, 2.718281828] is a 4-vector over ℝ, a member of ℝ⁴.
- Representations:
  - list: `[2.0, 3.0]`;
  - function: a vector maps each index to a value, f : S → ℝ where S = {0, 1, 2, 3}. Written 0 ↦ 2.0, 1 ↦ 3.14159 and so on (↦ means "maps to");
  - dictionary: the function view stored directly, `{0: 2.0, 1: 3.14159, 2: -1.0, 3: 2.718281828}`; handy for sparse vectors where most entries are 0;
  - 1D array: a fixed-length array of one numeric type, for example `Dim example(3) As Single` in VB.NET;
  - arrow: a 2-vector [2.0, 3.0] can be drawn as an arrow from the origin to the point (2.0, 3.0). The arrow has a length (magnitude) and a direction.
- Vector addition adds matching entries: [a₁, a₂] + [b₁, b₂] = [a₁ + b₁, a₂ + b₂]. Geometrically, it translates (moves) a point.
- Scalar–vector multiplication multiplies every entry by a number: k[a₁, a₂] = [ka₁, ka₂]. Geometrically, it scales the arrow (k > 1 longer, 0 < k < 1 shorter, negative k reverses).
- Convex combination of u and v: αu + βv with α, β ≥ 0 and α + β = 1. Every convex combination lies on the straight line segment between the tips of u and v. With α = β = 0.5 you get the midpoint.
- Dot (scalar) product: u · v = u₁v₁ + u₂v₂ + … + uₙvₙ. The result is a single number, not a vector.
- Angle between two vectors: u · v = |u| |v| cos θ, so cos θ = (u · v) ÷ (|u| |v|), where |u| = √(u₁² + … + uₙ²). If u · v = 0, the vectors are at right angles (perpendicular).

#### Explained

Vectors appear across computing:

- Graphics: positions, movements and scaling of objects in 2D and 3D (vector graphics in file 19).
- Games: a character's velocity is a vector; add it to the position vector each frame to move.
- Machine learning and information retrieval: a document becomes a vector of word counts (one entry per word in the vocabulary). Two documents point in similar directions if they use similar words; the cosine of the angle between them (cosine similarity) measures how alike they are.
- Parity: AQA also links the dot product over the field {0, 1} with arithmetic mod 2 to parity checking: the dot product of a bit pattern with [1, 1, …, 1] mod 2 is the parity bit for even parity.

#### Worked example

u = [3, 4], v = [1, −2].

1. u + v = [3 + 1, 4 + (−2)] = [4, 2].
2. 2u = [6, 8].
3. Convex combination 0.25u + 0.75v = [0.75 + 0.75, 1 + (−1.5)] = [1.5, −0.5]. Check: 0.25 + 0.75 = 1 and both ≥ 0, so this point lies on the segment between (3, 4) and (1, −2), three-quarters of the way towards v.
4. u · v = 3 × 1 + 4 × (−2) = 3 − 8 = −5.
5. |u| = √(9 + 16) = 5; |v| = √(1 + 4) = √5 ≈ 2.236.
6. cos θ = −5 ÷ (5 × 2.236) = −0.4472, so θ ≈ 116.6°.

Document similarity. Vocabulary [cat, dog, fish]. Document A = "cat cat dog" → [2, 1, 0]. Document B = "cat dog dog" → [1, 2, 0]. Document C = "fish" → [0, 0, 1].

- A · B = 2 + 2 + 0 = 4; |A| = |B| = √5; cos θ = 4 ÷ 5 = 0.8 (similar).
- A · C = 0, so cos θ = 0: no words in common (perpendicular).

```python
import math

def add(u, v):      return [a + b for a, b in zip(u, v)]
def scale(k, u):    return [k * a for a in u]
def dot(u, v):      return sum(a * b for a, b in zip(u, v))
def length(u):      return math.sqrt(dot(u, u))

def angle_deg(u, v):
    return math.degrees(math.acos(dot(u, v) / (length(u) * length(v))))

def convex(alpha, u, v):
    assert 0 <= alpha <= 1
    return add(scale(alpha, u), scale(1 - alpha, v))

u, v = [3, 4], [1, -2]
print(add(u, v), scale(2, u), dot(u, v), round(angle_deg(u, v), 1))
## [4, 2] [6, 8] -5 116.6

sparse = {0: 2.0, 3: 2.718281828}     # dictionary form: missing indices are 0
```

#### Common mistakes

- Giving the dot product as a vector. It is a single number (a scalar).
- Forgetting the conditions on a convex combination: both coefficients must be non-negative and must add to 1.
- Adding vectors of different lengths. Both must have the same number of entries (be in the same ℝⁿ).
- Mixing degrees and radians when using inverse cosine in code.

### Check questions

1. Write the 3-vector [5.0, −1.5, 0.0] over ℝ in function (↦) notation and as a Python dictionary. [2 marks]
2. State what is meant by "a 4-vector over ℝ". [1 mark]
3. Given a = [2, −1, 3] and b = [0, 4, 1], calculate a + b and 3a. [2 marks]
4. Calculate a · b for the vectors in Q3. [1 mark]
5. Find the angle between p = [1, 0] and q = [1, 1]. [3 marks]
6. Show that [6, 3] is not a convex combination of [2, 1] and [4, 2]. [2 marks]
7. Calculate the convex combination 0.5u + 0.5v where u = [−2, 8] and v = [6, 0], and describe what it represents geometrically. [2 marks]
8. Explain what vector addition and scalar–vector multiplication do geometrically. [2 marks]
9. Write a function `dot(u, v)` that returns the dot product of two lists of equal length. [3 marks]
10. Two documents have word-count vectors [1, 0, 2] and [2, 1, 0]. Use the dot product to calculate the cosine of the angle between them and comment on their similarity. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 0 ↦ 5.0, 1 ↦ −1.5, 2 ↦ 0.0 (1); {0: 5.0, 1: -1.5, 2: 0.0} (1).
2. An ordered list of four values, each a real number (a member of ℝ⁴) (1).
3. a + b = [2, 3, 4] (1); 3a = [6, −3, 9] (1).
4. 0 − 4 + 3 = −1 (1).
5. p · q = 1 (1); |p| = 1, |q| = √2 so cos θ = 1/√2 (1); θ = 45° (1).
6. Convex combinations lie on the segment between (2, 1) and (4, 2) / need α + β = 1 with α, β ≥ 0 (1); [6, 3] would need e.g. α = 0, β = 1.5 or α = 3, β = 0, so α + β ≠ 1 / the point lies beyond (4, 2) (1).
7. [2, 4] (1); the midpoint of the line between the two points/tips (1).
8. Addition translates/moves a point or arrow by the second vector (1); scalar multiplication scales/changes the length of the arrow (reversing it if negative) (1).
9. Example: `def dot(u, v):` total ← 0 (1); loop over indices adding u[i] × v[i] (1); return total (1).
10. Dot product = 2 + 0 + 0 = 2 (1); lengths √5 and √5 so cos θ = 2/5 = 0.4 (1); some similarity but not very similar (θ ≈ 66°) (1).

## Graph traversal, tree traversal and Reverse Polish

This file covers breadth-first and depth-first search of a graph, pre-order, in-order and post-order traversal of a tree, and converting between infix and Reverse Polish notation and evaluating it with a stack. The structures themselves are in [05](05-stacks-queues-and-linked-lists.md) and [06](06-graphs-trees-hash-tables-and-dictionaries.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Graph traversal | Trace BFS and DFS; uses | Depth-first and breadth-first traversal | "Traversal of data structures" (algorithms to traverse) |
| Tree traversal | Pre-, in-, post-order; uses | Depth-first (post-order) and breadth-first traversal of trees | As above |
| Reverse Polish | Infix ↔ RPN; why and where used | Not named | Not named |

OCR describes depth-first traversal of a tree as post-order. Learn all three orders anyway, as OCR questions may ask you to trace one given in pseudo-code.

### 4.3.1.1 Simple graph-traversal algorithms

*Refs: AQA 4.3.1.1 · OCR 2.3.1(e) · Eduqas C1.3: Traversal of data structures.*

#### What you need to know

- Traversal means visiting every vertex reachable from a start vertex exactly once. Both methods keep track of visited vertices so they do not loop forever round a cycle.
- Breadth-first search (BFS): visit the start vertex, then all its neighbours, then all their unvisited neighbours, and so on, level by level. Uses a queue.
- Depth-first search (DFS): go as far as possible along one path, then backtrack to the last vertex with an unvisited neighbour and continue. Uses a stack (explicitly, or the call stack through recursion).
- Applications:
  - BFS finds the shortest path (fewest edges) in an unweighted graph; also "friends of friends" within n steps, web crawlers by distance, network broadcasting.
  - DFS: navigating or solving a maze, detecting cycles, topological sorting of tasks, finding whether a path exists, backtracking puzzles.
- Both are O(V + E) with an adjacency list.

#### Worked example

Graph (adjacency list, neighbours taken in alphabetical order):

```
A: B, C
B: A, D, E
C: A, F
D: B
E: B, F
F: C, E
```

BFS from A:

| Step | Dequeued (visit) | Queue after adding unvisited neighbours | Visited |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | A | B, C | A, B, C |
| 2 | B | C, D, E | + D, E |
| 3 | C | D, E, F | + F |
| 4 | D | E, F | |
| 5 | E | F | (B, F already discovered) |
| 6 | F | empty | |

Order: A, B, C, D, E, F. Here vertices are marked as discovered when added to the queue, so none is queued twice.

DFS from A (recursive):

visit A → first unvisited neighbour B → visit B → its first unvisited neighbour D → visit D → D has no unvisited neighbours, backtrack to B → E → visit E → F → visit F → F's neighbours C (unvisited) → visit C → backtrack all the way. Order: A, B, D, E, F, C.

```python
from collections import deque

def bfs(graph, start):
    visited = [start]
    queue = deque([start])
    order = []
    while queue:
        v = queue.popleft()
        order.append(v)
        for n in graph[v]:
            if n not in visited:
                visited.append(n)
                queue.append(n)
    return order

def dfs(graph, v, visited=None):
    if visited is None:
        visited = []
    visited.append(v)
    for n in graph[v]:
        if n not in visited:
            dfs(graph, n, visited)
    return visited
```

Shortest path in an unweighted graph: in BFS, record each vertex's predecessor (the vertex it was discovered from). To get the path to F, follow predecessors back: F ← C ← A, so A–C–F, two edges.

#### Common mistakes

- Using a stack for BFS or a queue for DFS.
- Visiting a vertex twice because it was not marked as visited.
- Claiming BFS finds the shortest path in a weighted graph. That needs Dijkstra's algorithm (file 10).

### 4.3.2.1 Simple tree-traversal algorithms

*Refs: AQA 4.3.2.1 · OCR 2.3.1(e), 1.4.2(c) · Eduqas C1.3: Traversal of data structures.*

#### What you need to know

All three are depth-first and are most easily written recursively. The name says when the node itself is visited relative to its subtrees:

- Pre-order: node, then left subtree, then right subtree. Use: copying a tree (each parent is created before its children); producing prefix (Polish) notation from an expression tree.
- In-order: left subtree, node, right subtree. Use: outputting the contents of a binary search tree in ascending order; producing infix from an expression tree.
- Post-order: left subtree, right subtree, node. Use: producing Reverse Polish (postfix) from an expression tree; deleting (emptying) a tree, since children are removed before their parent; evaluating expression trees.
- Breadth-first traversal of a tree visits level by level using a queue (OCR).

Quick method for tracing on paper: draw a line round the tree anticlockwise starting at the left of the root. Pre-order: list a node when you pass its left side. In-order: when you pass underneath. Post-order: when you pass its right side.

#### Worked example

```
          F
        /   \
       B     G
      / \     \
     A   D     I
        / \   /
       C   E H
```

- Pre-order: F, B, A, D, C, E, G, I, H
- In-order: A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I (ascending, as this is a BST)
- Post-order: A, C, E, D, B, H, I, G, F
- Breadth-first: F, B, G, A, D, I, C, E, H

```python
def preorder(node):
    if node:
        print(node.data)
        preorder(node.left)
        preorder(node.right)

def inorder(node):
    if node:
        inorder(node.left)
        print(node.data)
        inorder(node.right)

def postorder(node):
    if node:
        postorder(node.left)
        postorder(node.right)
        print(node.data)
```

#### Common mistakes

- Visiting the right subtree before the left.
- In-order on a tree that is not a BST does not give sorted output.

### 4.3.3.1 Reverse Polish – infix transformations

*Refs: AQA 4.3.3.1.*

#### What you need to know

- Infix: the operator goes between its operands, as normally written: 3 + 4. Needs brackets and precedence rules.
- Reverse Polish notation (RPN, postfix): the operator follows its operands: 3 4 +.
- Advantages of RPN: no brackets needed; no precedence rules needed (order is fixed by position); evaluated left to right in one pass with a single stack, which is simple and fast for a machine.
- Where it is used: stack-based interpreters and virtual machines such as PostScript (printer language) and bytecode (Java Virtual Machine, Python bytecode); some calculators; compilers convert infix to RPN as an intermediate step.
- Evaluating RPN with a stack: read left to right. Operand: push. Operator: pop two values (the first popped is the right operand), apply the operator, push the result. At the end, the single value on the stack is the answer.
- Infix to RPN by hand: fully bracket by precedence, then move each operator to just after its closing bracket and remove brackets. Or draw the expression tree and traverse post-order. (The shunting-yard algorithm does this with a stack.)

#### Worked example

Convert (5 + 3) × (8 − 2) ÷ 4 to RPN.

1. Fully bracket by precedence (× and ÷ left to right): (((5 + 3) × (8 − 2)) ÷ 4).
2. Move each operator after its operands: 5 3 + gives the first bracket, 8 2 − the second, then ×, then 4 ÷.
3. RPN: 5 3 + 8 2 − × 4 ÷

Evaluate with a stack:

| Token | Action | Stack (top at right) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | push | 5 |
| 3 | push | 5 3 |
| + | pop 3, pop 5, push 8 | 8 |
| 8 | push | 8 8 |
| 2 | push | 8 8 2 |
| − | pop 2, pop 8, push 6 | 8 6 |
| × | pop 6, pop 8, push 48 | 48 |
| 4 | push | 48 4 |
| ÷ | pop 4, pop 48, push 12 | 12 |

Answer 12.

Convert RPN to infix: 7 2 3 × − 1 +. Use a stack of expressions: push 7, 2, 3; × combines 2 and 3 into (2 × 3); − combines 7 and that into (7 − 2 × 3); push 1; + gives 7 − 2 × 3 + 1 = 2.

```python
def eval_rpn(tokens):
    stack = []
    for t in tokens:
        if t in "+-*/":
            right = stack.pop()       # first popped is the RIGHT operand
            left = stack.pop()
            if t == "+": stack.append(left + right)
            elif t == "-": stack.append(left - right)
            elif t == "*": stack.append(left * right)
            else: stack.append(left / right)
        else:
            stack.append(float(t))
    return stack.pop()

print(eval_rpn("5 3 + 8 2 - * 4 /".split()))   # 12.0
```

#### Common mistakes

- Popping operands in the wrong order for − and ÷: 8 2 − is 8 − 2, not 2 − 8.
- Changing the order of the operands when converting. In RPN the operands stay in the same left-to-right order as in infix; only the operators move.
- Saying RPN "needs brackets for complex expressions". It never needs brackets.

### Check questions

1. State which data structure is used by (a) breadth-first search and (b) depth-first search. [2 marks]
2. Using the graph below, give the order vertices are visited by BFS and by DFS from P, taking neighbours alphabetically. [4 marks]

```
P: Q, R
Q: P, S
R: P, S, T
S: Q, R, U
T: R
U: S
```

3. Give one application of BFS and one of DFS. [2 marks]
4. For the tree with root 8, left child 3 (children 1 and 6), right child 10 (right child 14, which has left child 13), give the pre-order, in-order and post-order traversals. [3 marks]
5. State a use of post-order traversal. [1 mark]
6. Convert to RPN: (a) a × (b + c), (b) (a − b) ÷ (c + d × e). [2 marks]
7. Convert the RPN 4 6 2 − × 3 + to infix and evaluate it. [2 marks]
8. Show the stack after each token when evaluating 9 3 ÷ 2 4 × +. [3 marks]
9. Explain two advantages of RPN over infix. [2 marks]
10. Write a recursive in-order traversal procedure in pseudo-code or Python. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) Queue (1); (b) stack (accept recursion/call stack) (1).
2. BFS: P, Q, R, S, T, U (2). DFS: P, Q, S, R, T, U (2). (One mark each if one error.)
3. BFS: shortest path in an unweighted graph/social network degrees/web crawling (1). DFS: maze solving/cycle detection/checking a path exists/topological sort (1).
4. Pre-order: 8, 3, 1, 6, 10, 14, 13 (1). In-order: 1, 3, 6, 8, 10, 13, 14 (1). Post-order: 1, 6, 3, 13, 14, 10, 8 (1).
5. Any one: producing RPN from an expression tree; deleting/emptying a tree; evaluating an expression tree (1).
6. (a) a b c + × (1). (b) a b − c d e × + ÷ (1).
7. 4 × (6 − 2) + 3 (1); = 19 (1).
8. 9; 9 3; 3 (after ÷) (1); 3 2; 3 2 4; 3 8 (after ×) (1); 11 (after +) (1).
9. Any two: no brackets needed (1); no precedence rules needed (1); can be evaluated left to right with a single stack, simple/efficient for machines (1).
10. Base case: if node is null return (1); recursive call on left subtree, then output node's data (1); then recursive call on right subtree (1).

## Searching and sorting algorithms

This file covers linear search, binary search and binary tree search, and bubble sort, insertion sort, merge sort and quick sort: how each works, traces, code, time and space complexity, and when to use each. Big-O itself is explained in [14-big-o-and-limits-of-computation.md](14-big-o-and-limits-of-computation.md).

Builds on [GCSE: Searching and sorting algorithms](../../gcse/computer-science/02-searching-and-sorting-algorithms.md), which covers linear and binary search, bubble, merge and insertion sort without complexity analysis. Here you also analyse them, write them, and learn quick sort.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Searches | Linear O(n), binary O(log n), binary tree search O(log n) | Linear and binary search | Linear and binary search; when to use each; Big-O for time and space |
| Sorts | Bubble O(n²), merge O(n log n) | Bubble, insertion, merge, quick | Bubble, insertion, quicksort; need for recursive and non-recursive sorts |
| Extra | Merge sort as divide and conquer | Compare suitability for a task and data set | Effect of storage space, comparisons, exchanges and passes on efficiency |

AQA does not require insertion or quick sort, but they make good comparison material.

### 4.3.4.1 Linear search

*Refs: AQA 4.3.4.1 · OCR 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Searching.*

#### What you need to know

- Check each item in turn from the start until the target is found or the list ends.
- Works on unsorted data and on structures without direct access (linked lists).
- Time: best case O(1) (first item); worst and average O(n). Space: O(1) extra.

```python
def linear_search(items, target):
    for i in range(len(items)):
        if items[i] == target:
            return i
    return -1
```

### 4.3.4.2 Binary search

*Refs: AQA 4.3.4.2 · OCR 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Searching.*

#### What you need to know

- The data must be sorted and allow direct access by index (an array).
- Compare the target with the middle item. If equal, done. If the target is smaller, search the left half; if larger, the right half. Repeat until found or the search range is empty.
- Each comparison halves the range, so at most about log₂ n + 1 comparisons: O(log n). A million items need at most 20 comparisons.
- Iterative space O(1); recursive space O(log n) for the call stack.

#### Worked example

Find 47 in [3, 9, 14, 21, 30, 38, 47, 52, 66, 71] (indices 0 to 9), with mid = (low + high) DIV 2.

| low | high | mid | items[mid] | Action |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 9 | 4 | 30 | 47 > 30 → low = 5 |
| 5 | 9 | 7 | 52 | 47 < 52 → high = 6 |
| 5 | 6 | 5 | 38 | 47 > 38 → low = 6 |
| 6 | 6 | 6 | 47 | found at index 6 |

Searching for 40 would continue to low = 6, high = 5: low > high, so not found.

```python
def binary_search(items, target):
    low, high = 0, len(items) - 1
    while low <= high:
        mid = (low + high) // 2
        if items[mid] == target:
            return mid
        elif target < items[mid]:
            high = mid - 1
        else:
            low = mid + 1
    return -1

def binary_search_rec(items, target, low, high):
    if low > high:
        return -1                     # base case: not found
    mid = (low + high) // 2
    if items[mid] == target:
        return mid
    if target < items[mid]:
        return binary_search_rec(items, target, low, mid - 1)
    return binary_search_rec(items, target, mid + 1, high)
```

#### Common mistakes

- Using binary search on unsorted data.
- Setting `high = mid` rather than `mid − 1`, which can loop forever.
- Forgetting the cost of sorting first: for a single search of unsorted data, linear search is quicker overall.

### 4.3.4.3 Binary tree search

*Refs: AQA 4.3.4.3 · OCR 2.3.1(e).*

#### What you need to know

- Search a binary search tree: start at the root; if the target equals the node, found; if smaller, go to the left child; if larger, go right; if you reach a null pointer, it is not there.
- Balanced tree: O(log n), because each step drops one level and the height is about log₂ n. Unbalanced (degenerate) tree, such as one built from sorted data: O(n).
- Advantage over binary search on an array: insertions and deletions do not shift items.

```python
def tree_search(node, target):
    while node is not None:
        if target == node.data:
            return True
        node = node.left if target < node.data else node.right
    return False
```

Using the tree in file 06 (50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80, 35), finding 35 visits 50 → 30 → 40 → 35: four comparisons.

### 4.3.5.1 Bubble sort

*Refs: AQA 4.3.5.1 · OCR 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Sorting.*

#### What you need to know

- Compare each pair of adjacent items and swap them if they are in the wrong order. One pass moves the largest unsorted item to its final place at the end. Repeat passes until a pass makes no swaps.
- Improvements: each pass can stop one item earlier; stop as soon as a pass makes no swaps.
- Time: worst and average O(n²) (about n²/2 comparisons); best O(n) on already-sorted data with the no-swap flag. Space O(1): it sorts in place.
- AQA uses it as the example of a time-inefficient sort. Its advantages are simplicity and low memory.

#### Worked example

Sort [5, 1, 4, 2, 8].

| Pass | Comparisons and swaps | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 5>1 swap; 5>4 swap; 5>2 swap; 5<8 no | [1, 4, 2, 5, 8] |
| 2 | 1<4; 4>2 swap; 4<5 | [1, 2, 4, 5, 8] |
| 3 | 1<2; 2<4; no swaps | stop: sorted |

```python
def bubble_sort(a):
    n = len(a)
    swapped = True
    while swapped:
        swapped = False
        for i in range(n - 1):
            if a[i] > a[i + 1]:
                a[i], a[i + 1] = a[i + 1], a[i]
                swapped = True
        n -= 1                      # last item now in place
    return a
```

### Insertion sort

*Refs: OCR 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Sorting.*

#### What you need to know

- Build a sorted part at the start of the list. Take the next unsorted item and move it left past every larger item in the sorted part, then insert it.
- Time: worst and average O(n²); best O(n) for nearly sorted data, where it is very fast. Space O(1), in place. Stable (equal items keep their order).
- Good for small or nearly sorted lists and for data arriving one item at a time.

#### Worked example

Sort [6, 3, 8, 1].

| Step | Item | Sorted part after insertion |
| --- | --- | --- |
| start | | [6] |
| 1 | 3 | 3 < 6, shift 6 right: [3, 6] |
| 2 | 8 | 8 > 6, stays: [3, 6, 8] |
| 3 | 1 | shift 8, 6, 3 right: [1, 3, 6, 8] |

```python
def insertion_sort(a):
    for i in range(1, len(a)):
        current = a[i]
        j = i - 1
        while j >= 0 and a[j] > current:
            a[j + 1] = a[j]           # shift larger item right
            j -= 1
        a[j + 1] = current
    return a
```

### 4.3.5.2 Merge sort

*Refs: AQA 4.3.5.2 · OCR 2.3.1(f).*

#### What you need to know

- A divide and conquer algorithm: split the list in half repeatedly until each sub-list has one item (a one-item list is sorted), then merge pairs of sorted sub-lists into larger sorted lists until one list remains.
- Merging two sorted lists: compare the front items, move the smaller to the output, repeat; then copy what remains.
- Time: O(n log n) in every case. There are about log₂ n levels of splitting, and each level does O(n) work merging.
- Space: O(n) extra for the merged lists (not in place). Stable.
- Faster than bubble or insertion sort for large lists; slower setup and more memory, so not ideal for tiny lists.

#### Worked example

Sort [38, 27, 43, 3, 9, 82, 10, 5].

```
Split:  [38 27 43 3] [9 82 10 5]
        [38 27] [43 3] [9 82] [10 5]
        [38] [27] [43] [3] [9] [82] [10] [5]
Merge:  [27 38] [3 43] [9 82] [5 10]
        [3 27 38 43]   [5 9 10 82]
        [3 5 9 10 27 38 43 82]
```

Final merge in detail: 3 vs 5 → 3; 27 vs 5 → 5; 27 vs 9 → 9; 27 vs 10 → 10; 27 vs 82 → 27; 38 vs 82 → 38; 43 vs 82 → 43; the left list is now empty, so copy 82.

```python
def merge_sort(a):
    if len(a) <= 1:
        return a
    mid = len(a) // 2
    left = merge_sort(a[:mid])
    right = merge_sort(a[mid:])
    return merge(left, right)

def merge(left, right):
    out = []
    i = j = 0
    while i < len(left) and j < len(right):
        if left[i] <= right[j]:       # <= keeps it stable
            out.append(left[i]); i += 1
        else:
            out.append(right[j]); j += 1
    out.extend(left[i:])
    out.extend(right[j:])
    return out
```

### Quick sort

*Refs: OCR 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Sorting.*

#### What you need to know

- Divide and conquer. Choose a pivot (often the first or middle item). Partition: rearrange so items smaller than the pivot are on its left and larger on its right; the pivot is now in its final place. Recursively quick sort the left and right parts.
- Time: average O(n log n); worst O(n²) when the pivot is always the smallest or largest item (for example first-item pivot on already sorted data). Good pivot choices (middle, median of three, random) make the worst case rare.
- Space: in place apart from the recursion stack, O(log n) on average. Not stable. Usually the fastest general-purpose sort in practice.

#### Worked example

Sort [7, 2, 9, 4, 1, 8] with the first item as pivot (simple list-partition version).

1. Pivot 7: smaller [2, 4, 1], larger [9, 8] → [2, 4, 1] + [7] + [9, 8].
2. [2, 4, 1], pivot 2: smaller [1], larger [4] → [1, 2, 4].
3. [9, 8], pivot 9: smaller [8] → [8, 9].
4. Result [1, 2, 4, 7, 8, 9].

```python
def quick_sort(a):
    if len(a) <= 1:
        return a
    pivot = a[0]
    smaller = [x for x in a[1:] if x < pivot]
    larger = [x for x in a[1:] if x >= pivot]
    return quick_sort(smaller) + [pivot] + quick_sort(larger)

## in-place version with two pointers (Hoare-style), as often shown in exams
def quick_sort_inplace(a, low=0, high=None):
    if high is None:
        high = len(a) - 1
    if low >= high:
        return
    pivot = a[(low + high) // 2]
    i, j = low, high
    while i <= j:
        while a[i] < pivot: i += 1
        while a[j] > pivot: j -= 1
        if i <= j:
            a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]
            i += 1; j -= 1
    quick_sort_inplace(a, low, j)
    quick_sort_inplace(a, i, high)
```

### Comparing algorithms

*Refs: OCR 2.3.1(a), 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Sorting, Searching.*

| Algorithm | Best | Average | Worst | Extra space | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Linear search | O(1) | O(n) | O(n) | O(1) | Any data |
| Binary search | O(1) | O(log n) | O(log n) | O(1) iterative | Sorted, indexed data |
| Binary tree search | O(1) | O(log n) | O(n) | O(1) | Degenerate tree is the worst |
| Bubble sort | O(n) | O(n²) | O(n²) | O(1) | Simple; many swaps |
| Insertion sort | O(n) | O(n²) | O(n²) | O(1) | Fast for nearly sorted data |
| Merge sort | O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n) | Predictable; stable; good for huge data and linked lists |
| Quick sort | O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n²) | O(log n) stack | Usually fastest in practice |

Eduqas asks you to explain efficiency in terms of:

- storage space (in-place sorts need almost none; merge sort needs a second copy);
- number of comparisons (dominant cost for most sorts);
- number of exchanges (swaps): bubble sort may swap many times per pass; insertion sort shifts instead; selection-style sorts swap once per pass;
- number of passes through the data (bubble sort may need n − 1; the early-exit flag can cut this to 1 for sorted data).

Why have several sorts (Eduqas)? No sort is best for every case: data size, how sorted the data already is, memory available, whether stability matters, and whether recursion is acceptable (recursive sorts use stack space and can overflow on huge inputs) all change the best choice.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying merge sort is O(n²) in the worst case. It is always O(n log n); it is quick sort that degrades.
- Saying quick sort needs no memory at all. Recursion uses stack space.
- Forgetting that binary search needs sorted data.

### Check questions

1. State two conditions needed for binary search to be used. [2 marks]
2. Trace a binary search for 31 in [2, 5, 8, 12, 15, 19, 23, 31, 40], showing low, high and mid each time. [4 marks]
3. Explain why binary search is O(log n). [2 marks]
4. Show the list after each pass of bubble sort on [9, 4, 7, 1, 3]. [3 marks]
5. Explain why bubble sort is O(n²) in the worst case, and when its best case is O(n). [3 marks]
6. Show each step of insertion sort on [5, 2, 9, 1, 6]. [3 marks]
7. Show how merge sort splits and merges [8, 3, 5, 1, 7, 2]. [4 marks]
8. Quick sort [6, 3, 9, 1, 7, 2, 8] using the first item as the pivot each time, showing each partition. [3 marks]
9. Explain why quick sort is O(n²) on already sorted data when the first item is the pivot. [2 marks]
10. A school sorts 2 million exam scripts by candidate number each year, and the data arrives in random order. Recommend a sort and justify your choice. [3 marks]
11. Write a recursive binary search in Python. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Data must be sorted (1); must allow direct/indexed access (e.g. an array) (1).
2. low 0, high 8, mid 4: 15 < 31 so low = 5 (1); low 5, high 8, mid 6: 23 < 31 so low = 7 (1); low 7, high 8, mid 7: 31 found (1); at index 7 (1). Accept mid rounded up if used consistently.
3. Each comparison halves the remaining items (1); so at most about log₂ n comparisons are needed (1).
4. Pass 1: [4, 7, 1, 3, 9] (1). Pass 2: [4, 1, 3, 7, 9] (1). Pass 3: [1, 3, 4, 7, 9]; pass 4 makes no swaps (1).
5. Up to n − 1 passes (1) each with up to n − 1 comparisons, about n² comparisons in total (1). Best case: data already sorted and the algorithm stops after a pass with no swaps, so one pass of n − 1 comparisons (1).
6. Insert 2: [2, 5, 9, 1, 6]; insert 9: no change (1); insert 1: [1, 2, 5, 9, 6] (1); insert 6: [1, 2, 5, 6, 9] (1).
7. Split into [8, 3, 5] [1, 7, 2], then [8] [3, 5] [1] [7, 2], then single items (2). Merge: [3, 5], [2, 7]; [3, 5, 8], [1, 2, 7]; [1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 8] (2).
8. Pivot 6: [3, 1, 2] 6 [9, 7, 8] (1). [3, 1, 2] pivot 3: [1, 2] 3; [9, 7, 8] pivot 9: [7, 8] 9 (1). Remaining partitions give [1, 2, 3, 6, 7, 8, 9] (1).
9. Each pivot is the smallest item so one partition is empty and the other has n − 1 items (1); n levels of recursion, each with O(n) comparisons, so O(n²) (1).
10. Merge sort or quick sort (1); O(n log n) is much faster than O(n²) for 2 million items (1); merge sort is guaranteed O(n log n)/stable; quick sort is fast on average with random data and uses little extra memory (1).
11. Base case low > high returns not found (1); mid calculated (1); found test returns mid (1); recursive calls on correct halves with mid ± 1, returning the result (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/10-shortest-path-dijkstra-and-a-star.md -->

## Shortest paths: Dijkstra and A*

This file covers Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm (tracing it and its uses) and the A* algorithm with heuristics. Weighted graphs are in [06-graphs-trees-hash-tables-and-dictionaries.md](06-graphs-trees-hash-tables-and-dictionaries.md); breadth-first search, which finds shortest paths in unweighted graphs, is in [08](08-graph-and-tree-traversal-and-reverse-polish.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dijkstra | Trace it; know applications. You do not need to recall the steps | Know and trace | "A shortest-path algorithm" (C1.3); calculate lowest-cost routes on a network (C2.2) |
| A* | Not required | Know and trace | Not required |

### 4.3.6.1 Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm

*Refs: AQA 4.3.6.1 · OCR 2.3.1(f) · Eduqas C1.3: Searching, C2.2: Communication networks.*

#### What you need to know

- Dijkstra's algorithm finds the shortest (lowest total weight) path from one start vertex to every other vertex in a weighted graph. Weights must not be negative.
- Idea: keep a tentative distance for every vertex (0 for the start, ∞ for the rest). Repeatedly pick the unvisited vertex with the smallest tentative distance, mark it visited (its distance is now final), and update ("relax") each unvisited neighbour: if distance(current) + edge weight is smaller than the neighbour's tentative distance, replace it and record current as the neighbour's previous vertex.
- Stop when all vertices are visited (or when the target is visited, if you only need one path). Rebuild the route by following previous vertices back from the target.
- A priority queue (file 05) is the natural structure for "pick the smallest". Time is about O(V²) with a simple array, or O((V + E) log V) with a heap-based priority queue.
- It is a greedy algorithm: it always takes the locally best choice, and with non-negative weights that is guaranteed to be optimal.
- Applications: satellite navigation and route planning; routing packets on the internet (link-state routing protocols such as OSPF use it); finding the cheapest or fastest connection in transport networks; network latency planning; games pathfinding (often via A*); planning cable or pipe layouts.

#### Worked example

Find the shortest path from A to every vertex.

```
A–B 4    A–C 2    B–C 1    B–D 5    C–D 8    C–E 10    D–E 2    D–F 6    E–F 2
```

| Step | Visit | A | B | C | D | E | F |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| start | | 0 | ∞ | ∞ | ∞ | ∞ | ∞ |
| 1 | A (0) | **0** | 4 (A) | 2 (A) | ∞ | ∞ | ∞ |
| 2 | C (2) | | 3 (C) | **2** | 10 (C) | 12 (C) | ∞ |
| 3 | B (3) | | **3** | | 8 (B) | 12 | ∞ |
| 4 | D (8) | | | | **8** | 10 (D) | 14 (D) |
| 5 | E (10) | | | | | **10** | 12 (E) |
| 6 | F (12) | | | | | | **12** |

Reading the steps:

- Step 2: from C, B can be reached in 2 + 1 = 3 < 4, so B's distance improves and its previous vertex becomes C.
- Step 3: from B, D is 3 + 5 = 8 < 10, so D improves.
- Step 4: from D, E is 8 + 2 = 10 < 12; F is 8 + 6 = 14.
- Step 5: from E, F is 10 + 2 = 12 < 14.

Shortest path A to F: follow previous vertices back: F ← E ← D ← B ← C ← A. Path A → C → B → D → E → F, length 12.

```python
import heapq

def dijkstra(graph, start):
    dist = {v: float("inf") for v in graph}
    prev = {v: None for v in graph}
    dist[start] = 0
    pq = [(0, start)]                       # priority queue of (distance, vertex)
    visited = set()
    while pq:
        d, v = heapq.heappop(pq)
        if v in visited:
            continue
        visited.add(v)                      # distance to v is now final
        for n, w in graph[v].items():
            if n not in visited and d + w < dist[n]:
                dist[n] = d + w             # relax the edge
                prev[n] = v
                heapq.heappush(pq, (dist[n], n))
    return dist, prev

def path_to(prev, target):
    path = []
    while target is not None:
        path.insert(0, target)
        target = prev[target]
    return path
```

#### Common mistakes

- Visiting vertices in alphabetical order rather than smallest tentative distance first.
- Updating the distance of an already visited vertex. Once visited, its distance is final.
- Stopping when the target is first given a tentative distance. It is only final when the target is visited.
- Using Dijkstra on graphs with negative weights: it can give wrong answers.

### The A* algorithm

*Refs: OCR 2.3.1(f).*

#### What you need to know

- A* finds the shortest path from a start to one goal vertex, usually faster than Dijkstra, by using a heuristic to guide the search towards the goal.
- For each vertex n: g(n) is the actual cost of the best path found so far from the start to n; h(n) is the heuristic, an estimate of the cost from n to the goal; f(n) = g(n) + h(n) is the estimated total cost of a path through n.
- Always expand the open (unvisited) vertex with the smallest f. Update neighbours' g, f and previous vertex as in Dijkstra. Stop when the goal is chosen for expansion.
- The heuristic must be admissible (never overestimate the real remaining cost) for A* to be guaranteed to find the shortest path. Common heuristics: straight-line (Euclidean) distance on maps; Manhattan distance (|Δx| + |Δy|) on grids with no diagonal moves.
- If h(n) = 0 for all n, A* behaves exactly like Dijkstra.
- Advantages: explores far fewer vertices than Dijkstra for a single goal, so faster. Disadvantages: needs a good heuristic; only finds a path to one goal; a poor (overestimating) heuristic may give a non-optimal path.
- Uses: games (moving characters around obstacles), robot navigation, route planners.

#### Worked example

Find the shortest path from S to G. Edge weights and heuristic values (estimated distance to G) are:

```
S–A 2    S–B 4    A–C 5    B–C 2    B–D 6    C–G 3    D–G 1
h: S 7, A 6, B 4, C 3, D 1, G 0
```
