Convert 2.7 with the same format: 0010; 0.7 → 0.5 (1, remainder 0.2) → 0.25 too big (0) → 0.125 (1, remainder 0.075) → 0.0625 (1, remainder 0.0125). So 0010.1011 = 2.6875, the closest that fits (by truncation).

#### Floating point: what you need to know

- A floating point number has a mantissa (the significant digits) and an exponent (the power of 2 that says where the binary point really goes): value = mantissa × 2^exponent.
- In the AQA (and usual OCR) format both are two's complement. The mantissa has its binary point just after the first (sign) bit: 0.1011000 or 1.0110000.
- Decode: work out the exponent (in two's complement); move the binary point in the mantissa that many places right (positive exponent) or left (negative exponent); then read the fixed point number.
- Encode: write the number in binary, then move the point so the mantissa is normalised (below), counting how many places it moved: that is the exponent.

#### Floating point: worked example

8-bit mantissa, 4-bit exponent.

Decode 0.1011000 | 0011. Exponent 3: move the point 3 right: 0101.1000 = 5.5.

Decode 1.0110000 | 0010. Exponent 2: move the point 2 right: 101.10000. In two's complement: −4 + 1 + 0.5 = −2.5.

Decode 0.1100000 | 1110. Exponent −2: move the point 2 left: 0.0011000 = 0.125 + 0.0625 = 0.1875.

Encode 13.25: 1101.01 = 0.110101 × 2⁴. Mantissa 0.1101010, exponent 0100.

Encode −13.25: start from the positive mantissa 0.1101010 and take its two's complement: 1.0010110. Exponent 0100. Check: (−1 + 0.125 + 0.03125 + 0.015625) × 16 = −0.828125 × 16 = −13.25 ✓.

Encode 0.375: 0.011 = 0.11 × 2⁻¹. Mantissa 0.1100000, exponent 1111 (−1).

### 4.5.4.8 Normalisation of floating point form

*Refs: AQA 4.5.4.8 · OCR 1.4.1(g).*

#### What you need to know

- A normalised floating point number has its mantissa start with 0.1 if positive or 1.0 if negative: the first two bits differ.
- Why normalise:
  - it gives the greatest possible precision for the number of mantissa bits (no wasted leading 0s or 1s);
  - every number has only one representation, which makes comparison and testing for equality easier;
  - it makes the best use of the range.
- To normalise: shift the mantissa left until the first two bits differ, and decrease the exponent by the number of places shifted (or, if the mantissa has overflowed, shift right and increase the exponent).

#### Worked example

Normalise 0.0010110 | 0101. Shift the mantissa left 2 places: 0.1011000. Exponent 5 − 2 = 3: 0011. Value check: before 0.0010110 × 2⁵ = 101.10 = 5.5; after 0.1011 × 2³ = 101.1 = 5.5 ✓.

Normalise 1.1101000 | 0011. Leading 1s are redundant, like leading 0s in a positive number. Shift left 2: 1.0100000. Exponent 3 − 2 = 1: 0001. Check: before (−1 + 0.5 + 0.25 + 0.0625) × 8 = −1.5; after (−1 + 0.25) × 2 = −1.5 ✓.

### 4.5.4.5 Rounding errors

*Refs: AQA 4.5.4.5 · Eduqas C2.3: Representation of numbers as bit patterns.*

#### What you need to know

- Both fixed and floating point can be inaccurate because only a limited number of bits is available.
- A decimal value can only be stored exactly if it can be written as a binary fraction (sum of halves, quarters, eighths and so on) within the bits available. Some values can never be stored exactly, whatever the number of bits, for example 0.1₁₀ (in binary it is 0.000110011001100… recurring), just as 1/3 recurs in decimal.
- Truncation: drop the bits that do not fit (always towards zero). Rounding: choose the nearest value that fits. Rounding gives a smaller maximum error; truncation is simpler. Errors grow when many calculations are chained (for example adding 0.1 a million times).

#### Worked example

Store 0.1 in a fixed point format with 8 fraction bits by truncation: 0.00011001 = 1/16 + 1/32 + 1/256 = 0.09765625.

```python
print(0.1 + 0.2)            # 0.30000000000000004
print(sum([0.1] * 10))      # 0.9999999999999999
```

### 4.5.4.6 Absolute and relative errors

*Refs: AQA 4.5.4.6.*

#### What you need to know

- Absolute error = |true value − stored value|.
- Relative error = absolute error ÷ |true value| (often given as a percentage).
- For very large numbers, an absolute error that looks big may be a tiny relative error; for very small numbers, a tiny absolute error may be a huge relative error. For numbers close to 1, absolute and relative errors are about the same.

#### Worked example

0.1 stored as 0.09765625. Absolute error = 0.00234375. Relative error = 0.00234375 ÷ 0.1 = 0.0234 = 2.34%.

A distance of 1,000,000 m stored as 999,936 m: absolute error 64 m, relative error 0.0064%. A length of 0.002 m stored as 0.0015 m: absolute error only 0.0005 m but relative error 25%.

### 4.5.4.7 Range and precision

*Refs: AQA 4.5.4.7 · Eduqas C2.3: Representation of numbers as bit patterns.*

#### What you need to know

- Range: the gap between the largest and smallest (most negative) values that can be stored. Precision: how close together the values that can be stored are (how many significant bits).
- For a fixed total number of bits in floating point: more mantissa bits gives more precision; more exponent bits gives more range.

| | Fixed point | Floating point |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Range | Small for the number of bits | Much larger for the same bits |
| Precision | Absolute precision is the same everywhere (the smallest fraction bit) | Relative precision is roughly constant; absolute precision is fine for small numbers and coarse for large ones |
| Speed | Faster: processed like integers | Slower: needs normalising and aligning; needs a floating point unit |
| Simplicity | Simpler hardware | More complex |
| Good for | Money (pence), sensors with a known range, small devices | Science and graphics with very large and very small values |

- Eduqas: integers versus floating point. Integers are exact, quick to process and use less storage, but have no fractions and limited range. Floating point stores fractions and very large or small values, but is usually approximate and slower.

### 4.5.4.9 Underflow and overflow

*Refs: AQA 4.5.4.9 · Eduqas C2.3: Representation of numbers as bit patterns.*

#### What you need to know

- Overflow: the result of a calculation is too large (in magnitude) to be represented with the bits available. In floating point, the exponent would need to be bigger than its maximum. Example: multiplying two very large numbers.
- Underflow: the result is too small in magnitude (too close to zero) to be represented: the exponent would need to be more negative than its minimum. The result is usually set to 0, losing information. Example: dividing a very small number by a very large one.
- In integer arithmetic, "overflow" also describes a carry out of the available bits (file 16); Eduqas also uses "underflow" for a result below the minimum value (for example below −128 in 8-bit two's complement).

#### Worked example

With a 4-bit two's complement exponent, the range of exponents is −8 to +7. The largest positive normalised value with an 8-bit mantissa is 0.1111111 × 2⁷ = 1111111.0 = 127. Doubling 100 would overflow. The smallest positive normalised value is 0.1000000 × 2⁻⁸ = 2⁻⁹ ≈ 0.00195; halving that underflows.

### Floating point addition and subtraction

*Refs: OCR 1.4.1(h).*

#### What you need to know

1. Make the exponents the same: shift the mantissa of the number with the smaller exponent right, increasing its exponent by one for each place, until the exponents match.
2. Add (or, for subtraction, add the two's complement of the second mantissa).
3. Normalise the result (and watch for a mantissa overflow, which needs a shift right and the exponent increased).

#### Worked example

A = 0.1100000 | 0011 (6). B = 0.1010000 | 0001 (1.25). 8-bit mantissa, 4-bit exponent.

A + B: B's exponent is 2 less, so shift B's mantissa right 2: 0.0010100 | 0011.

```
   0.1100000
 + 0.0010100
 -----------
   0.1110100   exponent 0011 → 111.01 = 7.25 ✓ (already normalised)
```

A − B: negate B's aligned mantissa: 0.0010100 → 1.1101100.

```
   0.1100000
 + 1.1101100
 -----------
  10.1001100   discard the carry → 0.1001100 | 0011 → 100.11 = 4.75 ✓
```

Shifting right to align can push 1s off the end, losing precision: adding a very small number to a very large one may leave the large one unchanged.

### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that the exponent is in two's complement: 1110 is −2, not 14.
- Moving the binary point the wrong way: a positive exponent moves it right.
- Negating a floating point number by changing only the first bit. Take the two's complement of the whole mantissa.
- Normalising a negative mantissa to start 1.1. It must start 1.0.
- Changing the exponent the wrong way when normalising: shifting the mantissa left makes it bigger, so the exponent must go down.

### Check questions

1. Convert 0101.1100 (4.4 fixed point, two's complement) and 1101.0100 to decimal. [2 marks]
2. Represent 3.625 in 8-bit fixed point with 4 fraction bits. [1 mark]
3. Floating point: 8-bit mantissa, 4-bit exponent, both two's complement. Convert to decimal: (a) 0.1101000 | 0010; (b) 1.0100000 | 0011; (c) 0.1000000 | 1101. [3 marks]
4. Represent 22.5 as a normalised floating point number in this format. [2 marks]
5. Represent −0.75 as a normalised floating point number in this format. [2 marks]
6. Normalise 0.0001101 | 0110 and 1.1110010 | 0010. [4 marks]
7. Give two reasons why floating point numbers are normalised. [2 marks]
8. Explain why 0.2 cannot be stored exactly in binary floating point. [2 marks]
9. 0.6 is stored as 0.59375. Calculate the absolute and relative errors. [2 marks]
10. A designer has 16 bits for a floating point number. Explain the effect of increasing the number of exponent bits. [2 marks]
11. Explain the difference between overflow and underflow in floating point. [2 marks]
12. Compare fixed point and floating point for storing amounts of money in a banking system. [3 marks]
13. (OCR) Add 0.1010000 | 0010 and 0.1100000 | 0001, giving a normalised result. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 4 + 1 + 0.5 + 0.25 = 5.75 (1); −8 + 4 + 1 + 0.25 = −2.75 (1).
2. 0011.1010 (1).
3. (a) 011.01000 = 3.25 (1). (b) 1010.0000 = −6 (1). (c) exponent −3: 0.0001000 = 0.0625 (1).
4. 22.5 = 10110.1 = 0.101101 × 2⁵ (1); mantissa 0.1011010, exponent 0101 (1).
5. 0.75 = 0.11; −0.75 = 1.01 (two's complement of 0.1100000 is 1.0100000) (1); mantissa 1.0100000 with exponent 0000 (1). (Check: −1 + 0.25 = −0.75.)
6. 0.0001101 | 0110: shift left 3 → 0.1101000 (1); exponent 6 − 3 = 3 → 0011 (1). 1.1110010 | 0010: shift left 3 → 1.0010000 (1); exponent 2 − 3 = −1 → 1111 (1).
7. Any two: maximum precision for the bits available (1); unique representation of each number/easier comparison (1); best use of range (1).
8. 0.2 is a recurring fraction in binary (0.001100110011…) (1); a finite number of bits can only hold an approximation (1).
9. Absolute 0.00625 (1); relative 0.00625 ÷ 0.6 ≈ 0.0104 or 1.04% (1).
10. Larger range of values (1); fewer mantissa bits, so less precision (1).
11. Overflow: number too large in magnitude/exponent above the maximum (1). Underflow: number too close to zero/exponent below the minimum, often stored as 0 (1).
12. Fixed point (or integer pence) gives exact values for pence with no rounding errors (1); faster/simpler (1); floating point's larger range is not needed and its approximations could cause errors in accounts (1).
13. Align: second number shifted right 1 → 0.0110000 | 0010 (1); sum 0.1010000 + 0.0110000 = 1.0000000, which is a mantissa overflow (it reads as negative) (1); shift right and increase exponent: 0.1000000 | 0011 = 4 (2.5 + 1.5 = 4 ✓) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/18-character-codes-and-error-checking.md -->

## Character codes and error checking

This file covers the difference between a digit stored as a character and as a number, ASCII and Unicode, and error checking and correction with parity bits, majority voting, checksums and check digits.

Builds on [GCSE: Representing text, images and sound](../../gcse/computer-science/10-representing-text-images-and-sound.md) (ASCII and Unicode basics) and the check digit and validation ideas in [GCSE: Robust programs and testing](../../gcse/computer-science/06-robust-programs-and-testing.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Character sets | Character form of a digit; ASCII and Unicode, why Unicode was introduced | ASCII and Unicode used to represent text | How characters are stored; standardised character sets |
| Error checking | Parity, majority voting, checksums, check digits | Not in this section (checksums appear with networks, file 29) | Validation and verification (C1.3, C2.5: file 30); online integrity (C2.8: file 36) |

### 4.5.5.1 Character form of a decimal digit

*Refs: AQA 4.5.5.1 · Eduqas C2.3: Storage of characters.*

#### What you need to know

- A digit can be stored as a character (its character code) or as a number (pure binary). They are different bit patterns.
- The character "7" has ASCII code 55 (0011 0111). The number 7 is 0000 0111. In ASCII, the code of a digit character is the digit + 48 (0011 0000), so the lower nibble of a digit's code equals its value.
- You cannot do arithmetic directly on character codes: "7" + "2" as characters is not 9. Text read from a keyboard or file must be converted (string to integer) before calculating.

#### Worked example

"2" + "5" as character codes: 50 + 53 = 103, which is the code for "g". To get 7, convert: (50 − 48) + (53 − 48) = 7.

### 4.5.5.2 ASCII and Unicode

*Refs: AQA 4.5.5.2 · OCR 1.4.1(j) · Eduqas C2.3: Storage of characters.*

#### What you need to know

- A character set is a defined list of characters a computer recognises, each with a unique binary code. Sender and receiver must use the same set.
- ASCII (American Standard Code for Information Interchange) uses 7 bits: 2⁷ = 128 characters: English upper and lower case letters, digits, punctuation and control codes (such as carriage return). Extended ASCII uses 8 bits for 256 characters, but the extra 128 vary between versions.
- Codes are in order: "A" = 65, "B" = 66, …; "a" = 97 (so lower case = upper case + 32); "0" = 48.
- Unicode was introduced because ASCII cannot represent the characters of most of the world's languages (Arabic, Chinese, Hindi), mathematical symbols or emoji, and different extended ASCII versions clashed. Unicode aims to give a unique code (code point) to every character in every writing system: over a million possible code points.
- Unicode encodings: UTF-8 uses 1 to 4 bytes per character and is backwards compatible with ASCII (the first 128 code points are the same and take one byte); UTF-16 uses 2 or 4 bytes; UTF-32 uses 4 bytes always.
- Trade-off: Unicode text may take more storage and transmission time than ASCII, but it allows global, multilingual text in one standard.
- Eduqas: storage of characters means each character is held as its code in binary; the number of bits per character sets how many characters can be represented.

#### Worked example

"Hi!" in ASCII: H = 72 = 100 1000, i = 105 = 110 1001, ! = 33 = 010 0001. In 8-bit bytes: 01001000 01101001 00100001: 3 bytes. In UTF-32 it would take 12 bytes; in UTF-8, 3 bytes.

"س" (Arabic letter seen) is code point U+0633. It cannot be written in ASCII; in UTF-8 it takes 2 bytes.

```python
print(ord("A"), chr(97), ord("7") - 48)       # 65 a 7
print("س".encode("utf-8"), len("س".encode("utf-8")))   # b'\xd8\xb3' 2
```

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ASCII uses 8 bits. Standard ASCII is 7 bits; 8 bits is extended ASCII.
- Saying Unicode always uses 16 bits. The number of bytes depends on the encoding.

### 4.5.5.3 Error checking and correction

*Refs: AQA 4.5.5.3.*

#### What you need to know

Data can be corrupted during transmission (interference, noise) or storage. Error detection finds that an error has happened; error correction also fixes it.

- Parity bit: an extra bit added to each group of bits (often a byte) so the total number of 1s is even (even parity) or odd (odd parity), as agreed. The receiver counts the 1s; if the parity is wrong, an error is detected and the data is re-sent. Detects any single-bit error (any odd number of flipped bits), but not an even number of flipped bits, and cannot say which bit is wrong.
- Majority voting: each bit is sent several times (an odd number, usually three). The receiver takes the value that appears most often in each group. Can detect and correct single-bit errors in a group, but triples the data sent.
- Checksum: the sender calculates a value from the block of data (for example the sum of the bytes MOD 256) and sends it with the data. The receiver recalculates it; if it differs, an error has occurred and the block is re-sent. Detects many errors in a block; cannot correct; some errors (for example two bytes swapped, with a simple sum) are not detected.
- Check digit: an extra digit calculated from the other digits of a code number (ISBN, barcodes, bank account numbers) and added to the end. Recalculating it detects mistakes in data entry, especially single-digit errors and swapped (transposed) digits when the digits are weighted. Used for typed or scanned data rather than transmission.

#### Worked example

Even parity: data 1011 001 (7 bits) has four 1s, so the parity bit is 0: sent as 0101 1001 with parity first, or 1011 0010 with parity last, depending on the agreed position. If the receiver gets 0101 1101, it counts five 1s: odd, so error detected.

Majority voting: sending 1 0 1 as 111 000 111. Received 101 000 111: first group has two 1s and one 0, so it is taken as 1. Data correctly recovered as 1 0 1.

Checksum (sum MOD 256): bytes 200, 100, 50 → 350 MOD 256 = 94. Send 200, 100, 50, 94. If 100 arrives as 101, the receiver gets 351 MOD 256 = 95 ≠ 94: error.

Check digit using the ISBN-13 rule: multiply digits alternately by 1 and 3, add, and choose the check digit that makes the total a multiple of 10. For 978-0-306-40615-?:

| Digit | 9 | 7 | 8 | 0 | 3 | 0 | 6 | 4 | 0 | 6 | 1 | 5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Weight | 1 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 1 | 3 |
| Product | 9 | 21 | 8 | 0 | 3 | 0 | 6 | 12 | 0 | 18 | 1 | 15 |

Sum = 93. Next multiple of 10 is 100, so the check digit is 7.

```python
def even_parity_bit(bits):
    return bits.count("1") % 2           # 1 if odd number of 1s, to make it even

def majority(triple):
    return "1" if triple.count("1") >= 2 else "0"

def isbn13_check(first12):
    total = sum(int(d) * (1 if i % 2 == 0 else 3) for i, d in enumerate(first12))
    return (10 - total % 10) % 10

print(even_parity_bit("1011001"), majority("101"), isbn13_check("978030640615"))  # 0 1 7
```

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a parity bit corrects errors. It only detects (and only an odd number of flipped bits).
- Saying a check digit checks that the data is correct. It checks the number was entered or read correctly, not that it is the right number for that person.
- Confusing a checksum (calculated over a block, for transmission) with a check digit (one extra digit in a code number).

### Check questions

1. The character "4" and the integer 4 are stored in 8 bits. Give both bit patterns. [2 marks]
2. Explain why Unicode was introduced. [2 marks]
3. Give one advantage of UTF-8 over UTF-32. [1 mark]
4. How many characters can 7-bit ASCII represent? [1 mark]
5. Add an odd parity bit (as the most significant bit) to 110 1100. [1 mark]
6. Explain why parity checking cannot detect two flipped bits in the same byte. [2 marks]
7. Using majority voting with three copies, decode 110 011 000 101. [2 marks]
8. A checksum is the sum of the bytes MOD 256. Calculate the checksum of 150, 180, 90. [1 mark]
9. Calculate the ISBN-13 check digit for 978-1-86197-271. [3 marks]
10. Compare majority voting with parity bits. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Character "4": 0011 0100 (1); integer 4: 0000 0100 (1).
2. ASCII could only represent 128/256 characters, mostly English (1); Unicode can represent characters from all the world's languages and symbols with one standard (1).
3. Uses less storage for English/ASCII text (1 byte per character) or is backwards compatible with ASCII (1).
4. 128 (1).
5. 110 1100 has four 1s, so odd parity bit is 1: 1110 1100 (1).
6. Two flips leave the count of 1s with the same parity (odd or even) (1); so the parity check still passes (1).
7. 110 → 1, 011 → 1, 000 → 0, 101 → 1 (1 for any three correct); data 1101 (1).
8. 420 MOD 256 = 164 (1).
9. Products: 9, 21, 8, 3, 8, 18, 1, 27, 7, 6, 7, 3 (1); sum 118 (1); check digit 2 (1).
10. Majority voting can correct errors as well as detect them (1); parity only detects (1); majority voting sends three times the data, parity adds only one bit per byte (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/19-images-sound-and-analogue-digital.md -->

## Images, sound and analogue/digital conversion

This file covers how bit patterns represent other data, analogue and digital data and signals, analogue-to-digital and digital-to-analogue converters, bitmapped images (resolution, colour depth, storage, metadata), vector graphics and how they compare with bitmaps, digital sound (sampling rate, sample resolution, Nyquist theorem, file sizes) and MIDI. Compression and encryption are in [20-compression-encryption-and-hashing.md](20-compression-encryption-and-hashing.md).

Builds on [GCSE: Representing text, images and sound](../../gcse/computer-science/10-representing-text-images-and-sound.md). New here: analogue and digital signals, ADC and DAC, dots per inch, metadata, vector graphics, the Nyquist theorem and MIDI.

#### Board differences

Only AQA 7517 names this content. OCR H446 and Eduqas A500QS do not examine image and sound representation in this detail (Eduqas covers input and output devices in C2.1, file 25).

### 4.5.6.1 Bit patterns, images, sound and other data

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.1.*

#### What you need to know

- All data in a computer (numbers, text, images, sound, video, instructions) is stored as bit patterns. A bit pattern has no meaning on its own: the same 8 bits could be a number, a character, the colour of one pixel or one sound sample. The program decides how to interpret it.

### 4.5.6.2 Analogue and digital

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.2.*

#### What you need to know

- Analogue data varies continuously and can take any value in a range (temperature, sound pressure, light level).
- Digital data takes only discrete (separate, distinct) values, for example whole numbers from 0 to 255.
- An analogue signal is a continuously varying electrical (or other) quantity, such as the voltage from a microphone. A digital signal switches between a fixed number of levels (usually two, representing 0 and 1).
- Digital signals are less affected by noise: a slightly distorted 1 is still read as 1, and digital signals can be regenerated exactly; analogue signals pick up noise that cannot be removed.

### 4.5.6.3 Analogue/digital conversion

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.3.*

#### What you need to know

- Analogue to digital converter (ADC): measures (samples) the analogue signal at regular time intervals and converts each measurement to the nearest of a fixed set of levels (quantisation), output as a binary number. Used with analogue sensors (temperature, light, pressure, microphones) so a computer can process their readings.
- Digital to analogue converter (DAC): turns a sequence of binary values into a voltage level for each one, producing a (stepped, then smoothed) analogue signal. Most common use: converting digital audio to an analogue signal to drive speakers or headphones.

### 4.5.6.4 Bitmapped graphics

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.4.*

#### What you need to know

- A bitmap stores an image as a grid of pixels (picture elements). Each pixel's colour is stored as a binary number.
- Size in pixels = width in pixels × height in pixels (sometimes called the image's resolution).
- Resolution is also expressed as dots per inch (dpi), where a dot is a pixel: how many pixels are printed or displayed per inch. Higher dpi means more detail at a given physical size.
- Colour depth = the number of bits stored for each pixel. n bits give 2ⁿ colours: 1 bit black and white; 8 bits 256 colours; 24 bits (8 each for red, green and blue) about 16.7 million.
- Storage (ignoring metadata) = size in pixels × colour depth (in bits). Divide by 8 for bytes.
- Metadata: data about the image stored in the file, such as width, height, colour depth, and often file format, date, camera settings and location. It lets software display the image correctly.

#### Worked example

A 1920 × 1080 image with 24-bit colour: 1920 × 1080 × 24 = 49,766,400 bits ÷ 8 = 6,220,800 bytes ≈ 6.2 MB (≈ 5.93 MiB).

A 6 × 4 inch photo scanned at 300 dpi with 16-bit colour: (6 × 300) × (4 × 300) = 1800 × 1200 = 2,160,000 pixels × 16 bits = 34,560,000 bits = 4,320,000 bytes = 4.32 MB.

An image has 100 colours: the minimum colour depth is 7 bits (2⁶ = 64 is too few, 2⁷ = 128 is enough).

### 4.5.6.5 Vector graphics

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.5.*

#### What you need to know

- A vector graphic stores an image as a list of geometric objects (primitives) such as lines, rectangles, circles, polygons, curves and text, each with its properties.
- Typical properties: position (x, y coordinates), size (width, height, radius), line (stroke) colour and thickness, fill colour, rotation, layer (drawing order).
- To display it, the computer reads the list and draws each object, so it can be redrawn at any size.

#### Worked example

A simple flag as a vector list (SVG, a common vector format):

```xml
<svg width="300" height="200">
  <rect x="0" y="0" width="300" height="200" fill="green"/>
  <circle cx="150" cy="100" r="50" fill="white"/>
  <line x1="0" y1="0" x2="300" y2="200" stroke="black" stroke-width="4"/>
</svg>
```

Three objects describe the whole image. As a 300 × 200 bitmap with 24-bit colour it would need 180,000 bytes.

### 4.5.6.6 Vector graphics versus bitmapped graphics

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.6.*

| | Vector | Bitmap |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scaling | Scales to any size with no loss of quality | Becomes blocky (pixelated) when enlarged |
| File size | Usually small for simple images; grows with the number of objects | Depends on pixel count and colour depth, not content |
| Editing | Each object can be moved or changed separately | Edit pixel by pixel |
| Realism | Poor for photographs and complex shading | Ideal for photographs and natural images |
| Processing | Must be calculated (rendered) to display | Displayed directly |
| Uses | Logos, icons, diagrams, maps, fonts, CAD | Photos, scanned images, detailed artwork, screenshots |

### 4.5.6.7 Digital representation of sound

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.7.*

#### What you need to know

- Sound is an analogue wave. To store it, an ADC samples its amplitude at regular intervals.
- Sampling rate: the number of samples per second, in hertz (Hz). CD quality is 44,100 Hz.
- Sample resolution: the number of bits used for each sample (CD quality: 16 bits). More bits mean more possible amplitude levels, so the stored value is closer to the true amplitude and there is less quantisation noise.
- Nyquist theorem: to reproduce a signal accurately, the sampling rate must be at least twice the highest frequency in the signal. Human hearing reaches about 20 kHz, so 44.1 kHz is enough.
- File size (bits) = sampling rate × sample resolution × duration in seconds (× number of channels, for stereo).
- Higher rate and resolution give better quality but larger files.

#### Worked example

A 3-minute stereo recording at 44.1 kHz with 16-bit samples: 44,100 × 16 × 180 × 2 = 254,016,000 bits = 31,752,000 bytes ≈ 31.8 MB.

A sound with frequencies up to 8 kHz must be sampled at least 16,000 times per second.

### 4.5.6.8 Musical Instrument Digital Interface (MIDI)

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.8.*

#### What you need to know

- MIDI is a standard protocol that lets electronic musical instruments, computers and other devices communicate. It does not store sound itself; it stores instructions for playing it.
- A MIDI file is a sequence of event messages, such as: note on / note off (which note), velocity (how hard it was played, so how loud), instrument (program change), duration and timing, and controls such as sustain or pitch bend.
- Advantages: files are very small (a few bytes per note rather than thousands of samples per second); music is easy to edit (change a note, the tempo, the key or the instrument) without re-recording; instruments can be changed after recording; notation can be produced directly; devices from different makers work together.
- Disadvantages: the sound depends on the device playing it, so it may not sound the same everywhere; it cannot store speech or real recordings.

### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to divide by 8 to convert bits to bytes, or mixing up MB and MiB.
- Saying resolution is the same as colour depth. Resolution is about pixel count (or density); colour depth is bits per pixel.
- Saying a higher sampling rate "makes the sound louder". It makes the stored wave closer to the original.
- Saying MIDI "compresses" audio. It stores instructions, not audio samples.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between analogue and digital data. [2 marks]
2. Describe how an ADC converts a microphone signal into digital data. [3 marks]
3. Give the most common use of a DAC. [1 mark]
4. Calculate the storage needed, in kilobytes, for an 800 × 600 bitmap with 256 colours (ignore metadata). [3 marks]
5. State two items of metadata stored with a bitmap image and why they are needed. [2 marks]
6. List the vector objects and properties needed to draw a red square with sides of 100 units centred at (200, 200), with a black border 2 units wide. [3 marks]
7. Give two advantages of vector graphics over bitmaps and one situation where a bitmap is better. [3 marks]
8. Explain the Nyquist theorem and apply it to a sound containing frequencies up to 12 kHz. [2 marks]
9. Calculate the size in MB of a 2-minute mono recording at 22,050 Hz with 8-bit samples. [2 marks]
10. Explain two advantages of storing music as MIDI rather than as sampled audio. [2 marks]
11. Explain how increasing sample resolution improves sound quality. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Analogue data varies continuously and can take any value in a range (1); digital data takes only discrete values (1).
2. The signal is sampled at regular intervals (1); each sample's amplitude is measured and matched to the nearest available level/quantised (1); the level is output as a binary number (1).
3. Converting digital audio to an analogue signal for speakers/headphones (1).
4. 256 colours → 8 bits per pixel (1); 800 × 600 × 8 = 3,840,000 bits = 480,000 bytes (1); = 480 kB (accept 468.75 KiB) (1).
5. Any two from width, height, colour depth (1); needed so the software knows how to interpret the bits/how to rebuild the grid of pixels (1).
6. Rectangle (or square) object (1); position: x 150, y 150 (top-left) and width 100, height 100 (1); fill colour red, stroke colour black, stroke width 2 (1).
7. Advantages: scale without losing quality (1); smaller files for simple images/objects can be edited separately (1). Bitmap better for photographs/realistic images (1).
8. Sample at least twice the highest frequency (1); at least 24 kHz (24,000 samples per second) (1).
9. 22,050 × 8 × 120 = 21,168,000 bits (1); = 2,646,000 bytes ≈ 2.65 MB (1).
10. Any two: much smaller files (1); easy to edit notes/tempo/instruments (1); can produce sheet music (1).
11. More bits per sample gives more possible amplitude levels (1); so each sample is nearer the true value/less quantisation error (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/20-compression-encryption-and-hashing.md -->

## Compression, encryption and hashing

This file covers why files are compressed, lossy and lossless compression, run length encoding and dictionary-based methods, measuring compression, encryption terms, the Caesar and Vernam ciphers, perfect versus computational security, symmetric and asymmetric encryption, and the uses of hashing. Digital certificates and signatures are in [28-the-internet-and-internet-security.md](28-the-internet-and-internet-security.md).

Builds on [GCSE: Units and compression](../../gcse/computer-science/11-units-and-compression.md) (lossy and lossless, RLE, Huffman for AQA) and the encryption basics in [GCSE: Cyber security](../../gcse/computer-science/17-cyber-security.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Compression | Why; lossy vs lossless; RLE and dictionary-based | Lossy vs lossless; RLE and dictionary coding | Compression algorithms; compare by ratio, compression and decompression time, saving % |
| Ciphers | Caesar and Vernam; perfect vs computational security | Symmetric and asymmetric encryption | Need for cryptography; follow cryptographic algorithms; compare strengths; XOR in encryption (C1.2) |
| Hashing | Not here (hash tables in file 06) | Different uses of hashing | Hashing for files (file 36) |
| Public key | In 4.9.3.2 (file 28) | Asymmetric here | Compare methods |

### 4.5.6.9 Data compression

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.9 · OCR 1.3.1(a), 1.3.1(b) · Eduqas C1.3: Compression.*

#### What you need to know

- Why compress: smaller files take less storage, transfer faster over networks (less bandwidth and lower cost), stream with less buffering, and fit more in an email attachment or on a device. Images and sound are often compressed because raw files are very large; text and program files can be compressed too.
- Lossless compression: the original file can be rebuilt exactly. Needed for text, program code, spreadsheets and anything where every bit matters. Usually smaller savings. Formats: ZIP, PNG, FLAC, GIF.
- Lossy compression: permanently removes data that is judged least noticeable (detail the eye or ear is less sensitive to, such as sounds outside hearing range or tiny colour changes). Much bigger savings, but quality is reduced and the original cannot be recovered; repeated saving loses more. Formats: JPEG, MP3, MP4/H.264. Not suitable for text or code.
- Run length encoding (RLE, lossless): replace a run of repeated values with one copy of the value and a count. Works well on data with long runs (simple graphics, icons, fax images), but can make data with few runs larger.
- Dictionary-based methods (lossless; LZ-family, used in ZIP and GIF): build a dictionary of repeated sequences (words or byte patterns) and replace each occurrence with a short reference (index) into the dictionary. The dictionary is stored with the file or rebuilt during decompression. Works well on text with repeated words.
- Eduqas measures of efficiency:
  - compression ratio = original size ÷ compressed size (for example 4:1);
  - saving percentage = (original − compressed) ÷ original × 100;
  - compression time and decompression time: a method with a better ratio may be slower; for streaming, fast decompression matters most.

#### Worked example

RLE on a row of pixels: `WWWWWWBBBWWWWWWWWRR` → `6W3B8W2R`: 19 characters become 8. On `WBWBWB` it gives `1W1B1W1B1W1B`: longer than the original.

RLE in code:

```python
def rle_encode(s):
    out = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        run = 1
        while i + run < len(s) and s[i + run] == s[i]:
            run += 1
        out.append(f"{run}{s[i]}")
        i += run
    return "".join(out)

print(rle_encode("WWWWWWBBBWWWWWWWWRR"))   # 6W3B8W2R
```

Dictionary method on "THE CAT AND THE HAT AND THE BAT":

| Index | Entry |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | THE |
| 2 | CAT |
| 3 | AND |
| 4 | HAT |
| 5 | BAT |

Encoded: 1 2 3 1 4 3 1 5 (spaces implied). Eight words become eight small indices; each repeated word is stored only once in the dictionary. The longer the text and the more repetition, the bigger the saving.

Measuring: a 12 MB file compresses to 3 MB. Ratio 12 ÷ 3 = 4:1. Saving (12 − 3) ÷ 12 = 75%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying lossy compression "removes unnecessary data". It removes real data that is judged less noticeable; it cannot be recovered.
- Recommending lossy compression for text, code or medical images where every detail matters.

### 4.5.6.10 Encryption

*Refs: AQA 4.5.6.10 · OCR 1.3.1(c) · Eduqas C2.8: Cryptography, C1.2: Logical operations.*

#### What you need to know

- Encryption transforms plaintext (the readable message) into ciphertext (unreadable without the key) using a cipher (the algorithm) and a key, so that only intended recipients can read it. Decryption reverses it.
- Cryptography is needed to protect the confidentiality of data in transmission (online banking, messaging) and in storage (stolen laptops, cloud), and to support authentication and integrity.
- Caesar cipher: shift every letter a fixed number of places along the alphabet (the key). Easily cracked because there are only 25 possible keys (brute force) and letter frequencies are preserved (frequency analysis: the most common ciphertext letter probably stands for E).
- Vernam cipher (one-time pad): combine each bit (or character) of the plaintext with the matching bit of a key using XOR. The key must be:
  - truly random,
  - at least as long as the message,
  - used once only, and kept secret.
  Decryption XORs the ciphertext with the same key.
- Why Vernam has perfect security: because the key is chosen uniformly at random and used once, the ciphertext is also uniformly random. Every possible plaintext of that length is equally likely, so the ciphertext gives an attacker no information at all, whatever computing power they have. It is the only cipher mathematically proved to be unbreakable.
- Computational security: every other cipher can in theory be broken given enough ciphertext and time; it is considered secure because breaking it would take an impractical amount of time with current computers (for example trying 2²⁵⁶ keys). These ciphers are practical because keys are short and reusable. The Vernam cipher's drawback is key distribution: a key as long as all the data must be shared securely in advance and never reused.
- Symmetric encryption (OCR): the same key encrypts and decrypts (AES, Caesar, Vernam). Fast, but the key must be shared securely, and each pair of users needs its own key.
- Asymmetric (public key) encryption (OCR, and AQA 4.9.3.2): each user has a public key (shared with anyone) and a private key (kept secret), mathematically linked. Data encrypted with the recipient's public key can only be decrypted with their private key. No secret key needs to be exchanged, but it is much slower. In practice (for example in TLS), asymmetric encryption is used to exchange a symmetric session key, which then encrypts the rest of the data.
- Eduqas: compare methods by strength: key length (number of possible keys), whether frequency patterns survive, resistance to brute force, and key management.

#### Worked example

Caesar with key 3: plaintext ATTACK → DWWDFN (A→D, T→W, C→F, K→N). Decrypt by shifting back 3.

Vernam with bits. Plaintext letter "H" = 0100 1000; key 1100 1011.

```
plaintext   0100 1000
key       ⊕ 1100 1011
ciphertext  1000 0011
key       ⊕ 1100 1011
plaintext   0100 1000   ← XOR with the same key restores it
```

Vernam with letters (A = 0 … Z = 25, add mod 26): plaintext HI = 7, 8; key XM = 23, 12. Ciphertext (7 + 23) mod 26 = 4 → E; (8 + 12) mod 26 = 20 → U. "EU". Decrypt by subtracting the key mod 26.

```python
import secrets

def vernam(data: bytes, key: bytes) -> bytes:
    assert len(key) >= len(data)
    return bytes(d ^ k for d, k in zip(data, key))

msg = b"MEET AT NOON"
key = secrets.token_bytes(len(msg))      # truly random, used once
cipher = vernam(msg, key)
print(vernam(cipher, key))               # b'MEET AT NOON'
```

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Vernam is unbreakable "because the key is long". The key must also be truly random and never reused; reusing it breaks the security.
- Saying asymmetric encryption encrypts with the private key for confidentiality. For confidentiality you encrypt with the recipient's public key.
- Mixing up encryption (reversible with the key) and hashing (one-way).

### Hashing

*Refs: OCR 1.3.1(d).*

#### What you need to know

- A hash function turns data of any length into a fixed-length value (hash, digest). A cryptographic hash is one-way (you cannot get the input back from the hash), deterministic (same input, same hash), and a tiny change in the input changes the hash completely; it should be very hard to find two inputs with the same hash.
- Uses:
  - storing passwords: store the hash (with a random salt), not the password. At login, hash what is typed and compare. A data breach does not reveal the passwords directly;
  - checking integrity: compare the hash of a downloaded file with the published hash to show it has not been changed or corrupted;
  - digital signatures: the sender's private key encrypts a hash of the message (file 28);
  - hash tables and database indexing for fast look-up (file 06);
  - detecting duplicate files.

#### Worked example

```python
import hashlib
print(hashlib.sha256(b"password1").hexdigest()[:16])
print(hashlib.sha256(b"password2").hexdigest()[:16])
## two completely different digests from a one-character change
```

### Check questions

1. Give two reasons for compressing a video before uploading it. [2 marks]
2. Explain why lossy compression is not suitable for a program's source code. [2 marks]
3. Encode `AAAABBBCCDAAAA` with RLE and calculate the compression ratio (count each character or digit as one unit). [3 marks]
4. Describe how a dictionary-based method compresses text. [3 marks]
5. (Eduqas) A 40 MB file compresses to 16 MB. Calculate the compression ratio and the saving percentage. [2 marks]
6. Encrypt COMPUTER with a Caesar cipher using a shift of 5. [1 mark]
7. Explain two reasons why the Caesar cipher is easily cracked. [2 marks]
8. Encrypt the bit pattern 1011 0110 with the Vernam key 0110 1100, then show decryption. [2 marks]
9. State three conditions on the key for a Vernam cipher to give perfect security. [3 marks]
10. Compare the Vernam cipher with ciphers that depend on computational security. [3 marks]
11. (OCR) Explain the difference between symmetric and asymmetric encryption, and why both are often used together. [4 marks]
12. (OCR) Explain why passwords are stored as hashes. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: less storage on the server (1); faster upload/download (1); less bandwidth/data used (1); smoother streaming (1).
2. Lossy removes data permanently (1); every character of code matters, so the program would not work/the original cannot be recovered exactly (1).
3. 4A3B2C1D4A (1); 14 units to 10 units (1); ratio 1.4:1 (1).
4. Repeated words/patterns are found and stored once in a dictionary (1); each occurrence is replaced by a short reference/index to the dictionary entry (1); the dictionary is stored/rebuilt so the text can be decompressed exactly (1).
5. Ratio 40 ÷ 16 = 2.5:1 (1); saving 24 ÷ 40 = 60% (1).
6. HTRUZYJW (1).
7. Only 25 possible keys so brute force is quick (1); letter frequencies are preserved so frequency analysis works (1).
8. Ciphertext 1101 1010 (1); 1101 1010 XOR 0110 1100 = 1011 0110 (1).
9. Truly random (1); at least as long as the message (1); used only once/kept secret (1).
10. Vernam is mathematically proven perfectly secure/gives no information about the plaintext (1); other ciphers could be broken in theory given enough time and ciphertext; they rely on it taking too long (1); Vernam keys are hard to generate and distribute securely, so computationally secure ciphers are more practical (1).
11. Symmetric: same key to encrypt and decrypt (1). Asymmetric: public key to encrypt, matching private key to decrypt (1). Asymmetric avoids sharing a secret key but is slow; symmetric is fast but needs a shared key (1). So asymmetric is used to exchange a symmetric session key, which encrypts the bulk data (1).
12. Hashes are one-way, so if the database is stolen the passwords cannot be read (1); at login the entered password is hashed and compared with the stored hash (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/21-software-languages-and-translators.md -->

## Software, languages and translators

This file covers hardware and software, system and application software, operating systems (in outline), utilities and libraries, open source and closed source, bespoke and off-the-shelf software, machine code, assembly and high-level languages, assemblers, compilers and interpreters, bytecode and intermediate code, source and object code, the stages of compilation, translation and execution errors, and linkers and loaders. Operating systems in detail (memory management, scheduling, interrupts) are in [34-operating-systems.md](34-operating-systems.md).

Builds on [GCSE: System software](../../gcse/computer-science/14-system-software.md) and [GCSE: Languages, translators and IDEs](../../gcse/computer-science/08-languages-translators-and-ides.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Software types | System vs application; OS, utilities, libraries, translators | Nature of applications; utilities; open vs closed source | Utilities (C2.6); open source, bespoke, off-the-shelf (C2.7) |
| Languages | Machine code, assembly, imperative high-level | (Paradigms in 1.2.4, file 02) | High-level vs low-level and when each is needed (C1.4) |
| Translators | Assembler, compiler, interpreter; bytecode; source vs object code | Interpreters, compilers, assemblers | Same, with examples |
| Compilation stages | Not required | Lexical analysis, syntax analysis, code generation, optimisation | Lexical analysis, symbol table, syntax analysis, semantic analysis, code generation, optimisation |
| Linkers and loaders | Not required | Linkers, loaders and libraries | Not named |
| Errors | Not named | Not named | Translation errors vs execution errors |

### 4.6.1.1 Relationship between hardware and software

*Refs: AQA 4.6.1.1.*

#### What you need to know

- Hardware: the physical components of a computer system (processor, memory, storage, input and output devices, circuit boards).
- Software: the programs (sequences of instructions) and data that run on the hardware.
- They depend on each other: hardware does nothing useful without software to control it, and software needs hardware to run on. The operating system and drivers sit between applications and hardware.

### 4.6.1.2 Classification of software

*Refs: AQA 4.6.1.2 · OCR 1.2.2(a), 1.2.2(c) · Eduqas C2.7: Types of software.*

#### What you need to know

- System software manages and controls the computer's hardware and provides a platform for other software: operating systems, utilities, libraries, translators.
- Application software lets users do useful tasks: word processors, spreadsheets, databases, web browsers, games, graphics editors, payroll and stock control systems.
- OCR: justify suitable applications for a purpose (for example a database rather than a spreadsheet for linked customer and order records that many staff update; presentation software for a talk).
- Open source software: the source code is published and users may study, change and share it (usually under a licence such as the GPL). Usually free; community support and many eyes finding bugs; can be customised. But support may be less formal, quality varies, and changes may have to be shared under the same licence.
- Closed source (proprietary) software: only the compiled program is supplied; the code is protected and you buy a licence to use it. Usually professional support, documentation and regular tested updates; but costs money, cannot be changed, and you depend on the vendor.
- Off-the-shelf (generic) software: ready-made for a wide market. Cheaper, available at once, well tested, with support and training material, but may include unneeded features or lack needed ones.
- Bespoke software: written specially for one organisation. Does exactly what is needed and can give a competitive edge, but is expensive, slow to develop, and may have more bugs at first; support depends on the developer.

### 4.6.1.3 System software

*Refs: AQA 4.6.1.3 · OCR 1.2.2(b) · Eduqas C2.6: Utility software.*

#### What you need to know

- Operating system: manages the hardware and provides an interface and services for programs and users (next point and file 34).
- Utility programs: small programs that maintain and look after the computer, such as disk defragmenter (rearranges fragmented files into contiguous blocks so they load faster on a hard disk; not needed on SSDs), backup, compression, antivirus/anti-malware, disk cleanup, encryption, file managers, firewall, system monitors.
- Libraries: collections of pre-written, pre-compiled, tested subroutines (for example maths, graphics, file handling) that programs can call. They save development time, are reliable, and can be shared between programs (dynamic link libraries are loaded at run time).
- Translators: convert program source code into machine code (assembler, compiler, interpreter: below).

### 4.6.1.4 Role of an operating system

*Refs: AQA 4.6.1.4.*

#### What you need to know

- The OS hides the complexities of the hardware from users and programs. A program asks the OS to "save this file" without knowing how the disk controller works; this is abstraction (a virtual machine view of the computer).
- The OS manages resources, allocating processors (processor scheduling), memory and input/output devices among competing processes, so several programs can run safely at once. Details are in [34-operating-systems.md](34-operating-systems.md).

### 4.6.2.1 Classification of programming languages

*Refs: AQA 4.6.2.1 · Eduqas C1.4: Levels of computer language.*

#### What you need to know

- Languages developed from machine code (1940s), through assembly languages (1950s), to high-level languages (FORTRAN, COBOL and on to Python today).
- Low-level languages: machine code and assembly language. Each is specific to one processor family (its instruction set).
  - Machine code: instructions as binary (an opcode and operands) that the processor executes directly.
  - Assembly language: uses mnemonics (such as ADD, LDR, BNE) and labels in place of binary. Usually one assembly instruction translates to one machine code instruction. Translated by an assembler.
- High-level languages are closer to English and mathematical notation and independent of the processor. An imperative high-level language describes a sequence of commands that change the program's state (variables), using constructs like selection, iteration and subroutines; each high-level statement translates into several machine code instructions.

| | Low-level (machine code, assembly) | High-level |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Speed and size | Can be very fast and compact; programmer controls exactly what the hardware does | Usually slower/larger, though modern compilers optimise well |
| Hardware access | Direct access to registers and memory addresses | Usually indirect |
| Portability | Processor-specific | Runs on any machine with a translator |
| Ease | Hard to write, read, debug and maintain; many lines | Easier, fewer lines, built-in structures and libraries |
| Errors | Easy to make, hard to find | Fewer errors; translator checks syntax |

- When to use low-level (Eduqas): device drivers, embedded systems with tiny memory, real-time systems needing exact timing, parts of operating systems and games where speed is critical, and accessing hardware directly. High-level for almost everything else: faster development and easier maintenance.

### 4.6.3.1 Types of program translator

*Refs: AQA 4.6.3.1 · OCR 1.2.2(d) · Eduqas C1.8: Compilers, interpreters and assemblers.*

#### What you need to know

- Source code: the program as written by the programmer (in a high-level or assembly language). Object code: the translated machine code (or intermediate code) output by a translator; an executable is object code ready to run.
- Assembler: translates assembly language into machine code, usually one-to-one.
- Compiler: translates the whole high-level program into object code before it runs, producing an executable file.
  - Advantages: the executable runs fast; the program can be distributed without the source (protecting it); no translator needed on the user's machine; the compiler can optimise the whole program.
  - Disadvantages: every change needs recompiling (slower development); errors are reported only after compiling the whole program; the executable only runs on one platform.
- Interpreter: translates and runs the program one statement at a time, every time it runs; no executable is produced.
  - Advantages: stops at the first error with its line, so easier debugging; quick to test small changes; source runs on any machine with the interpreter.
  - Disadvantages: slower to run (each statement is translated each time, including inside loops); the interpreter must be present; the source code must be given to users.
- Use a compiler for finished software for sale and for speed; use an interpreter during development, for scripts, and for teaching.
- Intermediate language (bytecode): some compilers (Java, C#, Python) translate to bytecode, a low-level code for a virtual machine rather than a real processor. The virtual machine (for example the Java Virtual Machine) then interprets or just-in-time compiles the bytecode on each platform. Why: portability ("write once, run anywhere": the same bytecode runs on any device with the VM); faster than interpreting source; the source code is not distributed; the VM can check the code for safety.

### Stages of compilation

*Refs: OCR 1.2.2(e) · Eduqas C1.8: Compilers, interpreters and assemblers.*

#### What you need to know

1. Lexical analysis: remove white space and comments; split the source into tokens (keywords, identifiers, operators, literals, punctuation); build the symbol table (Eduqas names symbol table construction as a stage): a record of each identifier with its type, scope and later its memory address. Lexical errors, such as an illegal character or badly formed number, are reported.
2. Syntax analysis: check the tokens follow the grammar of the language (often defined in BNF, file 13), building a parse tree (abstract syntax tree). Syntax errors, such as a missing bracket, are reported.
3. Semantic analysis (Eduqas): check the meaning makes sense: variables declared before use, types compatible in expressions and assignments, correct numbers of parameters. Some treat this as part of syntax analysis (OCR).
4. Code generation: produce object code (machine code or intermediate code) from the syntax tree, allocating memory addresses for variables using the symbol table.
5. Optimisation: improve the code so it runs faster or uses less memory without changing what it does: remove code that can never run, remove repeated calculations, move unchanging calculations out of loops, replace slow instructions with faster ones. Optimisation can happen before and after code generation; it makes compiling slower.

#### Worked example

Source line: `total = total + price * 2   # update`

- Lexical analysis drops the comment and spaces and produces tokens: `IDENT(total) ASSIGN IDENT(total) PLUS IDENT(price) TIMES INT(2)`; total and price go in the symbol table.
- Syntax analysis checks this matches `<assignment> ::= <ident> = <expression>` and builds the tree with × below + (precedence).
- Semantic analysis checks total and price are numeric and declared.
- Code generation produces, for example: `LDR R1, price` / `LSL R1, R1, #1` / `LDR R0, total` / `ADD R0, R0, R1` / `STR R0, total`. (Optimisation has already chosen a shift for × 2.)

### Translation and execution errors

*Refs: Eduqas C1.8: Compilers, interpreters and assemblers.*

#### What you need to know

- Translation (compile-time) errors are found by the translator before the program runs: syntax errors (`pritn("hi")`, missing colon), and semantic errors such as using an undeclared variable or a type mismatch in a statically typed language.
- Execution (run-time) errors happen while the program runs: division by zero, an array index out of range, a file not found, overflow, running out of memory. Logic errors (wrong output with no crash) are found by testing (file 33).

### Linkers, loaders and libraries

*Refs: OCR 1.2.2(f).*

#### What you need to know

- Linker: combines the object code of a program with the object code of the library routines (and other separately compiled modules) it uses into one executable, resolving references (filling in the addresses of external subroutines).
  - Static linking: library code is copied into the executable. The program is self-contained and always has the right version, but is larger and does not benefit from library updates.
  - Dynamic linking: the executable holds only references; libraries (DLLs, shared objects) are loaded at run time. Executables are smaller and libraries are shared in memory and updated in one place, but the program fails if the library is missing or the wrong version.
- Loader: part of the OS that copies an executable (and any dynamic libraries) from storage into main memory, sets up addresses (relocating them if the program is not loaded where it expected), and starts it.

### Common mistakes

- Saying an interpreter "produces machine code that is saved". It does not save a translated version.
- Saying compiled programs "run on any computer". They run only on the platform they were compiled for.
- Confusing a linker (combines object code files) with a loader (puts the executable into memory).
- Listing stages of compilation in the wrong order: lexical, syntax (and semantic), code generation, optimisation.

### Check questions

1. Define hardware and software and explain how they depend on each other. [3 marks]
2. Give two examples of utility programs and what each does. [2 marks]
3. Explain what is meant by "the operating system hides the complexities of the hardware". [2 marks]
4. Give two advantages and one disadvantage of writing in assembly language rather than a high-level language. [3 marks]
5. Describe two differences between compilation and interpretation. [4 marks]
6. Explain why some compilers produce bytecode, and how the bytecode is run. [3 marks]
7. (OCR, Eduqas) Describe what happens during lexical analysis and syntax analysis. [4 marks]
8. (OCR, Eduqas) Give two examples of code optimisation. [2 marks]
9. (OCR) Explain the role of a linker and compare static and dynamic linking. [4 marks]
10. (Eduqas) Explain the difference between a translation error and an execution error, with an example of each. [4 marks]
11. (OCR, Eduqas) A small charity needs a donor database. Discuss whether it should use open source or closed source software. [4 marks]
12. (Eduqas) Compare bespoke and off-the-shelf software for a large supermarket's stock control system. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Hardware: physical components (1); software: programs/instructions and data (1); hardware needs software to control it and software needs hardware to run on (1).
2. Any two, each with function: defragmenter (rearranges file blocks contiguously), backup, antivirus, compression, disk cleanup, encryption (2).
3. Programs/users can use hardware through simple OS services without knowing how the hardware works (1); e.g. saving a file without controlling the disk directly/the OS provides an abstraction or virtual machine (1).
4. Advantages: fast/efficient code; direct control of hardware/registers; small memory use (2). Disadvantage: hard to write/debug/maintain, or processor-specific so not portable (1).
5. Any two pairs: compiler translates the whole program before running, interpreter translates line by line as it runs (2); compiler produces an executable, interpreter does not (2); compiled code runs faster, interpreted code is translated every time (2); interpreter stops at the first error with its location, compiler reports errors after compiling (2).
6. For portability: bytecode can run on any platform with the virtual machine (1); the source code need not be distributed/faster than interpreting source (1); the VM interprets or just-in-time compiles the bytecode into the host's machine code (1).
7. Lexical: removes comments/white space (1); splits code into tokens and builds the symbol table (1). Syntax: checks tokens follow the language's grammar rules (1); builds a parse/abstract syntax tree and reports syntax errors (1).
8. Any two: removing unreachable/redundant code; moving loop-invariant calculations out of loops; replacing operations with faster ones (e.g. shift for × 2); avoiding repeated calculations (2).
9. Linker combines the program's object code with library/module object code into one executable, resolving addresses of external routines (2). Static: library code copied in, larger but self-contained (1). Dynamic: library loaded at run time, smaller and shared/updated centrally but may be missing/wrong version (1).
10. Translation error found by the translator before running (1), e.g. a syntax error such as a missing bracket (1). Execution error occurs while running (1), e.g. division by zero/index out of range (1).
11. Open source: no licence cost, which suits a charity's budget; can be adapted; community support (2). Closed source: professional support and updates, more polished/easier for non-specialist staff, but licence fees (1). Reasoned conclusion (1).
12. Bespoke: built exactly for the supermarket's processes and scale; can integrate with existing systems; competitive advantage; but costly and slow to develop (2). Off-the-shelf: cheaper, immediately available, tested, with support, but may not fit processes or scale (1). Justified recommendation (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/34-operating-systems.md -->

## Operating systems

This file covers what an operating system does and the kernel, the user interface, managing backing store (directories and file attributes), memory management (partitioning, paging, segmentation and virtual memory), interrupts and their priorities, buffering and double buffering, scheduling (process states, round robin, first come first served, multi-level feedback queues, shortest job first, shortest remaining time, time slicing, polling, threading), types of operating system and modes of operation, BIOS, device drivers and virtual machines.

AQA's outline of the OS's role is in [21-software-languages-and-translators.md](21-software-languages-and-translators.md); interrupts within the fetch-execute cycle are in [23-processor-and-fetch-execute.md](23-processor-and-fetch-execute.md). Builds on [GCSE: System software](../../gcse/computer-science/14-system-software.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Purpose | Hides hardware complexity; resource management (4.6.1.4) | Need for, function and purpose | Kernel manages peripherals, processes, memory protection and backing store; provides an interface |
| Memory | Not named | Paging, segmentation, virtual memory | Partitioning of main memory; buffering and double buffering |
| Interrupts | File 23 | Interrupts and ISRs | Conditions that cause interrupts; handling and priorities |
| Scheduling | Not named | Round robin, FCFS, multi-level feedback queues, SJF, SRT | High-level scheduling; process states; time slicing, polling, threading |
| Types | Not named | Distributed, embedded, multi-tasking, multi-user, real time | Batch, single-user, multi-user, multi-tasking, multi-programming; modes: batch, real-time control, real-time transaction |
| Other | Not named | BIOS, device drivers, virtual machines | Directories and file attributes; HCI for different users |

### Purpose and functions of an operating system

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(a) · Eduqas C2.6: Managing resources, Providing an interface · AQA 4.6.1.4.*

#### What you need to know

- An operating system (OS) is system software that manages the computer's hardware and resources and provides a platform for applications and an interface for users. Without it, every program would have to control the hardware itself.
- The kernel is the core of the OS that stays in memory and manages resources:
  - processes: creating, scheduling and ending them; sharing the processor;
  - memory: allocating and freeing memory, and memory protection (stopping one process reading or overwriting another's memory or the OS's);
  - peripherals: controlling input/output devices through device drivers; handling interrupts;
  - backing store: managing files and storage space.
- Other functions: providing a user interface; security (user accounts, passwords, access rights, file permissions); system utilities; error handling; logging.
- Providing an interface: the OS sits between the user and the hardware. Types: graphical user interface (GUI), command-line interface (CLI: powerful and precise for experts, low resource use, scriptable, but must remember commands), menu-driven, natural language and voice, and touch. For programs, the OS provides an application programming interface (system calls) so they can use hardware through simple requests.
- Consideration of human-computer interaction (Eduqas): systems must suit a wide variety of users at all levels (beginners to experts, children, people with disabilities) and environments (noisy factories, cars, outdoors, small screens). Examples: accessibility settings (screen readers, magnification, high contrast, voice control), shortcuts for experts, simple menus for beginners, large touch targets in vehicles.

### Managing backing store

*Refs: Eduqas C2.6: Managing backing store.*

#### What you need to know

- The file manager organises files in a hierarchical (tree) directory structure: a root directory containing files and sub-directories (folders), each of which can contain more. A file is located by its path, such as /home/sana/coursework/report.docx.
- Benefits: files grouped logically; the same file name can be used in different folders; permissions can be set per folder.
- File attributes (metadata held by the file system): name and extension, type, size, location on disk, dates created, modified and accessed, owner, permissions (read, write, execute), and flags such as hidden, read-only, system and archive (needs backing up).
- The OS tracks which blocks on the disk belong to each file and which are free (for example a file allocation table).

### Memory management

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(b) · Eduqas C2.6: Memory management and buffering.*

#### What you need to know

- The OS must share main memory between the OS and several processes, keep them apart (protection), and let programs run that are larger than the free memory.
- Partitioning (Eduqas): dividing main memory into sections, each holding one process.
  - Fixed partitions: set sizes. Simple, but a small process in a big partition wastes space (internal fragmentation), and a big process may not fit anywhere.
  - Variable (dynamic) partitions: each process gets exactly what it needs. Less waste at first, but as processes end, free memory is broken into scattered small gaps (external fragmentation), which may need compaction (moving processes together).
  - Reasons: run several programs at once; protect processes from each other. Consequences: fragmentation, limits on how many or how large processes can be, overhead of managing partitions.
- Paging: memory is split into equal-sized blocks called pages (in virtual memory) and page frames (in physical memory). A process's pages can be placed in any free frames, not necessarily next to each other; a page table maps each page to its frame. Pages are a physical division, not related to the program's structure. No external fragmentation; small waste in the last page.
- Segmentation: memory is split into variable-sized segments that match logical parts of a program (a function, a module, the stack, an array). A segment table records each segment's start address and length. Segments can be shared and protected individually, but cause external fragmentation.
- Virtual memory: uses part of secondary storage as if it were extra main memory. Pages not currently needed are moved out of RAM to disk (paged out or swapped) and brought back (paged in) when needed.
  - Benefit: programs larger than physical RAM can run; more programs can be open at once.
  - Drawback: disk is much slower than RAM. If too many pages are swapped back and forth, the computer spends more time moving pages than doing work: disk thrashing.
- Buffering: a buffer is an area of memory that holds data temporarily while it is moved between devices or processes that work at different speeds (processor and printer; network and video player). The faster device fills or empties the buffer and can do other work meanwhile; an interrupt signals when the buffer needs attention.
- Double buffering: two buffers are used; while one is being filled, the other is being emptied (processed or displayed); then they swap. Data flows continuously with no waiting. Used for video and audio streaming and in graphics (draw the next frame in a hidden buffer, then swap, so the screen never shows a half-drawn image, avoiding flicker).

#### Worked example

Paging: pages are 4 KiB. A 14 KiB program needs 4 pages (the last only half used). Its page table:

| Page | Frame |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | 7 |
| 1 | 2 |
| 2 | (on disk) |
| 3 | 11 |

When the program accesses page 2, a page fault occurs; the OS loads page 2 from disk into a free frame (swapping another page out if memory is full) and updates the table.

### Interrupts and priorities

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(c) · Eduqas C2.6: Interrupts.*

#### What you need to know

- Events that generate interrupts:
  - hardware: a key pressed, mouse moved, printer out of paper, disk transfer complete, data arrived on the network;
  - timer: the end of a time slice (used for scheduling);
  - software or program errors: division by zero, invalid memory access, an attempt to run an illegal instruction, a program requesting an OS service;
  - hardware failure: power failure, memory parity error.
- Handling: at the end of each fetch-execute cycle the processor checks for interrupts; the current state is saved on the stack; the matching interrupt service routine runs; the state is restored (full detail in file 23).
- Priorities: each interrupt type has a priority. A higher-priority interrupt can interrupt a lower-priority ISR; lower-priority interrupts wait (are queued or masked) until higher ones finish.
- Factors in allocating priorities: how urgent it is and how serious it would be to delay it (power failure and hardware faults highest, as data could be lost); how quickly the device's data would be lost or overwritten if not handled (fast devices whose buffers fill quickly come above slow ones like printers); safety in real-time systems; user responsiveness (a keyboard above a background disk task).

### Scheduling

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(d) · Eduqas C2.6: Scheduling.*

#### What you need to know

- In a multi-tasking system many processes compete for the processor. The scheduler decides which process runs and for how long, aiming to: keep the processor busy, give each process a fair share, respond quickly to users, maximise throughput (jobs completed), and meet deadlines.
- High-level scheduling (Eduqas) decides which jobs are admitted into memory to be run, allocates devices, and takes job priorities into account. Low-level scheduling picks which ready process gets the processor next.
- Process states:
  - running: currently using the processor;
  - ready: in memory, waiting only for the processor;
  - blocked (waiting): cannot continue until an event happens, such as input/output completing.
  Transitions: ready → running (dispatched by the scheduler); running → ready (time slice ends or a higher-priority process arrives); running → blocked (requests I/O); blocked → ready (I/O completes, signalled by an interrupt).
- Time slicing: each process gets a short fixed period of processor time (a quantum) before the timer interrupts and the next process runs. Gives the impression that all processes run at once.
- Polling: the processor repeatedly checks each device in turn to see if it needs attention. Simple, but wastes processor time compared with interrupts.
- Threading: a thread is a single sequence of instructions within a process; a process can have several threads that share its memory and run concurrently (for example one thread for the user interface and another downloading a file), so the program stays responsive and can use several cores.

Scheduling algorithms (OCR):

| Algorithm | How it works | Pros | Cons |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| First come, first served (FCFS) | Run jobs in order of arrival, each to completion | Simple; fair in order | Short jobs wait behind long ones; not pre-emptive, so poor response |
| Round robin | Each job in the ready queue gets a fixed time slice in turn; unfinished jobs go to the back of the queue | Fair; good response for interactive users | Ignores priority and job length; many context switches; long jobs take a long time |
| Shortest job first (SJF) | Run the waiting job with the shortest estimated total time next, to completion | Maximises throughput; short average waiting time | Needs time estimates; long jobs may starve |
| Shortest remaining time (SRT) | Pre-emptive SJF: run the job with the least time left; a newly arrived shorter job interrupts the current one | Very short average waiting time | Needs estimates; long jobs may starve; more switching |
| Multi-level feedback queues (MLFQ) | Several queues of different priority; new jobs start in the top queue; a job that uses its whole time slice moves down; jobs waiting a long time can move up | Favours short and interactive (I/O-bound) jobs while preventing starvation; adapts to behaviour | Complex to set up and tune |

#### Worked example

Jobs arrive at time 0 in the order A (6 ms), B (2 ms), C (4 ms).

- FCFS: A 0–6, B 6–8, C 8–12. Waiting times: A 0, B 6, C 8. Average 14 ÷ 3 ≈ 4.7 ms.
- SJF: B 0–2, C 2–6, A 6–12. Waiting: B 0, C 2, A 6. Average 8 ÷ 3 ≈ 2.7 ms.
- Round robin, quantum 2 ms: A 0–2, B 2–4 (done), C 4–6, A 6–8, C 8–10 (done), A 10–12 (done). Completion: B 4, C 10, A 12.

SRT example: A (6 ms) arrives at 0; B (2 ms) arrives at 1. At time 1, A has 5 ms left and B needs 2, so B pre-empts A: A 0–1, B 1–3, A 3–8.

### Types of operating system and modes of operation

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(e) · Eduqas C2.6: Types of operating system, Modes of operation.*

#### What you need to know

- Single-user (standalone): one user at a time (a personal laptop). Can still be multi-tasking.
- Multi-user (multi-access): several users share one computer's resources at the same time, usually through terminals (a mainframe or server). The OS must schedule fairly and keep users' data separate and secure.
- Multi-tasking: one user (or the system) runs several programs apparently at once, by rapidly switching between them (time slicing) or using several cores.
- Multi-programming (Eduqas): several programs are held in memory at once; when one waits for I/O, the processor switches to another, so it is never idle. The basis of multi-tasking.
- Batch (Eduqas): jobs (programs and data) are collected and processed in a group, without user interaction, often at a scheduled time. Suits large, repetitive work where an immediate result is not needed: payroll, utility bills, bank statements, exam marking.
- Real time: the system must respond to input within a guaranteed, very short time, because delays could be dangerous or costly. Must be highly reliable, often with redundant hardware.
  - Real-time control (Eduqas): controlling physical processes using sensors and actuators in a feedback loop: aircraft autopilots, car anti-lock brakes, pacemakers, chemical plants, traffic lights.
  - Real-time transaction processing (Eduqas): each transaction is processed immediately as it happens, so data is always up to date and, for example, the same seat cannot be sold twice: booking systems, cash machines, online shopping stock levels.
- Distributed (OCR): one OS runs across several networked computers, making them appear as a single system and sharing out tasks and resources (used in clusters and some cloud systems).
- Embedded (OCR): a small, efficient OS built into a device with a dedicated function (washing machine, router, smart TV, car engine management). Limited resources, often real time, usually no general user interface; highly reliable.

### BIOS

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(f).*

#### What you need to know

- The Basic Input/Output System (BIOS; on modern PCs, UEFI) is firmware stored in ROM (flash) on the motherboard. It runs first when the computer is switched on.
- It performs the power-on self-test (POST: checks the processor, memory and essential hardware), initialises hardware, then finds a bootable device and runs the bootstrap loader, which loads the operating system into RAM. It also holds settings such as boot order and clock.

### Device drivers

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(g).*

#### What you need to know

- A device driver is software that lets the OS communicate with a particular hardware device. It translates the OS's general instructions ("print this page") into the specific commands that device understands, and handles its interrupts.
- Drivers are specific to the device and to the OS. New hardware needs a driver installed (often automatically); updated drivers fix bugs and improve performance. They let the OS work with thousands of devices without knowing each one's details.

### Virtual machines

*Refs: OCR 1.2.1(h).*

#### What you need to know

- A virtual machine is any instance where software takes on the function of a machine.
- System virtual machines: software (a hypervisor) lets one physical computer run several operating systems at once, each in its own isolated virtual machine with virtual hardware. Uses: running several servers on one physical machine (saving cost and energy), testing software on different OSs, running old software, safely testing malware in isolation, cloud computing.
- Process virtual machines and intermediate code: a program is compiled to intermediate code (bytecode) that is run by a virtual machine (the Java Virtual Machine, Python's VM) rather than directly by the processor (file 21). Benefit: portability; drawback: slower than native code.
- Drawbacks of virtual machines generally: slower than running directly on hardware; one physical failure affects all VMs on it.

### Common mistakes

- Saying virtual memory makes the computer faster. It allows more to run, but swapping to disk is slow and can cause thrashing.
- Mixing up pages (fixed size, physical) and segments (variable size, logical).
- Describing round robin as "shortest job first". Round robin gives equal time slices in turn.
- Saying a blocked process goes straight back to running when its I/O completes. It moves to the ready state.
- Saying the BIOS is the operating system. It loads the OS.

### Check questions

1. State four functions of an operating system's kernel. [4 marks]
2. (Eduqas) Explain the purpose of a hierarchical directory structure and list four file attributes. [4 marks]
3. (OCR) Compare paging and segmentation. [4 marks]
4. (OCR) Explain how virtual memory works and what is meant by disk thrashing. [4 marks]
5. (Eduqas) Explain why main memory is partitioned and one consequence of using variable partitions. [3 marks]
6. (Eduqas) Explain double buffering and give a use. [3 marks]
7. (Eduqas) Explain why a power failure interrupt has a higher priority than a printer interrupt. [2 marks]
8. Draw or describe the three process states and the transitions between them. [4 marks]
