- Bit rate: the number of bits transmitted per second (bps). If each signal change can represent more than one bit (for example 4 voltage levels give 2 bits per symbol), bit rate = baud rate × bits per symbol, so the bit rate can be higher than the baud rate.
- Bandwidth: the range of frequencies a channel can carry (in Hz). Bit rate is directly proportional to bandwidth: a higher bandwidth channel can carry more bits per second. (In everyday use "bandwidth" often means the maximum bit rate.)
- Latency: the time delay between data being sent and arriving (caused by distance, the number of devices it passes through, and processing). Matters for gaming, video calls and trading.
- Protocol: an agreed set of rules for communication between devices, covering format of data, timing, error checking, addressing and how to start and end a connection. Standards (Eduqas, OCR) are protocols and hardware specifications agreed by bodies such as the IEEE and IETF, so equipment from different makers works together.
- Data transfer time (Eduqas) = file size in bits ÷ bit rate (bits per second). Remember 1 byte = 8 bits and check the prefixes.

#### Worked example

A line has a baud rate of 2,400 and each symbol encodes 4 bits: bit rate = 9,600 bps.

Time to send a 15 MB file on a 40 Mbps link: 15 × 10⁶ × 8 = 120 × 10⁶ bits; 120 × 10⁶ ÷ 40 × 10⁶ = 3 s. With asynchronous framing of 1 start and 1 stop bit per byte, 10 bits are sent per byte, so 150 × 10⁶ bits: 3.75 s.

### Multiplexing and switching

*Refs: Eduqas C2.2: Data transmission.*

#### What you need to know

- Multiplexing combines several signals so they share one channel (one cable or radio band), which is cheaper than a separate link for each. Time-division multiplexing gives each signal a time slot in turn; frequency-division multiplexing gives each a different frequency band (as in broadband over phone lines, or radio). A demultiplexer separates them at the other end.
- Switching decides how data is routed through a network: circuit switching sets up a dedicated path for the whole communication (traditional phone calls); packet switching splits data into packets that are routed independently (the internet). Both are compared in file 28.

### 4.9.2.1 Network topology

*Refs: AQA 4.9.2.1 · OCR 1.3.3(a).*

#### What you need to know

- A LAN (local area network) covers a small area such as a building or site; the organisation owns the equipment. A WAN (wide area network) covers a large geographical area, often using communication links leased from telecoms companies; the internet is the largest WAN.
- Physical topology: how the devices are actually wired. Logical topology: how data actually flows between them.
- Physical star: each device has its own cable to a central switch (or hub).
  - Operation: with a switch, a frame goes only to the device it is addressed to (using MAC addresses).
  - Advantages: a cable fault only affects one device; easy to add devices; good performance as each link is separate; secure as data goes only to its recipient. Disadvantages: more cable; if the central switch fails, the whole network fails.
- Logical bus: all devices share one communication channel, so a transmission reaches every device and each one checks whether it is the recipient. Only one device can transmit at a time, and collisions can happen.
- A network physically wired as a star can behave logically as a bus if the centre is a hub (or the protocol broadcasts every frame to all ports), as early Ethernet did.

### 4.9.2.2 Types of networking between hosts

*Refs: AQA 4.9.2.2 · OCR 1.3.3(d).*

#### What you need to know

- Peer-to-peer (P2P): every computer has equal status and can act as both client and server, sharing its own resources directly with others.
  - Advantages: cheap (no dedicated server), easy to set up, no single point of failure; for file sharing, load is spread across many peers.
  - Disadvantages: no central management, backup or security; files scattered; performance depends on peers being online.
  - Uses: small home or office networks; file sharing (BitTorrent); some video calling and blockchain systems.
- Client-server: one or more computers are servers that provide services (files, email, web pages, printing, databases, authentication); the rest are clients that request services.
  - Advantages: central control of security, user accounts, backups and updates; files in one place; scales to large organisations.
  - Disadvantages: servers are expensive and need specialist staff; if a server fails, its service is lost (single point of failure unless duplicated).
  - Uses: schools, businesses, websites, online games.

### Network hardware

*Refs: OCR 1.3.3(c) · Eduqas C2.1: Networking.*

#### What you need to know

- Network interface card (NIC) or network adapter: connects a device to the network; has a unique MAC address. A wireless network adapter does this by radio.
- Switch: connects devices on a LAN; learns which MAC address is on which port and forwards each frame only to the right port.
- Hub (older): forwards every frame to all ports (creating a logical bus).
- Router: connects different networks (for example a LAN to the internet) and forwards packets between them using IP addresses.
- Wireless access point (WAP): lets wireless devices join a wired network. Home "routers" usually combine a router, switch, WAP and modem.
- Modem: converts digital data to signals for a phone or cable line and back.
- Transmission media: copper (twisted pair Ethernet), fibre optic (very fast, long distance, immune to electrical interference, expensive), and wireless (radio).

### 4.9.2.3 Wireless networking

*Refs: AQA 4.9.2.3 · Eduqas C2.1: Networking.*

#### What you need to know

- Wi-Fi is a wireless local area network technology based on international standards (IEEE 802.11). Its purpose: let devices connect to a network without cables, so they can move around.
- Components: a wireless network adapter in each device, and a wireless access point connected to the wired network.
- SSID (Service Set Identifier): the name of a wireless network, broadcast so devices can find and choose it (it also identifies which network each frame belongs to).
- Securing a wireless network:
  - strong encryption of transmitted data using WPA2 or WPA3 (Wi-Fi Protected Access) with a strong passphrase;
  - disabling SSID broadcast (hides the network from casual scanning, though it can still be found with tools);
  - a MAC address allow list (only listed devices may connect, though MAC addresses can be faked).
- CSMA/CA (Carrier Sense Multiple Access with Collision Avoidance): wireless devices cannot detect a collision while transmitting, so they try to avoid one:
  1. Listen to check the channel is idle (carrier sense).
  2. If it is busy, wait a random back-off time, then listen again.
  3. If it is idle, transmit the frame.
  4. Wait for an acknowledgement (ACK) from the receiver. If none arrives, assume a collision happened and try again after a random wait.
- RTS/CTS (Request to Send / Clear to Send), an optional addition: before sending, the device sends a short RTS frame to the access point; the access point replies with CTS, which all devices in range hear, so they stay quiet for the stated time. This solves the hidden node problem: two devices that are both in range of the access point but out of range of each other cannot hear each other's transmissions, so carrier sense alone would not stop them colliding.
- Eduqas: portable devices (laptops, phones, tablets, sensors) need networks for internet access, email, cloud storage, synchronisation, location services, and updates. Contemporary wireless technologies include Wi-Fi, Bluetooth (short range, personal area networks), mobile data (4G and 5G), NFC (very short range: contactless payment) and satellite.

### Collisions and handshaking

*Refs: Eduqas C2.1: Networking, C2.2: Communication networks.*

#### What you need to know

- A collision happens when two devices on a shared channel transmit at the same time and their signals interfere, so both frames are corrupted.
- CSMA/CD (Collision Detection), used on wired shared Ethernet: listen before sending; while sending, keep listening; if a collision is detected, stop, send a jam signal so all devices know, then each waits a random back-off time before trying again (random times make a second collision less likely). (AQA does not require CSMA/CD.) Switched full-duplex Ethernet has no collisions because each link is separate.
- Handshaking: an exchange of signals between two devices before (and during) communication to agree they are ready and to set the rules: speed, protocol, error checking. Example: TCP's three-way handshake (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK) sets up a connection (file 29); a printer signals "ready" or "busy" to the computer.

### Common mistakes

- Saying parallel is always faster because "more bits at once". Skew and crosstalk limit parallel links over distance.
- Mixing up baud rate and bit rate.
- Saying disabling SSID broadcast or MAC filtering makes a network secure. Encryption is the essential protection; the others only deter casual users.
- Saying CSMA/CA detects collisions. It avoids them, and infers a collision when no acknowledgement arrives.

### Check questions

1. Give two reasons why serial transmission is used for long-distance links instead of parallel. [2 marks]
2. Explain the purpose of start and stop bits in asynchronous transmission. [2 marks]
3. Compare synchronous and asynchronous transmission. [3 marks]
4. A modem uses 8 signal levels at 1,200 baud. Calculate its bit rate. [2 marks]
5. Define latency and bandwidth, and state the relationship between bandwidth and bit rate. [3 marks]
6. (Eduqas) Calculate the time to transfer a 600 MB file over a 100 Mbps connection. [2 marks]
7. Explain how a network can be a physical star but a logical bus. [2 marks]
8. A small dental surgery has five computers. Recommend peer-to-peer or client-server, with reasons. [3 marks]
9. Describe three ways of securing a Wi-Fi network. [3 marks]
10. Describe how CSMA/CA with RTS/CTS works and the problem RTS/CTS solves. [5 marks]
11. (Eduqas) Explain what a network collision is and how CSMA/CD deals with it. [4 marks]
12. (Eduqas) Explain the difference between half duplex and full duplex, with an example of each. [2 marks]
13. (OCR) State the function of a switch and a router. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: no skew between wires (1); less crosstalk/interference (1); fewer wires so cheaper (1); can run at higher frequency/faster (1).
2. Start bit signals the start of a character and lets the receiver synchronise its clock (1); stop bit marks the end and gives time before the next character (1).
3. Synchronous uses a shared clock/timing signal and asynchronous does not (1); synchronous sends continuous blocks, asynchronous sends one character at a time with start/stop bits (1); synchronous is more efficient for large data, asynchronous suits irregular data/is simpler (1).
4. 8 levels = 3 bits per symbol (1); 3 × 1,200 = 3,600 bps (1).
5. Latency: delay between sending and receiving (1). Bandwidth: range of frequencies the channel can carry (1). Bit rate is directly proportional to bandwidth (1).
6. 600 × 8 = 4,800 Mb (1); 4,800 ÷ 100 = 48 s (1).
7. Each device is wired to a central hub/switch (physical star) (1); but the hub/protocol sends every transmission to all devices, which share one channel (logical bus) (1).
8. Peer-to-peer may suit the small size and cost (1); but client-server is better for patient records: central security, access control, backups and data protection compliance (1); justified recommendation (1).
9. WPA2/WPA3 encryption with a strong key (1); disable SSID broadcast (1); MAC address allow list (1).
10. Device listens for an idle channel; if busy, waits a random time (1). Sends RTS to the access point (1); AP replies with CTS, heard by all devices, which stay quiet (1); device sends data and waits for ACK; no ACK means retransmit after random back-off (1). Solves the hidden node problem where devices cannot hear each other but can both reach the AP (1).
11. Two devices transmit on a shared medium at the same time and the signals corrupt each other (1). Devices listen before sending and while sending (1); on detecting a collision, stop and send a jam signal (1); wait a random back-off time and retry (1).
12. Half duplex: both directions but only one at a time, e.g. walkie-talkie (1). Full duplex: both directions at once, e.g. telephone call (1).
13. Switch forwards frames within a LAN only to the destination device using MAC addresses (1); router forwards packets between networks using IP addresses (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/28-the-internet-and-internet-security.md -->

## The internet and internet security

This file covers the structure of the internet, packet switching and circuit switching, what is in a packet, routers and gateways, how routing works, URLs, fully qualified domain names, domain names and IP addresses, DNS, internet registries, firewalls (packet filtering, proxy servers, stateful inspection), symmetric and asymmetric encryption and key exchange, digital certificates and signatures, and worms, trojans and viruses and how to defend against them. TCP/IP layers and addressing are in [29-tcp-ip-addressing-and-client-server.md](29-tcp-ip-addressing-and-client-server.md); encryption basics in [20](20-compression-encryption-and-hashing.md).

Builds on [GCSE: Networks](../../gcse/computer-science/15-networks.md) and [GCSE: Cyber security](../../gcse/computer-science/17-cyber-security.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Structure | Internet structure; packet switching; routers; gateways; routing; URL, FQDN, DNS, registries | Internet structure; DNS; packet and circuit switching | The internet as a world-wide infrastructure; packet contents; routing traffic |
| Security | Firewalls (packet filtering, proxy, stateful inspection); encryption and key exchange; certificates and signatures; worms, trojans, viruses | Network security and threats; firewalls, proxies and encryption | Malware types, vectors; black hat, white hat, penetration testing (C2.8); more in file 36 |

### 4.9.3.1 The internet and how it works

*Refs: AQA 4.9.3.1 · OCR 1.3.3(a) · Eduqas C2.2: Communication networks.*

#### What you need to know

Structure:

- The internet is a global network of networks: millions of separate networks (homes, businesses, universities, ISPs) connected together and all using the TCP/IP protocols.
- The backbone is made of very high-capacity links (mostly fibre optic, including undersea cables) between major routers and internet exchange points run by large network providers. Internet service providers (ISPs) connect homes and organisations to it. Eduqas: it is a world-wide communications infrastructure that services such as the web, email, streaming and VoIP run on top of.
- The internet is not the World Wide Web: the web is one service (web pages over HTTP) that runs on the internet.

Packet switching:

- Data is split into packets. Each packet is sent independently and may take a different route; routers forward each packet towards its destination based on its address and current network conditions. At the destination, packets are put back in order using their sequence numbers, and missing ones are requested again.
- Main components of a packet:
  - header: source IP address, destination IP address, sequence number (packet number and total), protocol, time to live (TTL: a hop count, reduced by each router; the packet is discarded at 0 so it cannot circulate forever);
  - payload: the data itself;
  - trailer: an error check such as a checksum or CRC (in some protocols).
- Advantages: links are shared by many users, so efficient use of capacity; packets route around failures or congestion; no setup needed. Disadvantages: packets may arrive out of order or be delayed (variable latency), so less suited to real-time data without extra measures; overhead of headers.

Circuit switching (OCR, Eduqas):

- A dedicated path is set up between sender and receiver for the whole communication (as in traditional phone calls). All data follows that path in order.
- Advantages: guaranteed bandwidth, data arrives in order with constant delay: good for real-time voice and video. Disadvantages: the link is reserved even when nothing is sent (wasteful); setup time; a broken link ends the call.

Routers and gateways:

- A router connects two or more networks and forwards packets between them. It reads the destination IP address and uses its routing table to choose the next hop: the best neighbouring router towards the destination.
- A gateway connects networks that use different protocols, translating between them (for example changing the packet format). The device connecting a home network to the internet is often called the default gateway.
- Routing: each router only knows the next step. Routers share information with each other (routing protocols) to build their tables, often choosing paths with the fewest hops or lowest cost, using algorithms like Dijkstra's (file 10). If a link fails, tables are updated and packets take another path.

URLs, domain names and IP addresses:

- An IP address identifies a device's network interface on the internet (for example 151.101.0.81 in IPv4).
- A domain name is a human-readable name for a network or organisation, such as example.co.uk.
- A fully qualified domain name (FQDN) includes the host name too, identifying one specific host: www.example.co.uk or mail.example.co.uk.
- Domain names are organised hierarchically, read right to left: the root, then top-level domains (.com, .uk, .org), second-level domains (.co under .uk), the organisation's own name, then subdomains or host names.
- A URL (uniform resource locator) gives the full address of a resource on the internet: protocol (scheme), FQDN (or IP address), optional port, and path to the file. For https://www.example.co.uk/courses/cs.html: protocol https, FQDN www.example.co.uk, path /courses/cs.html.

DNS:

- The Domain Name System translates domain names into IP addresses, because people remember names but routers use numbers.
- How a look-up works: the browser checks its own cache, then the computer's, then asks the configured DNS resolver (usually the ISP's). If the resolver does not have it cached, it asks a root server, which refers it to the server for the top-level domain (.uk), which refers it to the server for example.co.uk, which returns the IP address. The resolver caches the answer (for a set time) and returns it to the browser, which then connects to that IP address.
- DNS is a distributed, hierarchical database: no single server holds everything, which makes it scalable and resilient.

Internet registries:

- Internet registries allocate and record IP addresses and domain names, so that each one is unique worldwide. IANA (the Internet Assigned Numbers Authority) manages the global pool and allocates blocks to five regional internet registries (such as RIPE NCC for Europe), which allocate them to ISPs and organisations. Domain registries keep the records for each top-level domain (Nominet for .uk).
- Needed because addresses and names must be unique or data could not be delivered, and because IPv4 addresses are scarce and must be shared out fairly. Registries also keep a public record of who holds each domain (WHOIS).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying each packet "follows the same route". In packet switching each one is routed independently.
- Saying DNS "stores websites". It maps names to IP addresses.
- Mixing up a domain name and an FQDN: the FQDN includes the host name.

### 4.9.3.2 Internet security

*Refs: AQA 4.9.3.2 · OCR 1.3.3(b) · Eduqas C2.8: Malicious software and mechanisms of attack and defence.*

#### Firewalls and proxies

- A firewall monitors and controls traffic between a trusted network and an untrusted one (the internet), allowing or blocking it according to rules. It can be hardware, software, or both.
- Packet filtering (static filtering): examines each packet's header (source and destination IP addresses, port numbers, protocol) against rules, and accepts or drops it. Fast, but each packet is judged on its own; it cannot see the packet's content or whether it belongs to a valid conversation. Blocking a port number blocks the service that uses it (for example blocking port 23 blocks Telnet).
- Stateful inspection: keeps track of active connections (state tables) and checks that each packet belongs to an established, legitimate conversation (for example, allowing incoming packets only in reply to a request from inside). It can also check packet contents. More secure, but uses more processing.
- Proxy server: acts as an intermediary. Clients send requests to the proxy, which makes the request to the external server on their behalf and passes the reply back. The external server sees only the proxy's IP address, hiding internal addresses. The proxy can filter content (block sites, scan for malware), log use, and cache popular pages to save bandwidth and speed up access.

#### Encryption and key exchange

- Symmetric encryption: one shared secret key encrypts and decrypts. Fast, but the key must be exchanged securely first, and if intercepted, everything is readable (the key exchange problem).
- Asymmetric (public key) encryption: each party has a key pair. The public key is shared openly; the private key is kept secret. Data encrypted with someone's public key can only be decrypted with their private key, so anyone can send them a confidential message without a shared secret.
- Key exchange in practice: asymmetric encryption is slow, so it is used to agree or send a symmetric session key securely (for example the client encrypts a random session key with the server's public key; only the server can decrypt it). Both then use the fast symmetric key for the rest of the session. This is what TLS (used by HTTPS) does.

#### Digital signatures

- Purpose: prove who sent a message (authentication), that it has not been changed (integrity), and that the sender cannot deny sending it (non-repudiation).
- Creating one: the sender hashes the message to get a digest, then encrypts the digest with their own private key. That encrypted digest is the signature, sent with the message.
- Checking one: the receiver decrypts the signature with the sender's public key to get the original digest, hashes the received message themselves, and compares. If they match, the message is from the holder of the private key and has not been altered.

#### Digital certificates

- Problem: how do you know a public key really belongs to the bank and not an attacker?
- A digital certificate is an electronic document issued by a trusted certificate authority (CA) that binds a public key to an identity (for example a website's domain). It contains the owner's name and domain, their public key, the CA's name, a serial number, validity dates, and the CA's digital signature.
- Obtaining one: the organisation generates a key pair and sends its public key and identity details to a CA; the CA checks the identity and then signs the certificate with the CA's private key.
- Using one: when a browser connects to a site over HTTPS, the server sends its certificate. The browser checks the CA's signature using the CA's public key (built into the browser or OS), that the certificate is in date and not revoked, and that the domain matches. If all is well, the browser trusts the server's public key (and shows a padlock).

#### Worms, trojans and viruses

| Malware | How it spreads | What it exploits |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Virus | Attaches itself to a host file or program; spreads when the infected file is run and copied or shared | Users running infected files; lack of scanning |
| Worm | Standalone; replicates itself across networks automatically, without a host file or user action | Unpatched software vulnerabilities (for example buffer overflows in network services), weak passwords, open ports |
| Trojan | Disguised as legitimate, useful software; the user installs it willingly; does not replicate | Social engineering: trusting users, downloads from unofficial sources |

- Effects: deleting or encrypting files (ransomware), stealing data, creating backdoors, using the computer in a botnet, slowing the network.
- Vulnerabilities exploited: software bugs (buffer overflows, injection), unpatched systems, default or weak passwords, users opening attachments and links, unnecessary open ports and services.

Addressing them:

- Improved code quality: secure coding practices (validate all input, check array bounds to prevent buffer overflows, use parameterised queries to prevent SQL injection), code reviews, testing (including penetration testing), and memory-safe languages, so there are fewer vulnerabilities to exploit.
- Monitoring: intrusion detection systems, firewall and network logs, checking for unusual traffic or behaviour, so attacks are spotted and stopped quickly.
- Protection: anti-malware software with up-to-date signatures and heuristic detection; installing security patches promptly; firewalls; least privilege (users and programs only have the access they need); user training about phishing and suspicious downloads; regular backups so data can be restored.

#### Hackers and penetration testing

*Refs: Eduqas C2.8: Malicious software and mechanisms of attack and defence.*

- Black hat hacking: breaking into systems without permission for malicious or criminal purposes (theft, damage). Illegal (Computer Misuse Act).
- White hat hacking (ethical hacking): security experts who, with the owner's permission, look for weaknesses so they can be fixed.
- Penetration testing: an authorised, simulated attack on a system to find vulnerabilities before criminals do. It can be external (attacking from outside like an outsider) or internal (simulating a malicious insider), and black box (testers have no inside knowledge) or white box (testers are given details of the system). Results are reported with recommended fixes.

### Common mistakes

- Saying a digital signature encrypts the whole message. It encrypts a hash (digest) of it with the sender's private key.
- Saying the CA "encrypts the website". The CA vouches for the link between a public key and an identity.
- Describing a trojan as self-replicating. Viruses and worms replicate; trojans do not.
- Saying a proxy server is the same as a firewall. A proxy can be part of a firewall setup but acts as an intermediary for requests.

### Check questions

1. Explain how packet switching is used to send an email across the internet. [4 marks]
2. List four items in a packet header. [2 marks]
3. Compare packet switching and circuit switching. [4 marks]
4. Explain the difference between a router and a gateway. [2 marks]
5. For the URL https://shop.example.com/basket/view, identify the protocol, the FQDN, the domain name and the path. [4 marks]
6. Describe how DNS resolves a domain name into an IP address. [4 marks]
7. Explain why internet registries are needed. [2 marks]
8. Compare packet filtering and stateful inspection. [3 marks]
9. Explain two ways a proxy server improves security or performance. [2 marks]
10. Explain how asymmetric encryption allows a symmetric key to be exchanged securely. [3 marks]
11. Describe how a digital signature is created and verified. [5 marks]
12. Explain how a browser uses a digital certificate to trust a website's public key. [3 marks]
13. Compare a worm with a virus, and explain how improved code quality reduces the threat from worms. [4 marks]
14. (Eduqas) Explain the purpose of penetration testing and the difference between white hat and black hat hacking. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Email split into packets (1); each packet given a header with source/destination address and sequence number (1); packets routed independently by routers, possibly by different routes (1); reassembled in order at the destination, missing packets re-requested (1).
2. Any four: source IP, destination IP, sequence number, protocol, time to live, packet length/total packets (2 for four, 1 for two).
3. Packet switching: data split into packets sent independently, links shared, no setup, may arrive out of order (2). Circuit switching: dedicated path set up for the whole communication, data in order with constant delay, link reserved even when idle (2).
4. Router forwards packets between networks using the same protocol, choosing the next hop (1); gateway connects networks with different protocols, translating between them (1).
5. Protocol https (1); FQDN shop.example.com (1); domain name example.com (1); path /basket/view (1).
6. Checks local/browser cache first (1); request sent to DNS resolver (1); resolver queries root, then TLD server, then authoritative server for the domain (1); IP address returned to the browser and cached (1).
7. IP addresses and domain names must be unique worldwide so data can be delivered (1); registries allocate and record them/share out scarce IPv4 addresses (1).
8. Packet filtering checks header details (IP addresses, ports, protocol) of each packet individually against rules (1); stateful inspection tracks connections and checks packets belong to a valid established conversation (1); stateful is more secure but slower/more processing (1).
9. Any two: hides internal IP addresses (1); filters content/blocks sites/scans for malware (1); caches pages to reduce bandwidth and speed access (1); logs use (1).
10. Client encrypts a random symmetric session key with the server's public key (1); only the server's private key can decrypt it, so an interceptor cannot read it (1); both then use the fast symmetric key for the session (1).
11. Sender hashes the message to produce a digest (1); encrypts the digest with the sender's private key to form the signature (1). Receiver decrypts the signature with the sender's public key (1); hashes the received message (1); if the digests match, the message is authentic and unaltered (1).
12. Server sends its certificate containing its public key, signed by a CA (1); browser verifies the CA's signature using the CA's public key built into the browser (1); checks dates/revocation/domain match, then trusts the public key (1).
13. Worm is standalone and spreads across networks by itself; virus needs a host file and user action to spread (2). Secure coding/input validation/bounds checking/testing removes vulnerabilities such as buffer overflows that worms exploit (2).
14. Authorised simulated attack to find vulnerabilities so they can be fixed (1). White hat: permission, aims to improve security (1). Black hat: no permission, malicious/criminal (1).

## TCP/IP, addressing and the client-server model

This file covers the four layers of the TCP/IP stack and why protocols are layered, sockets, ports and MAC addresses, TCP and UDP, application-layer protocols (HTTP, HTTPS, FTP, SMTP, POP3, IMAP, SSH, DHCP), web, email and FTP servers and browsers, IP address structure, subnet masks, IPv4 and IPv6, public and private addresses, DHCP, NAT, port forwarding, the client-server model, WebSocket, CRUD and REST, JSON and XML, and thin and thick clients.

Builds on [GCSE: Protocols and layers](../../gcse/computer-science/16-protocols-and-layers.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TCP/IP stack | Four layers; sockets; MAC addresses; well-known and client ports | TCP/IP stack; protocol layering | Typical packet contents using protocols such as TCP/IP |
| Protocols | FTP, HTTP, HTTPS, POP3, SMTP, SSH; email, web servers, browsers | (DNS, file 28) | HTTP, FTP, SMTP, TCP/IP, IMAP, DHCP, UDP, wireless protocols |
| Addressing | IP structure, subnets, v4 and v6, public and private, DHCP, NAT, port forwarding | Not named | DHCP |
| Web apps | Client-server, WebSocket, CRUD and REST, JSON vs XML, thin vs thick client | Client-server (1.3.3(d)); web technologies in file 35 | Not named |

### 4.9.4.1 TCP/IP

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.1 · OCR 1.3.3(a) · Eduqas C2.1: Networking, C2.2: Communication networks.*

#### What you need to know

- Protocol layering: network communication is split into layers, each with its own protocols and job, and each using the services of the layer below. Benefits: each layer can be developed, changed or replaced independently (Wi-Fi can replace Ethernet without changing HTTP); problems are easier to isolate; it is easier to standardise, so equipment and software from different makers work together.
- The four layers of the TCP/IP stack (sending goes down the stack; receiving goes up):
  1. Application layer: the protocols programs use to exchange data (HTTP, FTP, SMTP, DNS). Decides the format and meaning of the messages.
  2. Transport layer: sets up end-to-end communication between the two programs. TCP splits data into segments, numbers them, adds source and destination port numbers, and makes sure they arrive: acknowledgements, retransmission of lost segments, putting segments back in order, and flow control. UDP also adds ports but gives no guarantees: faster, used for streaming, gaming, DNS look-ups and VoIP, where speed matters more than every packet.
  3. Network (internet) layer: adds source and destination IP addresses to make packets; routers work at this layer to route packets across networks (IP).
  4. Link layer: sends the data across the physical network, adding source and destination MAC addresses for the next hop to make frames, and converting them into signals (Ethernet, Wi-Fi).
- Each layer adds its own header (encapsulation); the receiver's matching layer removes it.
- MAC (Media Access Control) address: a unique 48-bit address burned into each network interface card by the maker, written as 6 hex pairs (for example 3C:22:FB:9A:01:7E). Used by the link layer to deliver frames on the local network. MAC addresses change on every hop (each router addresses the frame to the next device); IP addresses stay the same end to end.
- Port: a 16-bit number identifying a particular application or service on a host, so arriving data reaches the right program.
  - Well-known ports (0 to 1023) are assigned to standard server services: HTTP 80, HTTPS 443, FTP 20 and 21, SSH 22, SMTP 25, POP3 110, IMAP 143, DNS 53.
  - Client (ephemeral) ports (usually 49152 and above) are picked temporarily by the client's OS for one connection, so replies come back to the right program and several connections can be open at once.
- Socket: the endpoint of a connection, IP address plus port, such as 192.168.1.5:51034. A connection is identified by a pair of sockets (client and server). Programs use sockets (through an API) to send and receive data via the transport layer.
- TCP three-way handshake (Eduqas handshaking): SYN (client asks to connect), SYN-ACK (server agrees), ACK (client confirms). Then data flows.

#### Worked example

Requesting a web page: the browser (application) creates an HTTP GET request; TCP (transport) puts it in a segment from client port 51034 to server port 443 (HTTPS); IP (network) adds source 86.12.4.9 and destination 151.101.0.81; Ethernet or Wi-Fi (link) adds the MAC address of the home router and sends the frame. Each router along the way strips the link header, reads the IP address, and adds a new link header for the next hop.

### 4.9.4.2 Standard application layer protocols

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.2 · Eduqas C2.1: Networking.*

#### What you need to know

| Protocol | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| HTTP | Requesting and sending web pages and resources between browser and web server |
| HTTPS | HTTP over an encrypted TLS connection: confidentiality, integrity and server authentication using certificates |
| FTP | Transferring files between a client and a server |
| SMTP | Sending email from a client to a mail server, and between mail servers |
| POP3 | Retrieving email from a server; usually downloads and deletes it from the server, so mail lives on one device |
| IMAP (Eduqas) | Retrieving email while keeping it on the server, synchronised across several devices |
| SSH | Secure, encrypted remote login and command execution on another computer |
| DHCP (Eduqas) | Automatically giving devices IP addresses and settings (below) |
| UDP (Eduqas) | Connectionless transport protocol (above) |

- FTP access: anonymous access lets anyone download public files with no account (username "anonymous"); non-anonymous access needs a username and password and lets users upload and manage files. An FTP client program connects to an FTP server to list, upload and download files.
- SSH for remote management: an administrator uses an SSH client to log in securely to a remote server and run commands, as if at its keyboard; everything, including the password, is encrypted. An SSH client can also open a TCP connection to another port on the remote machine and send application-level commands directly, such as an HTTP `GET`, SMTP commands (HELO, MAIL FROM, RCPT TO, DATA) to send an email, or POP3 commands (USER, PASS, LIST, RETR) to retrieve one.
- Email server: stores mailboxes; receives incoming email by SMTP from other servers; sends outgoing email by SMTP; lets users retrieve mail with POP3 or IMAP.
- Web server: stores web pages and resources and responds to HTTP(S) requests by sending them as text (HTML, CSS, JavaScript) or files (images); may generate pages dynamically.
- Web browser: requests a page, receives the HTML, then requests the other resources the page refers to (images, stylesheets, scripts), and renders (displays) the page, running any JavaScript.

### 4.9.4.3 IP address structure

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.3.*

#### What you need to know

- An IP address is split into a network identifier (which network the device is on: routers use this) and a host identifier (which device on that network).
- An IPv4 address is 32 bits, written as four denary numbers 0 to 255 separated by dots (dotted decimal): 192.168.10.75.

### 4.9.4.4 Subnet masking

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.4.*

#### What you need to know

- A network can be divided into smaller subnets (for example one per department), which reduces broadcast traffic, improves security and makes management easier.
- A subnet mask shows which bits of the IP address are the network (and subnet) identifier: 1s for network bits, 0s for host bits. It can be written in dotted decimal (255.255.255.0) or as a prefix length (/24: the first 24 bits are network).
- IP address AND subnet mask gives the network identifier. Two devices are on the same subnet if they have the same network identifier; otherwise traffic between them goes through a router.

#### Worked example

IP address 192.168.10.75, mask 255.255.255.192 (/26).

- Last byte: 75 = 0100 1011; mask 1100 0000. AND: 0100 0000 = 64.
- Network identifier 192.168.10.64. Host part 00 1011 = 11.
- With 6 host bits: 2⁶ = 64 addresses per subnet, of which 62 are usable for hosts (the all-0s address is the network and all-1s is the broadcast address). This subnet runs from 192.168.10.64 to .127.
- Is 192.168.10.130 on the same subnet? 130 = 1000 0010 AND 1100 0000 = 1000 0000 = 128. Network 192.168.10.128: different, so a router is needed.

### 4.9.4.5 IP standards

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.5.*

#### What you need to know

- IPv4: 32-bit addresses: about 4.3 billion (2³²). Not enough for every device in the world.
- IPv6: 128-bit addresses, written as eight groups of four hex digits separated by colons (2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334, shortened by dropping leading zeros and replacing one run of zero groups with ::). About 3.4 × 10³⁸ addresses.
- Why IPv6 was introduced: IPv4 addresses ran out because of the growth in internet users and connected devices (phones, Internet of Things). IPv6 also simplifies headers for faster routing, removes the need for NAT, and builds in support for security and auto-configuration.

### 4.9.4.6 Public and private IP addresses

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.6.*

#### What you need to know

- Public (routable) addresses are unique across the whole internet and can be reached from anywhere; they are allocated by registries through ISPs.
- Private (non-routable) addresses are reserved for use inside local networks and are not routed on the internet, so the same private addresses can be reused in millions of networks: 10.0.0.0 to 10.255.255.255, 172.16.0.0 to 172.31.255.255, 192.168.0.0 to 192.168.255.255. Devices with private addresses reach the internet through NAT.

### 4.9.4.7 Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP)

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.7 · Eduqas C2.1: Networking.*

#### What you need to know

- DHCP automatically assigns an IP address (and the subnet mask, default gateway and DNS server addresses) to a device when it joins a network, for a limited time (a lease).
- Process (DORA): the new device broadcasts a DHCP Discover; the DHCP server replies with an Offer of an address; the device sends a Request for it; the server sends an Acknowledgement. When the lease ends, the address returns to the pool unless renewed.
- Benefits: no manual configuration; no duplicate addresses; addresses are reused efficiently as devices come and go. Servers and printers often have fixed (static) addresses instead.

### 4.9.4.8 Network Address Translation (NAT)

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.8.*

#### What you need to know

- NAT lets many devices with private IP addresses share one public IP address. The router replaces each outgoing packet's private source address (and port) with its own public address (and a port it chooses), and records the mapping in a translation table. Replies arriving at that public address and port are translated back and sent to the right private device.
- Why it is used: it saves scarce IPv4 public addresses; it hides the internal network structure (outside devices cannot start connections to internal ones directly), adding some security.

### 4.9.4.9 Port forwarding

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.9.*

#### What you need to know

- Because NAT blocks unsolicited incoming connections, a server inside a private network (a game server, home web server or security camera) cannot normally be reached from outside.
- Port forwarding sets a rule on the router: incoming traffic on a given public port is forwarded to a specific private IP address and port. For example, traffic to the router's public address on port 8080 is sent to 192.168.1.20 port 80.
- It allows outside clients to reach the service, but opens a route through the router, so the service must be kept secure.

### 4.9.4.10 Client server model

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.10 · OCR 1.3.3(d).*

#### What you need to know

- In the client-server model, the client sends a request message to the server; the server processes it and replies with a response message. The server waits for requests from many clients.
- WebSocket: a protocol that sets up a persistent, full-duplex connection between a web browser and a server over TCP (starting as an HTTP request that is "upgraded"). Either side can send data at any time without a new request, with low overhead. Used where data must flow instantly both ways: chat apps, live sports scores, multiplayer browser games, collaborative editing, live dashboards and stock tickers. (Plain HTTP is request-response: the server cannot send unless asked, so polling would be needed.)
- Web CRUD applications and REST:
  - CRUD = Create, Retrieve (read), Update, Delete: the four basic operations on stored data.
  - REST (Representational State Transfer) is a style of API that uses HTTP request methods on resources identified by URLs. A REST API runs on the server; JavaScript in the browser (referenced by the HTML page) calls the API over HTTP, and the server talks to the database.
  - Mapping: HTTP GET → SQL SELECT (retrieve); POST → INSERT (create); PUT → UPDATE; DELETE → DELETE.
  - Data is sent between server and web app as JSON or XML.

#### Worked example

A REST API for a library:

| HTTP request | Meaning | SQL on the server |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GET /books/42 | Retrieve book 42 | SELECT * FROM Book WHERE BookID = 42 |
| POST /books (data in body) | Create a new book | INSERT INTO Book … |
| PUT /books/42 (data in body) | Update book 42 | UPDATE Book SET … WHERE BookID = 42 |
| DELETE /books/42 | Delete book 42 | DELETE FROM Book WHERE BookID = 42 |

The same book in JSON and XML:

```json
{"id": 42, "title": "Dune", "author": "Herbert", "available": true}
```

```xml
<book>
  <id>42</id>
  <title>Dune</title>
  <author>Herbert</author>
  <available>true</available>
</book>
```

- JSON compared with XML: easier for a human to read; more compact (no closing tags), so less data to send; easier to create; easier and so quicker for computers to parse (it maps directly onto JavaScript objects). XML is more verbose but supports schemas for validation, attributes and namespaces.

```python
import json
book = json.loads('{"id": 42, "title": "Dune", "available": true}')
print(book["title"])     # Dune
```

### 4.9.4.11 Thin- versus thick-client computing

*Refs: AQA 4.9.4.11.*

#### What you need to know

| | Thin client | Thick (fat) client |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Where processing happens | Mostly on the server; the client mainly displays and sends input | Mostly on the client, which has its own processing and storage |
| Client hardware | Cheap, low-power | Powerful, more expensive |
| Management | Central: software installed, updated and backed up once on the server; easier security | Each machine needs installing, updating and securing |
| Network | Depends heavily on the network and server; fails if they fail; heavy traffic | Can work offline; less traffic |
| Server | Needs powerful servers | Server load is lighter |
| Examples | Web apps, cloud desktops, school terminals | Desktop games, video editing, CAD workstations |

### Common mistakes

- Putting routing in the transport layer. Routing uses IP at the network layer.
- Saying MAC addresses are used to deliver packets across the internet. They are used only hop by hop on each local link.
- Saying NAT gives each device a public address. NAT shares one public address among many devices.
- Mapping POST to UPDATE. POST creates (INSERT); PUT updates.

### Check questions

1. Describe the role of each of the four layers of the TCP/IP stack. [4 marks]
2. Give two advantages of layering network protocols. [2 marks]
3. Explain what a socket is and why client ports are needed. [3 marks]
4. Explain the difference between a MAC address and an IP address, and when each is used. [3 marks]
5. Explain why UDP rather than TCP is used for live video calls. [2 marks]
6. Explain the difference between POP3 and IMAP. [2 marks]
7. Describe how SSH could be used by an administrator to manage a remote server. [2 marks]
8. A device has IP address 172.16.45.200 with mask 255.255.240.0. Work out the network identifier. [3 marks]
9. Give two reasons why IPv6 was introduced. [2 marks]
10. Explain how NAT allows a household of devices to share one public IP address. [3 marks]
11. Explain why port forwarding would be needed to host a game server at home. [2 marks]
12. Describe the DHCP process when a laptop joins a network. [3 marks]
13. Explain what WebSocket provides and give a suitable use. [3 marks]
14. A web app allows users to add, view, change and remove reviews. Map each action to an HTTP method and SQL command. [4 marks]
15. Give three reasons JSON is often preferred to XML. [3 marks]
16. A school is choosing between thin clients and thick clients for 200 computers. Discuss. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Application: protocols used by programs to format/exchange data, e.g. HTTP (1). Transport: splits data into segments with port numbers; TCP ensures reliable, ordered delivery (1). Network: adds IP addresses and routes packets across networks (1). Link: transmits frames over the physical network using MAC addresses (1).
2. Any two: layers developed/changed independently (1); easier to find faults (1); standards let different makers' equipment interoperate (1).
3. An endpoint made of an IP address and a port number (1). Client ports are temporary ports assigned by the client's OS (1) so that responses reach the right program and several connections can be open at once (1).
4. MAC is a fixed hardware address of a network interface; IP is a logical address assigned by the network (1). MAC is used to deliver frames on the local link/each hop (1); IP is used to route packets end to end across networks (1).
5. UDP has no acknowledgement/retransmission, so lower latency (1); a few lost packets matter less than delay in live video (1).
6. POP3 downloads email to one device and usually deletes it from the server (1); IMAP keeps email on the server and synchronises across devices (1).
7. SSH client makes an encrypted connection to the server (1); administrator logs in and runs commands remotely as if local (1).
8. Third byte: 45 = 0010 1101 AND 240 = 1111 0000 → 0010 0000 = 32 (1); fourth byte AND 0 = 0 (1); network 172.16.32.0 (1).
9. Any two: IPv4 addresses ran out (1); growth of devices/IoT needs many more addresses (1); removes need for NAT/simplified headers/built-in security (1).
10. Devices use private addresses inside the home (1); the router replaces the private source address/port with its public address and a port, recording the mapping (1); replies are translated back using the table to the correct device (1).
11. NAT blocks incoming connections not started from inside (1); port forwarding tells the router to send traffic on the game's port to the server's private IP address (1).
12. Laptop broadcasts a discover message (1); DHCP server offers an IP address/settings (1); laptop requests it and server acknowledges, leasing it for a time (1).
13. A persistent, full-duplex connection between browser and server over TCP (1); either side can send at any time without new requests, low overhead (1); use: chat/live scores/online game/collaborative editor (1).
14. Add: POST → INSERT (1). View: GET → SELECT (1). Change: PUT → UPDATE (1). Remove: DELETE → DELETE (1).
15. Any three: easier for humans to read (1); more compact (1); easier to create (1); quicker/easier for computers to parse (1).
16. Thin: cheaper clients, central management/updates/security, but needs powerful servers and network; single point of failure (2). Thick: works offline, less network load, suits demanding software, but more costly and harder to manage (1). Justified recommendation (1).
