## Databases, normalisation and SQL

This file covers flat file and relational databases, entities, attributes and relationships, entity relationship diagrams, primary, composite, foreign and secondary keys and indexes, consistency, redundancy and independence, normalisation to third normal form, SQL to define tables and to select, insert, update and delete across several tables, referential integrity, the DBMS and data dictionary, user views, validation and verification, capturing and exchanging data, client-server databases, concurrent access, record locking, serialisation, timestamp and commitment ordering, transactions and ACID.

Builds on [GCSE: Databases and SQL](../../gcse/computer-science/18-databases-and-sql.md) (tables, records, fields, primary and foreign keys, simple SELECT). New here: ER modelling, normalisation, joins and subqueries, DDL, and concurrency.

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Modelling | ER diagrams; Entity(Attribute1, …) notation | ER modelling | ER modelling; relationship symbols |
| Keys | Attribute, primary, composite primary, foreign | Primary, foreign, secondary; indexing | Primary, foreign; indexes |
| Normalisation | To 3NF; why | To 3NF | 1NF, 2NF, 3NF; restructure to 3NF |
| SQL | Retrieve, update, insert, delete across tables; CREATE TABLE | Interpret and modify SQL | CREATE TABLE, PRIMARY KEY, NOT NULL, Int, Char(n), Numeric(m,n), DateTime, INSERT, SELECT (with WHERE, IN, AND, OR, ORDER BY, GROUP BY), UPDATE, comparison operators, subqueries |
| Integrity | Concurrent access: record locks, serialisation, timestamp and commitment ordering | Referential integrity; transaction processing, ACID, record locking, redundancy | Consistency, redundancy, independence; validation and verification |
| Other | Client-server databases | Capturing, selecting, managing and exchanging data | DBMS and data dictionaries; user views; other contemporary database systems |

### Relational databases and keys

*Refs: AQA 4.10.2 · OCR 1.3.2(a) · Eduqas C2.5: Databases.*

#### What you need to know

- A flat file database stores all data in one table. Simple, but data is repeated (redundancy), which wastes space and causes inconsistency when one copy is updated but not others.
- A relational database stores data in several linked tables (relations), each about one entity, linked by keys. Each row is a record (tuple) and each column an attribute (field).
- Entity: a thing about which data is stored (Student, Course). Attribute: a property of an entity (Surname).
- Primary key: an attribute that uniquely identifies each record in a table.
- Composite primary key: a primary key made of two or more attributes together, used when no single attribute is unique (for example StudentID + CourseID in an Enrolment table).
- Foreign key: an attribute in one table that is the primary key of another table, creating a link (relationship) between them.
- Secondary key (OCR): an attribute other than the primary key that is indexed so records can be searched or sorted quickly by it (for example Surname).
- Index: a separate structure (often a B-tree or hash) of key values and record locations, so the DBMS can find records without scanning the whole table. Speeds up searching but uses space and slows inserts and updates.
- Eduqas:
  - data consistency: every copy of a data item has the same value;
  - data redundancy: unnecessary repetition of data;
  - data independence: the way data is stored can be changed without changing the programs that use it (the DBMS separates the logical view from the physical storage).
- Relational databases: flexible queries, little redundancy, strong integrity rules, mature and widely used; but they need a fixed schema and do not scale easily across many servers or handle unstructured data well. Other contemporary systems (Eduqas): NoSQL databases (document stores, key-value stores, graph databases) handle huge, unstructured or rapidly changing data and scale across many machines, with less strict consistency (see file 31).

### 4.10.1 Conceptual data models and entity relationship modelling

*Refs: AQA 4.10.1 · OCR 1.3.2(a) · Eduqas C2.5: Databases.*

#### What you need to know

- A data model describes the entities, their attributes and the relationships between them.
- Entity description notation (AQA): Entity(Attribute1, Attribute2, …), with the primary key underlined (in plain text, often marked another way, such as an asterisk or bold).
- Relationship degrees: one-to-one, one-to-many, many-to-many. On an ER diagram, entities are rectangles and relationships are lines; the "many" end is shown with a crow's foot.
- A many-to-many relationship cannot be implemented directly in a relational database: it is resolved with a link (junction) entity holding the foreign keys of both, giving two one-to-many relationships.

#### Worked example

A college: each student can enrol on many courses; each course has many students; each course is taught by one tutor; a tutor teaches many courses.

```
Tutor ──────< Course >────── Student          (Course–Student is many-to-many)

resolved:

Tutor ──────< Course ──────< Enrolment >────── Student
```

Entity descriptions:

- Tutor(<u>TutorID</u>, TutorName, Email)
- Course(<u>CourseID</u>, Title, TutorID)
- Student(<u>StudentID</u>, Forename, Surname, DateOfBirth)
- Enrolment(<u>StudentID, CourseID</u>, EnrolDate, Grade)

TutorID in Course is a foreign key; Enrolment has a composite primary key made of two foreign keys.

### 4.10.3 Database design and normalisation

*Refs: AQA 4.10.3 · OCR 1.3.2(c) · Eduqas C2.5: Databases.*

#### What you need to know

- Normalisation is the process of organising data into tables to remove redundancy and the problems it causes (update, insertion and deletion anomalies).
- First normal form (1NF): every attribute holds a single (atomic) value; there are no repeating groups of attributes; every record is unique (has a primary key).
- Second normal form (2NF): in 1NF, and there are no partial dependencies: every non-key attribute depends on the whole primary key, not just part of it. (Only possible to break when the key is composite.)
- Third normal form (3NF): in 2NF, and there are no non-key dependencies (transitive dependencies): every non-key attribute depends on the key and on nothing but the key. Summary: "the key, the whole key and nothing but the key".
- Why normalise:
  - less redundancy, so less storage and no inconsistent copies;
  - no update anomalies (change a value once, not in many rows);
  - no insertion anomalies (you can add a tutor before they have a course);
  - no deletion anomalies (deleting a course does not lose the only record of a tutor);
  - easier to maintain; data integrity is enforced.
- Drawback: more tables means queries need joins, which can be slower; very busy reporting systems sometimes denormalise for speed.

#### Worked example

Unnormalised data (one row per order, with repeating products):

| OrderID | OrderDate | CustID | CustName | CustTown | Products (ProductID, Desc, Price, Qty) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 501 | 03/02 | C7 | Patel | Leeds | P1 Pen 0.50 ×10; P4 Pad 2.00 ×2 |
| 502 | 04/02 | C9 | Ng | York | P1 Pen 0.50 ×5 |

1NF: remove the repeating group so each row holds one product. Primary key becomes (OrderID, ProductID).

OrderLine1NF(<u>OrderID, ProductID</u>, OrderDate, CustID, CustName, CustTown, Desc, Price, Qty)

2NF: remove partial dependencies on part of the composite key.

- OrderDate, CustID, CustName, CustTown depend only on OrderID.
- Desc and Price depend only on ProductID.
- Qty depends on both.

Order(<u>OrderID</u>, OrderDate, CustID, CustName, CustTown)
Product(<u>ProductID</u>, Desc, Price)
OrderLine(<u>OrderID, ProductID</u>, Qty)

3NF: remove non-key dependencies. In Order, CustName and CustTown depend on CustID, which is not the key.

Customer(<u>CustID</u>, CustName, CustTown)
Order(<u>OrderID</u>, OrderDate, CustID)
Product(<u>ProductID</u>, Desc, Price)
OrderLine(<u>OrderID, ProductID</u>, Qty)

Now a customer's town is stored once; a product's price changes in one place.

### 4.10.4 Structured Query Language (SQL)

*Refs: AQA 4.10.4 · OCR 1.3.2(d) · Eduqas C2.5: Searching data.*

#### What you need to know

- SQL is a declarative query language: you say what data you want, and the DBMS works out how to get it. Query languages let users and programs retrieve and change data without knowing how it is stored.
- Data definition (DDL): CREATE TABLE, with data types and constraints (PRIMARY KEY, NOT NULL, FOREIGN KEY … REFERENCES).
- Data manipulation (DML): SELECT, INSERT INTO … VALUES, UPDATE … SET, DELETE FROM.
- SELECT clauses: WHERE (conditions with =, <>, <, >, <=, >=, AND, OR, NOT, IN, BETWEEN, LIKE with wildcards), ORDER BY (ASC/DESC), GROUP BY with aggregate functions (COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX) and HAVING to filter groups, JOIN (or listing tables in FROM with a WHERE condition linking keys), and subqueries (a SELECT inside another, in brackets).
- Eduqas data types: Int, Char(n) (fixed-length text of n characters), Numeric(m,n) (m digits, n after the decimal point), DateTime. Other systems use VARCHAR(n), INTEGER, REAL, DATE, BOOLEAN.

#### Worked example

Using the 3NF tables above:

```sql
CREATE TABLE Customer (
    CustID    CHAR(4)      NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    CustName  VARCHAR(30)  NOT NULL,
    CustTown  VARCHAR(30)
);

CREATE TABLE Orders (
    OrderID   INT          NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    OrderDate DATETIME     NOT NULL,
    CustID    CHAR(4)      NOT NULL,
    FOREIGN KEY (CustID) REFERENCES Customer(CustID)
);

CREATE TABLE Product (
    ProductID CHAR(4)      NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    Descr     VARCHAR(40),
    Price     NUMERIC(6,2)
);

CREATE TABLE OrderLine (
    OrderID   INT     NOT NULL,
    ProductID CHAR(4) NOT NULL,
    Qty       INT     NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (OrderID, ProductID),
    FOREIGN KEY (OrderID) REFERENCES Orders(OrderID),
    FOREIGN KEY (ProductID) REFERENCES Product(ProductID)
);
```

(`Orders` is used because ORDER is a reserved word in SQL.)

Insert, update and delete:

```sql
INSERT INTO Customer (CustID, CustName, CustTown) VALUES ('C12', 'Ahmed', 'Hull');

UPDATE Product SET Price = 0.55 WHERE ProductID = 'P1';

DELETE FROM OrderLine WHERE OrderID = 502 AND ProductID = 'P1';
```

Retrieve from several tables: names of customers who ordered pens, alphabetically.

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT Customer.CustName
FROM Customer
JOIN Orders    ON Customer.CustID = Orders.CustID
JOIN OrderLine ON Orders.OrderID = OrderLine.OrderID
JOIN Product   ON OrderLine.ProductID = Product.ProductID
WHERE Product.Descr = 'Pen'
ORDER BY Customer.CustName ASC;
```

The same join in the older style:

```sql
SELECT Customer.CustName
FROM Customer, Orders
WHERE Customer.CustID = Orders.CustID AND Orders.OrderDate >= '2025-02-01';
```

Total value of each order, highest first:

```sql
SELECT OrderLine.OrderID, SUM(OrderLine.Qty * Product.Price) AS Total
FROM OrderLine JOIN Product ON OrderLine.ProductID = Product.ProductID
GROUP BY OrderLine.OrderID
ORDER BY Total DESC;
```

Subquery and IN: products never ordered.

```sql
SELECT Descr FROM Product
WHERE ProductID NOT IN (SELECT ProductID FROM OrderLine);
```

Customers from Leeds or York: `SELECT * FROM Customer WHERE CustTown IN ('Leeds', 'York');`

### Referential integrity

*Refs: OCR 1.3.2(e).*

#### What you need to know

- Referential integrity means every foreign key value must match an existing primary key value in the linked table (or be null if allowed), so there are no "orphan" records.
- The DBMS enforces it by refusing to: add an Orders record with a CustID that is not in Customer; delete a Customer who still has orders (or by cascading the delete, removing their orders too, or setting the foreign keys to null, if the designer chooses).

### DBMS, data dictionary and user views

*Refs: Eduqas C2.5: Database management systems, Databases.*

#### What you need to know

- A database management system (DBMS) is software that creates, maintains and controls access to a database. It provides: data definition and querying (SQL); data independence (programs do not need to know storage details); integrity (enforcing keys, data types, validation and referential integrity); security (user accounts, permissions); concurrency control; backup and recovery; and the data dictionary.
- A data dictionary (catalogue, metadata) describes the database: table names, field names, data types, sizes, validation rules, keys, relationships, indexes and access rights.
- Views: different users see different views of the same data (a virtual table defined by a query). A receptionist sees patients' names and appointments but not medical notes; a doctor sees more. Advantages: security and privacy (users only see what they need), simplicity (users see a tailored, uncluttered view), and the underlying structure can change without changing users' views.
- Turning data into useful information: queries, sorting, filtering, grouping, calculations and reports.

### Validation and verification

*Refs: Eduqas C2.5: Data validation and verification, C1.3: Validation and verification.*

#### What you need to know

- Validation: automatic checks that data is sensible and follows rules before it is accepted. It cannot check it is correct. Types:
  - presence check (not left blank);
  - type check (a number where a number is needed);
  - range check (mark between 0 and 100);
  - length check (password at least 8 characters);
  - format (picture) check (postcode pattern, date dd/mm/yyyy);
  - lookup (list) check (value is one of an allowed set);
  - check digit (file 18);
  - in databases also: uniqueness (primary key), referential integrity, and cross-field checks (end date after start date).
- Verification: checking that data has been entered or copied accurately, matching the original:
  - double entry (type twice and compare, as with passwords);
  - visual check / proofreading against the source document;
  - in transmission: parity and checksums.

```python
import re
def valid_mark(text):
    if text.strip() == "":                 # presence
        return False
    if not text.isdigit():                 # type
        return False
    return 0 <= int(text) <= 100           # range

def valid_postcode(text):                  # format check (simplified UK pattern)
    return re.fullmatch(r"[A-Z]{1,2}[0-9][A-Z0-9]? ?[0-9][A-Z]{2}", text) is not None
```

### Capturing, selecting, managing and exchanging data

*Refs: OCR 1.3.2(b).*

#### What you need to know

- Capturing: getting data into the system: forms (paper or online), OCR (optical character recognition) and OMR (optical mark recognition, for multiple-choice answer sheets), barcode and RFID scanners, sensors, and importing from other systems. Validation at entry keeps data clean.
- Selecting: choosing only the relevant data, for example with SQL queries and filters.
- Managing: using a DBMS to update, sort, back up, secure and keep data accurate; SQL for manipulation.
- Exchanging: moving data between systems: EDI (electronic data interchange, standard formats for business documents such as orders and invoices between companies), CSV files, JSON or XML through APIs (file 29). Using a standard format both systems understand is essential.

### 4.10.5 Client server databases, concurrency and transactions

*Refs: AQA 4.10.5 · OCR 1.3.2(f).*

#### What you need to know

- A client-server database: the database and DBMS run on a server; many clients send requests (queries) to it at the same time. The server processes them and returns results. Benefits: one consistent, central copy; central security and backup; clients need little processing.
- Concurrent access problem: the lost update. Two clients read the same record, both change it, and both write back; the first update is overwritten and lost. Example: stock is 10; A reads 10 and sells 3; B reads 10 and sells 2; A writes 7; B writes 8. Correct stock is 5.
- Ways to control concurrent access:
  - Record locking: when a client accesses a record to update it, the record is locked; other clients cannot change it (or sometimes read it) until the lock is released. Risk: deadlock, where two clients each wait for a record the other has locked; the DBMS must detect this and abort one.
  - Serialisation: transactions are scheduled so the result is the same as if they had run one after another, never interleaved in a way that interferes.
  - Timestamp ordering: each transaction gets a timestamp when it starts; each record stores the timestamps of the last read and write. If a transaction tries to change a record that a later transaction has already read or written, it is rejected and restarted, so conflicting operations happen in timestamp order.
  - Commitment ordering: transactions are committed in an order that respects their conflicts (dependencies), so the overall effect is serialisable; a transaction that would break the order is aborted.
- A transaction is a single logical unit of work made of one or more operations (for example a bank transfer: debit one account and credit another). It must be done completely or not at all.
- ACID (OCR):
  - Atomicity: a transaction is all or nothing; if any part fails, the whole thing is rolled back.
  - Consistency: a transaction takes the database from one valid state to another, keeping all rules (such as referential integrity and "total money unchanged by a transfer").
  - Isolation: concurrent transactions do not interfere; the result is as if they ran one at a time.
  - Durability: once committed, changes are permanent, surviving power cuts and crashes (written to non-volatile storage, with logs).
- Redundancy (OCR, in the sense of resilience): keeping duplicate copies of data or hardware (mirrored disks, backup servers in other locations) so the system keeps working and no data is lost if one part fails. (Contrast with data redundancy in normalisation, which is unwanted duplication.)

### Common mistakes

- Saying a foreign key must be unique. It usually is not: many orders share one CustID.
- Normalising to 3NF but leaving a many-to-many relationship unresolved.
- Writing SQL joins without the linking condition, which gives every combination of rows (a Cartesian product).
- Forgetting WHERE in UPDATE or DELETE, which changes or deletes every row.
- Using HAVING without GROUP BY, or WHERE to filter aggregate results.
- Confusing validation (is it sensible?) with verification (is it what was intended?).

### Check questions

1. Define the terms primary key, composite primary key and foreign key. [3 marks]
2. Give two problems with a flat file database that a relational database solves. [2 marks]
3. A hospital: each ward has many patients; each patient is on one ward; each patient can be seen by many doctors and each doctor sees many patients. Draw an ER diagram and write entity descriptions for a 3NF design. [6 marks]
4. State the conditions for a relation to be in third normal form. [3 marks]
5. Normalise to 3NF: Loan(BookID, Title, AuthorName, AuthorNationality, MemberID, MemberName, LoanDate), where each member can borrow many books and the key is (BookID, MemberID, LoanDate). [6 marks]
6. Write SQL to create a table Member with MemberID (primary key, 5 characters), Name (up to 40 characters, required) and JoinDate. [3 marks]
7. Using your tables from Q5, write SQL to list the titles of books borrowed by the member named 'Ruth Okafor', in date order. [4 marks]
8. Write SQL to increase the price of every product costing under £1 by 10%. [2 marks]
9. Write SQL to show how many orders each customer has made, only for customers with more than 3 orders. [3 marks]
10. (OCR) Explain referential integrity and how a DBMS enforces it. [3 marks]
11. Explain the lost update problem and how record locking prevents it. [4 marks]
12. Describe timestamp ordering. [2 marks]
13. (OCR) Explain the four ACID properties using a bank transfer as an example. [4 marks]
14. (Eduqas) Explain the advantages of giving different users different views of a database. [2 marks]
15. (Eduqas) Explain the difference between validation and verification, giving two examples of each. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Primary key: attribute that uniquely identifies each record (1). Composite: a primary key made of two or more attributes (1). Foreign key: attribute in one table that is the primary key in another, linking them (1).
2. Any two: removes redundant/repeated data (1); avoids inconsistency/update anomalies (1); saves storage (1); more flexible queries (1).
3. Diagram: Ward one-to-many Patient (1); Patient many-to-many Doctor resolved with a link entity, e.g. Consultation/Treatment (1). Descriptions: Ward(<u>WardID</u>, WardName, …) (1); Patient(<u>PatientID</u>, Name, …, WardID) (1); Doctor(<u>DoctorID</u>, Name, …) (1); Treatment(<u>PatientID, DoctorID</u>, …) with a date if needed in the key (1).
4. In 2NF (which needs 1NF: atomic values, no repeating groups) (1); every non-key attribute depends on the whole key (no partial dependencies) (1); no non-key attribute depends on another non-key attribute (no transitive dependencies) (1).
5. Identify partial dependencies: Title, AuthorName, AuthorNationality depend on BookID; MemberName on MemberID (2). Transitive: AuthorNationality depends on AuthorName (1). 3NF: Author(<u>AuthorID</u>, AuthorName, AuthorNationality) (1); Book(<u>BookID</u>, Title, AuthorID) (1); Member(<u>MemberID</u>, MemberName) and Loan(<u>BookID, MemberID, LoanDate</u>) (1).
6. `CREATE TABLE Member (MemberID CHAR(5) NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY, Name VARCHAR(40) NOT NULL, JoinDate DATE);` (1 for CREATE TABLE and fields, 1 for primary key, 1 for NOT NULL and types).
7. Example:

```sql
SELECT Book.Title
FROM Book JOIN Loan ON Book.BookID = Loan.BookID
          JOIN Member ON Loan.MemberID = Member.MemberID
WHERE Member.MemberName = 'Ruth Okafor'
ORDER BY Loan.LoanDate;
```

SELECT Title (1); correct joins (1); WHERE on name (1); ORDER BY LoanDate (1).
8. `UPDATE Product SET Price = Price * 1.1 WHERE Price < 1;` (1 for UPDATE … SET calculation, 1 for WHERE).
9. `SELECT CustID, COUNT(*) FROM Orders GROUP BY CustID HAVING COUNT(*) > 3;` (1 COUNT, 1 GROUP BY, 1 HAVING).
10. Every foreign key value must match an existing primary key in the linked table (1); DBMS refuses inserts with non-matching foreign keys (1); refuses or cascades deletes of records that are referenced (1).
11. Two users read the same record and both update it; the second write overwrites the first, so an update is lost (2). Record locking: the first user locks the record (1); the second cannot update until the lock is released and must reread the new value (1).
12. Each transaction is given a timestamp (1); a transaction is rejected/restarted if it tries to access a record already written/read by a later transaction, so operations happen in timestamp order (1).
13. Atomicity: debit and credit both happen or neither (1). Consistency: total money is the same before and after; rules kept (1). Isolation: another transaction cannot see the halfway state/two transfers do not interfere (1). Durability: once committed, the transfer survives a crash (1).
14. Security/privacy: users only see the data they need (1); simplicity: tailored view, easier to use; structure can change without affecting views (1).
15. Validation: automatic checks that data is reasonable/obeys rules (1), e.g. range check, presence check (1). Verification: checks that data matches the original/was entered accurately (1), e.g. double entry, visual check (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/31-big-data-and-distributed-systems.md -->

## Big Data and distributed systems

This file covers what Big Data is (volume, velocity, variety), why its lack of structure is the hardest part, why its processing must be distributed and why functional programming helps, the fact-based model and graph schemas, and (for Eduqas) predictive analytics, data warehousing, data mining, and distributed data and processing. Functional programming itself is in [32-functional-programming.md](32-functional-programming.md); relational databases in [30](30-databases-normalisation-and-sql.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Big Data | Volume, velocity, variety; unstructured data; machine learning; distributed processing and functional programming; fact-based model; graph schema | Data mining is a computational method (2.2.2, file 11) | Big Data, predictive analytics, data warehousing, data mining |
| Distribution | Processing across more than one machine | Not named | Distribution of data and processing; distributed databases and their advantages |

### 4.11.1 Big Data

*Refs: AQA 4.11.1 · Eduqas C2.5: Big Data.*

#### What you need to know

- "Big Data" is a catch-all term for data that will not fit the usual containers: it is too large, too fast or too varied to store and analyse with ordinary tools (a single server and a relational database). It is described by three Vs:
  - Volume: too big to fit on a single server (terabytes to petabytes and more).
  - Velocity: data arrives continuously as a stream and must be processed quickly, with responses needed within milliseconds to seconds (sensor networks, smartphones, video surveillance, mouse clicks, financial trades, social media).
  - Variety: data comes in many forms: structured (tables), semi-structured (JSON, logs), and unstructured (text, images, video, audio).
- The hardest part is often the lack of structure, not the size:
  - analysis is much more difficult;
  - relational databases are not appropriate, because they need data to fit rows and columns with a fixed schema;
  - machine learning techniques are needed to find patterns and extract useful information.
- Size matters when data does not fit on one server, because relational databases do not scale well across multiple machines (joins and transactions across servers are slow and complex).
- So the processing must be distributed across many machines (a cluster), each working on part of the data in parallel, with the results combined (for example the MapReduce approach: map a function over each machine's share of the data, then reduce the partial results into one answer).
- Functional programming is a solution because it makes it easier to write correct and efficient distributed code:
  - immutable data structures: data is never changed in place, so several machines can work on the same data without one corrupting another's copy, and there is no need for locks;
  - statelessness: functions have no side effects and their result depends only on their inputs, so any machine can run any piece of work in any order, and a failed piece can simply be re-run elsewhere, giving the same answer;
  - higher-order functions such as map, filter and reduce (fold) express "do this to every item" and "combine the results", which split naturally across machines.
  These features make code easier to reason about and test (correct), and safe to split up (distributable).

#### Fact-based model and graph schema

- In a fact-based model, each fact captures a single piece of information, for example "user 17 lives in Leeds (recorded 2025-03-01)". Facts are never updated or deleted; new facts are added, each with a timestamp, so the full history is kept and data is immutable. Benefits: errors can be corrected by adding new facts and nothing is ever lost; queries can find the state at any time; it fits distributed, append-only storage.
- A graph schema captures the structure of the dataset as a graph:
  - nodes represent entities (a person, a page, a product);
  - edges represent relationships between entities (follows, likes, bought);
  - properties hold information about nodes (and sometimes edges), for example a person's name or age.
- Graph schemas are diagrams with nodes as ovals or circles, edges as labelled lines, and properties as rectangles attached to their node. They are flexible (new kinds of node or edge are easy to add) and suit highly connected data such as social networks.

#### Worked example

A video platform records billions of view events a day from phones, TVs and browsers (velocity), with comments, thumbnails and video files (variety), totalling petabytes (volume). A relational database on one server could not store or query it fast enough.

Fact-based records:

```
(user: 17, watched: video 902, at: 2025-11-04T19:02:11)
(user: 17, liked: video 902, at: 2025-11-04T19:10:40)
(user: 17, unliked: video 902, at: 2025-11-05T08:00:03)
```

The unlike does not delete the like; it is a new fact. The current state is worked out from all the facts.

Graph schema (in words): node User (properties name, joined); node Video (properties title, length); edges User –watched→ Video, User –liked→ Video, User –follows→ User.

Distributed count of views per video using map and reduce:

```python
from functools import reduce
from collections import Counter

## each machine has its own chunk of the log
chunks = [["v1", "v2", "v1"], ["v2", "v2"], ["v1", "v3"]]

## map step (runs on each machine independently, no shared state)
partials = list(map(Counter, chunks))

## reduce step (combine partial counts)
total = reduce(lambda a, b: a + b, partials)
print(total)   # Counter({'v2': 3, 'v1': 3, 'v3': 1})
```

### Predictive analytics, data warehousing and data mining

*Refs: Eduqas C2.5: Big Data.*

#### What you need to know

- Data warehousing: copying data from many separate operational systems (sales, stock, website, customer service) into one large central store, cleaned and organised for analysis rather than for day-to-day transactions. Data is usually historical, read-only and summarised by subject and time. Supports reports and decision making.
- Data mining: searching large data sets to find hidden patterns, relationships and trends, using statistics and machine learning: for example customers who buy nappies often also buy certain snacks; unusual card transactions that suggest fraud.
- Predictive analytics: using historical data, statistics and machine learning models to predict future events or behaviour: likely sales next month, which customers may leave, a patient's risk of illness, when a machine part will fail.
- Benefits: better decisions, targeted marketing, fraud detection, efficiency. Concerns: privacy and consent, bias in data and models, security of huge data stores, and the cost of storage and processing (see file 26).

### Distributed systems

*Refs: Eduqas C2.5: Distributed systems.*

#### What you need to know

- Distribution can apply to both data and processing:
  - distributed processing: a task is split across several computers that work in parallel and communicate over a network (clusters, grid computing, cloud services);
  - distributed data: data is stored across several sites or servers rather than one.
- A distributed database stores parts or copies of the database at several sites connected by a network, but appears to users as a single database. Data may be partitioned (each site holds part, often the part it uses most) or replicated (copies at several sites).
- Advantages: data is near the users who need it, so faster local access and less network traffic; more reliable (if one site fails, others carry on; replicas act as backups); scalable (add more servers); can reflect the structure of an organisation with branches.
- Disadvantages: more complex to design and manage; keeping replicas consistent is hard (updates must reach every copy); security must be enforced at every site; network failures can make some data unavailable.

### Common mistakes

- Defining Big Data only as "a lot of data". Velocity and variety, and the lack of structure, matter too.
- Saying functional programming is used "because it is faster". It is used because immutability and statelessness make distributed code easier to write correctly.
- Saying facts in a fact-based model are updated. They are immutable; new facts are added.

### Check questions

1. Describe the three Vs of Big Data, with an example of each. [6 marks]
2. Explain why relational databases are often unsuitable for Big Data. [3 marks]
3. Explain why machine learning is needed to analyse Big Data. [2 marks]
4. Explain three features of functional programming that make it suitable for processing Big Data across many servers. [6 marks]
5. Describe the fact-based model for representing data and one advantage of it. [3 marks]
6. Draw a graph schema for a music streaming service with users, songs and artists, showing nodes, edges and properties. [4 marks]
7. (Eduqas) Explain the difference between data warehousing and data mining. [2 marks]
8. (Eduqas) Give an example of predictive analytics and one concern about its use. [2 marks]
9. (Eduqas) Describe what a distributed database is and give two advantages and one disadvantage. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Volume: data too large for one server (1), e.g. years of video uploads (1). Velocity: data arriving as a fast stream needing quick response (1), e.g. sensor or click data (1). Variety: many forms, structured and unstructured (1), e.g. text, images and video together (1).
2. Need data to fit a fixed row-and-column schema, but Big Data is unstructured/varied (1); do not scale well across multiple machines (1); joins/transactions across servers are slow (1).
3. The data is too large and unstructured for people or simple queries to find patterns (1); machine learning can detect patterns and extract useful information automatically (1).
4. Immutable data: cannot be changed, so machines cannot corrupt each other's data/no locking needed (2). Stateless/no side effects: result depends only on inputs, so tasks can run anywhere, in any order, and be re-run if a machine fails (2). Higher-order functions: map/filter/reduce split work across machines and combine results (2).
5. Each fact records a single piece of information, usually with a timestamp (1); facts are immutable: never changed or deleted, only added (1); advantage: full history kept/errors corrected without data loss/suits distributed storage (1).
6. Nodes User, Song, Artist (1); edges such as User–listened to→Song, User–follows→Artist, Artist–performed→Song (1); properties on nodes such as name, title, duration (1); clear labelled diagram (1).
7. Data warehousing collects and stores data from many systems in one central store for analysis (1); data mining searches large data sets to find hidden patterns/relationships (1).
8. Example: predicting which customers will cancel/sales forecasts/patient risk (1); concern: privacy/bias/accuracy/consent (1).
9. Database stored across several sites/servers but appearing as one database (1). Advantages: faster local access; reliability if one site fails; scalability (2). Disadvantage: complexity/keeping copies consistent/security at many sites (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/32-functional-programming.md -->

## Functional programming

This file covers function types, domains and co-domains, functions as first-class objects, function application, partial application, composition, writing functional programs with higher-order functions (map, filter, reduce/fold), and list processing with head and tail. Examples are given in Haskell (a pure functional language AQA suggests) and in Python. Why functional programming suits Big Data is in [31](31-big-data-and-distributed-systems.md).

New at A-level.

#### Board differences

Only AQA 7517 examines this content in detail. OCR H446 and Eduqas A500QS name declarative or functional paradigms only as types of language ([02](02-stack-frames-recursion-and-paradigms.md)).

### Key ideas of the paradigm

- A functional program is built from functions that take inputs and return outputs, like mathematical functions.
- Pure functions have no side effects: they do not change global variables, input or output, or their arguments, and they always give the same result for the same inputs (referential transparency).
- Data is immutable: values are not changed after they are created; instead, new values are made.
- There are no loops with changing counters; repetition is done by recursion and higher-order functions.
- Benefits: easier to reason about and test (a function can be checked on its own), fewer bugs from shared state, and safe to run in parallel.

### 4.12.1.1 Function type

*Refs: AQA 4.12.1.1.*

#### What you need to know

- A function f has a function type f: A → B, where A is the argument type and B is the result type.
- A is the domain (the set the inputs are chosen from); B is the co-domain (the set the outputs are chosen from). Not every member of the co-domain has to be an output.
- The domain and co-domain are always subsets of objects of some data type.

#### Worked example

f: {a, b, c, …, z} → {0, 1, 2, …, 25}, mapping a ↦ 0, b ↦ 1 and so on. Domain: lower-case letters; co-domain: integers 0 to 25.

`isEven: integer → Boolean`. The co-domain is {True, False}.

In Haskell, types are written with `::`:

```haskell
letterPos :: Char -> Int
letterPos c = fromEnum c - fromEnum 'a'

isEven :: Int -> Bool
isEven n = n `mod` 2 == 0
```

### 4.12.1.2 First-class object

*Refs: AQA 4.12.1.2.*

#### What you need to know

- A first-class object (value) can: appear in expressions, be assigned to a variable, be passed as an argument, and be returned from a function call. Integers, floats, characters and strings are first-class in most languages.
- In functional languages (and imperative languages that support it, such as Python, C#, Java 8+), functions are first-class objects: a function can be passed to another function, returned as a result, and stored in a variable or data structure.

```python
def square(x): return x * x
f = square                      # assigned to a variable
print(f(5))                     # 25
def apply_twice(g, x):          # function as an argument
    return g(g(x))
print(apply_twice(square, 3))   # 81
def make_adder(n):              # function as a result
    return lambda x: x + n
add10 = make_adder(10)
print(add10(5))                 # 15
```

### 4.12.1.3 Function application

*Refs: AQA 4.12.1.3.*

#### What you need to know

- Function application means giving a function its arguments to get a result: `add(3, 4)` applies `add` to 3 and 4.
- The type of `add` can be written `add: integer × integer → integer`, where integer × integer is the Cartesian product of the integers with themselves (file 12). So although we say `add` takes two arguments, strictly it takes one argument: a pair, such as (3, 4).
- In Haskell, application is written by putting arguments after the function name with spaces: `add 3 4`.

### 4.12.1.4 Partial function application

*Refs: AQA 4.12.1.4.*

#### What you need to know

- In the partial application scheme, a function of several arguments is viewed as taking its arguments one at a time. Each time it is given one argument, it returns a new function that waits for the rest.
- `add: integer → (integer → integer)`. Applying `add 4` gives a function of type integer → integer that adds 4 to its argument. Brackets can be dropped (→ groups to the right): `add: integer → integer → integer`.
- Three arguments: `f: A → B → C → D`. `f a` has type B → C → D; `f a b` has type C → D; `f a b c` has type D.
- Uses: making specialised functions from general ones without writing new code (`add 1` is an increment function; `multiply 2` is a doubling function), and passing them to higher-order functions.

#### Worked example

```haskell
add :: Int -> Int -> Int
add x y = x + y

addFour :: Int -> Int
addFour = add 4          -- partial application

volume :: Int -> Int -> Int -> Int
volume l w h = l * w * h

base10 = volume 2 5      -- type Int -> Int: needs only the height
-- addFour 7 = 11 ; base10 3 = 30
-- map (add 1) [1, 2, 3] = [2, 3, 4]
```

Python equivalent:

```python
from functools import partial
def add(x, y): return x + y
add_four = partial(add, 4)
print(add_four(7))            # 11
```

### 4.12.1.5 Composition of functions

*Refs: AQA 4.12.1.5.*

#### What you need to know

- Composition combines two functions into a new function. Given f: A → B and g: B → C, the composition g ∘ f ("g after f") is a function A → C: apply f first, then apply g to f's result. (g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)).
- The co-domain of f must match (be a subset of) the domain of g.
- In Haskell, composition is the `.` operator: `(g . f) x = g (f x)`.

#### Worked example

f(x) = x + 2, g(y) = y³, both ℝ → ℝ. g ∘ f = (x + 2)³. (g ∘ f)(1) = 27. Note f ∘ g = x³ + 2, which is different: (f ∘ g)(1) = 3. Order matters.

```haskell
f x = x + 2
g y = y ^ 3
h = g . f        -- h 1 == 27
k = f . g        -- k 1 == 3
```

### 4.12.2.1 Writing functional programs

*Refs: AQA 4.12.2.1.*

#### What you need to know

- Functional languages: Haskell, Standard ML, Scheme, Lisp. Languages that also support the functional style: Python, F#, C#, Scala, Java 8+, VB.NET (2008+), Delphi XE+.
- A higher-order function takes a function as an argument, returns a function as its result, or both.
- map: applies a given function to each element of a list, returning a list of the results.
- filter: goes through a list (or other structure) and returns a new list containing exactly those elements that satisfy a given condition (a predicate: a function returning a Boolean).
- reduce or fold: reduces a list to a single value by repeatedly applying a combining function to the list values, starting from an initial value. `foldl` combines from the left; `foldr` from the right. The results differ for operations that are not associative (such as subtraction).

#### Worked example

```haskell
map (*2) [1, 2, 3, 4]                -- [2, 4, 6, 8]
filter even [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]       -- [2, 4, 6]
foldl (+) 0 [1, 2, 3, 4]             -- 10: ((((0+1)+2)+3)+4)
foldr (+) 0 [1, 2, 3, 4]             -- 10: (1+(2+(3+(4+0))))
foldl (-) 0 [1, 2, 3]                -- -6: ((0-1)-2)-3
foldr (-) 0 [1, 2, 3]                -- 2:  1-(2-(3-0))

-- sum of the squares of the odd numbers in a list
sumOddSquares :: [Int] -> Int
sumOddSquares xs = foldl (+) 0 (map (^2) (filter odd xs))
-- sumOddSquares [1,2,3,4,5] = 1 + 9 + 25 = 35
```

Trace `foldl (-) 10 [2, 3]`: start 10; 10 − 2 = 8; 8 − 3 = 5. Result 5.

Python:

```python
from functools import reduce
nums = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
print(list(map(lambda x: x * 2, nums)))          # [2, 4, 6, 8, 10]
print(list(filter(lambda x: x % 2 == 1, nums)))  # [1, 3, 5]
print(reduce(lambda a, b: a + b, nums, 0))       # 15
```

### 4.12.3.1 List processing

*Refs: AQA 4.12.3.1.*

#### What you need to know

- In functional languages a list is either empty `[]`, or a head joined to a tail: `head:tail`. The head is an element (the first item); the tail is a list (everything else, possibly empty). [4, 3, 5] = 4:[3, 5] = 4:3:5:[].
- Operations you must be able to describe and use:

| Operation | Haskell | Result for xs = [4, 3, 5] |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Return head | head xs | 4 |
| Return tail | tail xs | [3, 5] |
| Test for empty | null xs | False |
| Return length | length xs | 3 |
| Construct an empty list | [] | [] |
| Prepend an item | 7 : xs | [7, 4, 3, 5] |
| Append an item | xs ++ [7] | [4, 3, 5, 7] |

- Prepending with `:` is fast (it makes a new head pointing to the existing list); appending with `++` has to copy the whole first list.
- Recursive list functions follow the pattern: a base case for `[]`, and a general case for `(x:xs)` that does something with the head x and recurses on the tail xs.

#### Worked example

```haskell
myLength :: [a] -> Int
myLength []     = 0
myLength (_:xs) = 1 + myLength xs

mySum :: [Int] -> Int
mySum []     = 0
mySum (x:xs) = x + mySum xs

myMap :: (a -> b) -> [a] -> [b]
myMap _ []     = []
myMap f (x:xs) = f x : myMap f xs
```

Trace `mySum [4, 3, 5]`: 4 + mySum [3, 5] = 4 + (3 + mySum [5]) = 4 + (3 + (5 + mySum [])) = 4 + (3 + (5 + 0)) = 12.

Python equivalents (without changing the original list):

```python
def head(xs): return xs[0]
def tail(xs): return xs[1:]
def is_empty(xs): return xs == []
def prepend(x, xs): return [x] + xs
def append(xs, x): return xs + [x]

def length(xs):
    return 0 if is_empty(xs) else 1 + length(tail(xs))
```

### Common mistakes

- Reading g ∘ f as "g first". f is applied first.
- Saying the tail is the last element. The tail is a list of everything after the head.
- Calling `head []`: there is no head of an empty list (an error in Haskell). Test for empty first.
- Saying map changes the original list. It returns a new list.
- Mixing up filter (keeps items meeting a condition) and map (transforms every item).

### Check questions

1. A function `charCode` maps each upper-case letter to its ASCII code. Write its function type and name the domain and co-domain. [3 marks]
2. State what is meant by a first-class object and give two examples of what can be done with a function that is first-class. [3 marks]
3. Explain why `add: integer × integer → integer` can be said to take one argument. [2 marks]
4. `multiply: integer → integer → integer`. State the type of `multiply 3` and the result of `(multiply 3) 7`. [2 marks]
5. f(x) = 3x and g(x) = x − 1. Calculate (g ∘ f)(4) and (f ∘ g)(4). [2 marks]
6. Explain what a higher-order function is and name three. [4 marks]
7. Evaluate: (a) `map (+3) [1, 5, 9]`; (b) `filter (>4) [3, 8, 1, 6]`; (c) `foldl (*) 1 [2, 3, 4]`; (d) `foldr (-) 0 [5, 3]`. [4 marks]
8. For the list [8, 1, 6], give its head, its tail, its length and the result of prepending 2. [4 marks]
9. Write a recursive Haskell (or Python) function `countEven` that returns how many even numbers are in a list, using head and tail. [4 marks]
10. Write a single expression using map, filter and fold to find the total length of all words longer than 3 characters in a list of strings. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. `charCode: {A, …, Z} → integer` (accept Char → Int) (1); domain: upper-case letters (1); co-domain: integers (only 65 to 90 are used) (1).
2. A value that can be assigned to a variable, passed as an argument, returned from a function and used in expressions (1); any two for functions, e.g. passed as an argument to another function, returned as a result, stored in a variable (2).
3. The input is a single pair (from the Cartesian product integer × integer) (1), e.g. (3, 4) (1).
4. integer → integer (1); 21 (1).
5. g(f(4)) = g(12) = 11 (1); f(g(4)) = f(3) = 9 (1).
6. A function that takes a function as an argument and/or returns a function (2); map, filter, reduce/fold (2 for three, 1 for two).
7. (a) [4, 8, 12] (1); (b) [8, 6] (1); (c) 24 (1); (d) 5 − (3 − 0) = 2 (1).
8. Head 8 (1); tail [1, 6] (1); length 3 (1); [2, 8, 1, 6] (1).
9. Example:

```haskell
countEven :: [Int] -> Int
countEven [] = 0
countEven (x:xs)
  | even x    = 1 + countEven xs
  | otherwise = countEven xs
```

Base case empty list returns 0 (1); splits into head and tail (1); adds 1 when head is even (1); recursive call on tail in both cases (1).
10. `foldl (+) 0 (map length (filter (\w -> length w > 3) ws))` or Python `reduce(lambda a, b: a + b, map(len, filter(lambda w: len(w) > 3, ws)), 0)` (1 each for correct filter, map and fold).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/computer-science/33-software-development-and-testing.md -->

## Software development and testing

This file covers the stages of developing a system (analysis, design, implementation, testing, evaluation), development methodologies (waterfall, agile, extreme programming, spiral, rapid application development), feasibility studies and methods of investigation, data flow diagrams, human-computer interaction and natural language interfaces, design reviews and evaluation criteria, testing (test data, dry runs, black box and white box, alpha, beta and acceptance), changeover methods, maintenance, backup and recovery, documentation, and software tools including IDEs, CASE tools and version control. This is the theory examined on paper; the coursework project itself is not covered.

Builds on [GCSE: Robust programs and testing](../../gcse/computer-science/06-robust-programs-and-testing.md) and [GCSE: Languages, translators and IDEs](../../gcse/computer-science/08-languages-translators-and-ides.md).

#### Board differences

| Content | AQA 7517 | OCR H446 | Eduqas A500QS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stages | Analysis, design, implementation, testing, evaluation, with prototyping and agile | Writing and following algorithms | Analysis and design approaches; feasibility; investigation; changeover; maintenance; documentation |
| Methodologies | Prototyping/agile named | Waterfall, agile, extreme programming, spiral, RAD; merits and when to use | Waterfall and agile |
| Testing | Normal, boundary, erroneous; acceptance testing | (Test strategies including black and white box, alpha and beta, are in OCR's AS content) | Test data; dry runs; alpha, beta and acceptance testing |
| Design | Data structures, algorithms, modular structure, user interface | Not named | HCI, natural language and spoken input; design review; evaluation criteria; DFDs |
| Tools | Not named | Use an IDE to develop and debug | Software tools (CASE), IDE tools, version management |

### 4.13.1.1 Analysis

*Refs: AQA 4.13.1.1 · Eduqas C1.5: Feasibility, Investigation, Analysis.*

#### What you need to know

- Before a problem can be solved it must be defined: what the problem is, who the users are, and what the system must do.
- Requirements must be established by interacting with the intended users (the client and end users), and recorded in a requirements specification: functional requirements (what it must do) and non-functional requirements (how well: speed, security, usability, platform).
- A data model is created: an abstraction of the real-world data the system needs (entities, attributes, relationships: file 30).
- Clarifying requirements may involve prototyping or an agile approach: show users an early version and refine requirements from their feedback.
- Feasibility study (Eduqas): before committing, an analyst checks whether the project is worth doing and possible:
  - technical (can it be built with available hardware, software and skills?);
  - economic (do the benefits outweigh the costs? cost-benefit analysis);
  - legal (does it comply with data protection and other laws?);
  - operational (will it work in the organisation and will staff use it?);
  - schedule (can it be delivered in time?).
  Proposed solutions must be cost-effective, developed to an agreed time scale and within an agreed budget.
- Methods of investigation (Eduqas):
  - interviews: detailed, can follow up answers; time-consuming, few people;
  - questionnaires: reach many people quickly and anonymously, easy to analyse; low response, no follow-up, questions may be misunderstood;
  - observation: see what really happens; people may act differently when watched (time-consuming);
  - document analysis: examine existing forms, reports and records to see what data is used; may be out of date.
- Analysis techniques (Eduqas): use abstraction and decomposition (file 11) to break the problem down; represent the system with diagrams showing the flow of data and processing requirements, such as data flow diagrams; choose suitable hardware and software for the requirements.
- Data flow diagrams (DFDs) show how data moves through a system. Symbols: external entity (a source or destination outside the system, such as Customer: a rectangle), process (something that transforms data: a rounded rectangle or circle), data store (a file or table: an open-ended rectangle), and data flow (a labelled arrow). Every flow is labelled with the data it carries.

#### Worked example

A DFD for an online order (described in words):

- Customer (external entity) → "order details" → Process 1 "Validate order" → "valid order" → Data store "Orders".
- Process 1 → "stock request" → Data store "Stock" → "stock level" → Process 1.
- Data store "Orders" → "order" → Process 2 "Dispatch order" → "dispatch note" → Warehouse (external entity).
- Process 2 → "confirmation email" → Customer.

### 4.13.1.2 Design

*Refs: AQA 4.13.1.2 · Eduqas C1.6: Human-computer interaction, Design validation, Design evaluation.*

#### What you need to know

- Before building, the solution is designed and specified: data structures for the data model, algorithms (pseudo-code, flowcharts), a modular structure with clear documented interfaces between modules (hierarchy charts, file 02), and the human user interface.
- Design can be iterative, using prototyping or an agile approach.
- Human-computer interaction (HCI) (Eduqas): designing how people communicate with computers. Contemporary approaches: graphical user interfaces (windows, icons, menus, pointer), touch and gestures, voice assistants, menus and forms, command lines (for experts), and augmented and virtual reality. Design should suit the users' abilities and experience, follow consistent conventions, give feedback, prevent and help recover from errors, and be accessible (colour contrast, screen readers, keyboard use).
- Natural language interfaces let users communicate in ordinary language, typed or spoken. Potential: very easy for new users, hands-free, accessible. Problems with spoken input: ambiguity (homophones like "write/right", unclear word boundaries, words with several meanings, sentences with several possible structures), accents, background noise, and context; a system may misinterpret commands, so it often asks for confirmation.
- Design validation (design review, Eduqas): before coding, the design is checked to make sure it matches the specification (every requirement is met), the most appropriate techniques have been used (suitable data structures, algorithms, security), and the user interface is appropriate for the users.
- Design evaluation criteria (Eduqas, and AQA 4.13.1.5): does it meet the requirements; is it efficient (speed, memory); is it reliable and robust; is it usable (easy to learn and use); is it maintainable (well structured, documented); is it secure; is it cost-effective; is it compatible with existing systems.

### 4.13.1.3 Implementation

*Refs: AQA 4.13.1.3 · OCR 1.2.3(c), 2.2.1(e) · Eduqas C1.7: Software tools.*

#### What you need to know

- The models and algorithms are implemented as data structures and code that a computer can run.
- The final solution may be reached iteratively, using prototyping or an agile approach, focusing on the critical path first (the parts the rest depends on, or that are most essential, are built and tested first).
- Programmers should be able to explain how their programs work and argue for their correctness and efficiency using logical reasoning, test data and user feedback.
- Integrated development environment (IDE) features for developing and debugging (OCR 2.2.1(e), Eduqas C1.7):
  - editor with syntax highlighting, auto-indentation, auto-completion and error underlining;
  - built-in translator (compile or run from the editor) and error messages that point to the line;
  - debugging tools: breakpoints (pause at a chosen line), stepping (run one line at a time), watch windows and variable inspection (see values change), call stack view;
  - other: code templates, refactoring, integration with version control, GUI designers.
- Software tools (CASE: computer-aided software engineering) support each stage: requirements and modelling tools (drawing DFDs, ER and UML diagrams), project management tools (Gantt charts, task tracking), code generators, automated testing tools (running test suites, unit tests), documentation generators, and bug trackers.
- Version management (version control, Eduqas): software such as Git records every change to the code with who made it and why. Benefits: roll back to an earlier working version; see what changed and when; several developers work on the same code at once using branches, which are later merged; a full backup history; supports releases (version numbers).

### 4.13.1.4 Testing

*Refs: AQA 4.13.1.4 · Eduqas C1.3: Testing, C1.5: Program testing.*

#### What you need to know

- Testing finds errors (syntax, logic and run-time errors) and checks the system meets its specification.
- Test data:
  - normal (typical) data: valid data the program should accept and process correctly;
  - boundary data: valid and invalid values at the edges of allowed ranges (for 1 to 100: 0, 1, 100, 101);
  - erroneous data: invalid data the program should reject (wrong type, out of range, blank).
- A test plan lists each test: its purpose, the test data, the expected result, the actual result, and any action taken.
- Dry run (Eduqas): working through a program or algorithm on paper with test data, using a trace table, to find errors (especially logic errors) and to work out what an algorithm does.
- Black box testing: tests the inputs and outputs against the specification without looking at the code. White box testing: tests designed from the code's structure, so that every path, branch and loop is exercised.
- Unit testing (each module), integration testing (modules together), system testing (the whole system).
- Alpha testing: done in-house by the developers' own testers (or selected staff) during development, on an unfinished version.
- Beta testing: a nearly finished version is released to a limited group of real users outside the company, who use it in real conditions and report bugs; finds problems the developers did not think of.
- Acceptance testing: done with or by the client and intended users at the end, to check the system meets the agreed requirements before it is accepted (signed off and paid for).

#### Worked example

A function accepts an exam mark from 0 to 100 as an integer.

| Test | Type | Data | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Normal | 57 | Accepted |
| 2 | Boundary (lowest valid) | 0 | Accepted |
| 3 | Boundary (highest valid) | 100 | Accepted |
| 4 | Boundary (just invalid) | −1 | Rejected with message |
| 5 | Boundary (just invalid) | 101 | Rejected with message |
| 6 | Erroneous | "abc" | Rejected with message |
| 7 | Erroneous | 45.5 | Rejected (not an integer) |
| 8 | Erroneous | blank | Rejected |

Dry run to find a logic error:

```
total ← 0
FOR i ← 1 TO 4
    total ← total + i
ENDFOR
OUTPUT total / i
```

Intended: average of 1 to 4 = 2.5. Trace: total becomes 10; after the loop i is 4 (or 5 in some languages). Output 2.5 in one language and 2 in another: a logic error that depends on the loop variable after the loop. Fix: divide by 4 (or a count variable).

### 4.13.1.5 Evaluation

*Refs: AQA 4.13.1.5 · Eduqas C1.6: Design evaluation.*

#### What you need to know

- Criteria for evaluating a computer system: does it meet all the requirements in the specification (functionality); correctness; reliability and robustness; efficiency (speed, memory, storage); usability (easy to learn and use, accessible); maintainability (readable, modular, documented code); security; compatibility; cost and time against budget; user feedback and satisfaction.
- Evaluation should judge the final system against the original requirements and identify improvements.

### Development methodologies

*Refs: OCR 1.2.3(a), 1.2.3(b) · Eduqas C1.5: Approaches.*

#### What you need to know

Waterfall:

- Stages in strict order: requirements/analysis, design, implementation, testing, installation, maintenance. Each stage is finished and signed off before the next begins; you may go back to the previous stage if a problem is found.
- Merits: clear structure and milestones; easy to manage and document; suits projects with fixed, well-understood requirements (safety-critical systems, large government contracts). Drawbacks: users see a working system only at the end; changes in requirements are costly; errors in early stages are found late.

Agile methodologies:

- Develop in short iterations (sprints) of a few weeks, each producing a working increment of the software, with frequent feedback from the client. Requirements can change as the project goes on. Emphasis on individuals, working software and collaboration over heavy documentation.
- Merits: flexible to changing requirements; users see progress early; problems found quickly; high client satisfaction. Drawbacks: less documentation; hard to predict final cost and time; needs a committed client and experienced team; can drift without discipline.
- Suits projects where requirements are unclear or will change (web and mobile apps, start-ups).

Extreme programming (XP), an agile method:

- Very short iterations; the client (or a representative) is part of the team and writes user stories; pair programming (two programmers at one computer, one coding and one reviewing); test-driven development (write tests before the code); continuous integration; frequent small releases; collective code ownership; refactoring; sustainable pace (no excessive overtime).
- Merits: very high code quality; quick response to change. Drawbacks: costly (two programmers per task); needs the client constantly available; little design documentation. Suits small to medium teams with changing requirements.

Spiral model:

- Repeats four phases in loops (each loop a more complete version): determine objectives; identify and resolve risks (often by building prototypes); develop and test; plan the next iteration. Risk analysis is the focus.
- Merits: risks found and dealt with early; suits large, expensive, high-risk projects; can include prototypes. Drawbacks: expensive and complex to manage; needs risk-assessment expertise; not worth it for small, low-risk projects.

Rapid application development (RAD):

- Gather requirements quickly (focus groups and workshops), then build a series of prototypes that users evaluate; each prototype is refined until it becomes the final system. Often uses time-boxing and reusable components or tools.
- Merits: fast delivery; users heavily involved, so the system meets their needs; requirements can change. Drawbacks: code may be less efficient; harder to scale for large systems; needs users available; less focus on non-functional requirements such as performance.
- Suits small to medium projects with a strong user interface element.

#### Worked example

Choose a methodology:

- Software for a hospital radiotherapy machine: requirements are fixed by regulators, safety is critical and full documentation is required: waterfall (or spiral for its risk analysis).
- A start-up's new social app: requirements will change with user feedback, speed to market matters: agile or XP.
- A booking system for a sports centre, needing staff to approve screens: RAD.
- A new air traffic control system with major technical risk and a huge budget: spiral.

### Changeover, maintenance, backup and documentation

*Refs: Eduqas C1.5: Changeover, Maintenance, Backup and recovery, Documentation.*

#### Changeover methods

| Method | How | Advantages | Disadvantages | Suitable when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Direct | Old system stops; new one starts at once | Fast, cheap, no duplication | High risk: if the new system fails there is no fallback | Small or non-critical systems; or when the old system cannot run on |
| Parallel | Old and new run side by side for a period; outputs compared | Low risk; old system is a fallback; results can be checked | Expensive and double the work for staff | Critical systems where failure is unacceptable (payroll, banking) |
| Pilot | New system is used in one branch or department first, then rolled out | Problems found on a small scale; staff there can train others | Slower full rollout; the pilot site carries the risk | Organisations with several similar branches |
| Phased | New system introduced one module or part at a time | Each part tested before the next; staff learn gradually | Takes longer; old and new parts must work together | Large modular systems |

#### Maintenance

- Corrective: fixing bugs found after the system is in use.
- Adaptive: changing the system to cope with a changed environment (new laws such as tax rates, new operating systems or hardware, new business processes).
- Perfective: improving performance or adding features and usability improvements requested by users, even though the system works.

#### Backup and recovery

- Backup procedures: full backups (everything), incremental (only what changed since the last backup of any kind: quick to make, slower to restore) and differential (everything changed since the last full backup). Store copies off-site or in the cloud; follow a schedule (for example the grandfather-father-son rotation); test that backups can be restored.
- Recovery: restore the latest full backup, then the incremental or differential backups, then re-apply transactions from the transaction log since the last backup. More on file security in [36-file-organisation-and-data-security.md](36-file-organisation-and-data-security.md).

#### Documentation

- Produced at different stages: requirements specification (analysis), design specification (design), program listings and technical documentation (implementation), test plans and results (testing), user guide and training material (installation), evaluation report.
- User documentation: for people using the system: how to install, start and use each feature, with screenshots, tutorials, troubleshooting, FAQs, error messages and what to do, hardware and software requirements.
- Maintenance (technical) documentation: for programmers who will maintain the system: system overview and design, annotated program listings (commented code), variable lists (names, types, purposes, scope), algorithms (pseudo-code or flowcharts), data dictionaries (fields, types, validation), file and database structures, test plans and results, and known limitations.

### Common mistakes

- Saying beta testing is done by the programmers. Beta testers are real external users.
- Calling a value just outside the range "erroneous" in AQA questions. It is boundary (invalid) data; erroneous is data of the wrong kind.
- Choosing direct changeover for a critical system without considering the risk.
- Confusing adaptive maintenance (the environment changed) with perfective (making it better).
- Describing agile as "no planning". Agile plans in short cycles.

### Check questions

1. Explain why requirements must be established with the intended users. [2 marks]
2. (Eduqas) Describe what is investigated in a feasibility study. [4 marks]
3. (Eduqas) Compare interviews and questionnaires as methods of investigation. [4 marks]
4. (Eduqas) Draw a DFD for a library loan: a member gives a book and card to the system, which checks the Members and Books stores and records the loan. [4 marks]
5. Describe what should be designed before a solution is constructed. [4 marks]
6. (Eduqas) Explain the problems of ambiguity in spoken input. [3 marks]
7. Give test data (normal, boundary and erroneous) for a field that accepts ages 16 to 19. [3 marks]
8. Explain the difference between alpha, beta and acceptance testing. [3 marks]
9. (OCR) Compare the waterfall model with agile methodologies. [6 marks]
10. (OCR) Describe two features of extreme programming. [2 marks]
11. (OCR) Explain why the spiral model suits a high-risk project. [2 marks]
12. (OCR, Eduqas) Describe three features of an IDE that help a programmer debug a program. [3 marks]
13. (Eduqas) Explain how version management helps a team of developers. [3 marks]
14. (Eduqas) Recommend a changeover method for a bank's new account system, with reasons. [3 marks]
15. (Eduqas) Explain the three types of maintenance with an example of each. [6 marks]
16. (Eduqas) Describe the contents of maintenance documentation. [4 marks]
17. State four criteria for evaluating a computer system. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Users know what the system needs to do/the problem best (1); so the system meets their needs and avoids costly changes later (1).
2. Technical: can it be built with available technology/skills (1); economic: do benefits outweigh costs/within budget (1); legal: complies with laws such as data protection (1); operational/schedule: will it work in the organisation/be delivered on time (1).
3. Interviews: detailed answers, follow-up questions (1); but time-consuming/few people (1). Questionnaires: many people quickly, anonymous, easy to analyse (1); but low response/cannot follow up/misunderstood questions (1).
4. External entity Member (1); process "Issue loan" with labelled flows of card and book details (1); data stores Members and Books read for checking (1); data store Loans written (1).
5. Data structures for the data model (1); algorithms (1); modular structure with interfaces (1); user interface (1).
6. Words that sound the same but mean different things (homophones) (1); unclear word boundaries/sentences with several meanings (1); accents and background noise cause misinterpretation, so commands may be wrong (1).
7. Normal e.g. 17 (1); boundary 16 and 19 (and 15, 20 as just invalid) (1); erroneous e.g. "sixteen" or blank (1).
8. Alpha: in-house testing by the developer's testers during development (1). Beta: pre-release version tested by real external users in real conditions (1). Acceptance: client/users check the system meets agreed requirements before sign-off (1).
9. Level-based. Waterfall: sequential stages, each completed before the next; clear documentation and milestones; suits fixed requirements; changes costly; users see the product late. Agile: short iterations with working increments; frequent client feedback; adapts to change; less documentation; harder to predict cost/time. Top band: clear comparison with suitable uses.
10. Any two: pair programming (1); test-driven development (1); client as part of the team/user stories (1); short iterations/frequent releases (1); continuous integration/refactoring (1).
11. Each loop includes risk analysis (1); prototypes are built to resolve the biggest risks early before major investment (1).
12. Any three: breakpoints (1); stepping through code line by line (1); watch windows/variable inspection (1); error messages with line numbers (1); call stack view (1).
13. Records every change, who made it and why (1); allows rolling back to a previous version (1); branching and merging lets several developers work at once without overwriting each other's work (1).
14. Parallel (1); failure would be very costly, so the old system remains as a fallback (1); results can be compared to check the new system is correct (1). Accept pilot with good justification.
15. Corrective: fixing bugs found in use, e.g. a crash when a date is entered (2). Adaptive: changing for a new environment, e.g. new tax law/new OS (2). Perfective: improving or adding features, e.g. faster searching/new report (2).
16. Any four: system design/overview (1); annotated program listings (1); variable lists (1); algorithms (1); data dictionary (1); test plans and results (1).
17. Any four: meets requirements (1); efficient (1); reliable/robust (1); usable (1); maintainable (1); secure (1); within budget/time (1).
